Interactive mathematical exposition

Heat Equation with a Dynamical Singular Potential on the Boundary

Can heat diffusion balance singular amplification at a moving point on the boundary? We study how the coupling strength and the trajectory determine existence, nonexistence and the behavior of solutions.

For advanced undergraduates and graduate students in mathematics
0 · The governing problemThe problem and its context1 · Heat and boundariesPhysical meaning of the equation2 · Mathematical formulationHeat kernels and integral solutions3 · Main theoremsSolvability and weighted bounds4 · Energy and critical couplingThe sharp form estimate5 · Parabolic scalingMotion versus diffusion6. Part I

0. Introduction

0.1 The governing initial-boundary value problem

Let \(N\ge3\) be the spatial dimension and \(T>0\) the time horizon. Write \(x=(x',x_N)\in\mathbb R^N\), with \(x'\in\mathbb R^{N-1}\). The fixed upper half-space is \(\mathcal H=\{x:x_N>0\}\) and its boundary \(\partial\mathcal H=\{x:x_N=0\}\). Let \(\xi:[0,T]\to\partial\mathcal H\) be a continuous prescribed path. The outward normal is \(\nu=-e_N\), where \(e_N\) is the last coordinate unit vector.

Initial-boundary value problem
\[\boxed{\begin{cases}\partial_tu=\Delta u &\text{in }\mathcal H\times(0,T),\\[3pt]\displaystyle\partial_\nu u=\frac{\lambda}{|x-\xi(t)|}u &\text{on }\Sigma_\xi,\\[3pt]u(x,0)=u_0(x)&\text{in }\mathcal H.\end{cases}}\]

Here \(\Sigma_\xi=\{(x,t)\in\partial\mathcal H\times(0,T):x\ne\xi(t)\}\). The coefficient \(\lambda/|x-\xi(t)|\) is undefined at \(x=\xi(t)\), so the Robin condition is written only for boundary points \(x\ne\xi(t)\). The integral formulation in Chapter 2 specifies what is meant by a solution.

SymbolMeaning
\(u(x,t),u_0(x)\)Unknown temperature and its prescribed initial distribution.
\(\partial_tu,\Delta u\)Time derivative and Laplacian \(\sum_{j=1}^N\partial_{x_j}^2u\): interior diffusion with diffusivity one.
\(\partial_\nu u=-\partial_{x_N}u\)Derivative in the outward normal direction.
\(\lambda>0\)Coupling strength of the boundary amplification.
\(\xi(t), |x-\xi(t)|\)Prescribed boundary pole and Euclidean distance from it.

The red segment marks strong boundary amplification; the blue point marks a boundary observation. This is a geometric diagram, not a temperature field.

0.2 What makes the boundary condition singular?

Inside the domain, heat diffuses normally. On the boundary, the Robin condition makes the outward normal derivative proportional to the temperature, with coefficient \(V(x,t)=\lambda/|x-\xi(t)|\) on the boundary. For nonnegative temperature, this sign gives inward heat flux (Chapter 1). The coefficient diverges as a boundary point approaches the pole. Only the coefficient’s center moves; the domain stays fixed.

Example. If \(\lambda=1\) and the boundary distances are 1, 0.1 and 0.01, the coefficients are 1, 10 and 100. These are coefficient values, not temperatures: the actual heat flux also depends on \(u\). A bounded approximation replaces the denominator by \(\sqrt{|x-\xi(t)|^2+\varepsilon^2}\), where \(\varepsilon>0\). The regularized and singular problems are different; Chapter 7 justifies the limit.

0.3 Related singular-potential models

The four models below differ in where the potential acts and whether its center moves. Initial conditions are omitted in this comparison.

0.4 Why these powers of distance?

For a stationary singularity at the origin, rescale space and time by setting \(u_r(x,t)=u(rx,r^2t)\), with \(r>0\). The chain rule gives \(\partial_tu_r=r^2u_t\), \(\Delta u_r=r^2\Delta u\), and \(\partial_\nu u_r=r\partial_\nu u\), with the original derivatives evaluated at the rescaled point. An interior coefficient proportional to \(|x|^{-2}\) therefore has the same scaling order as the Laplacian; a boundary coefficient proportional to \(|x|^{-1}\) has the same order as the normal derivative.

\[\begin{aligned} (u_r)_t&=\Delta u_r+\lambda|x|^{-2}u_r &&\text{(interior model)},\\ \partial_\nu u_r&=\lambda|x|^{-1}u_r &&\text{(boundary model)}.\end{aligned}\]

In the moving boundary model, the trajectory rescales to \(\xi_r(t)=r^{-1}\xi(r^2t)\). Scale invariance motivates a critical coupling but does not determine its value.

0.5 The mathematical questions

Can diffusion offset the singular boundary input? Is there a threshold in \(\lambda\)? If a finite nonnegative solution exists, how large can it become near the pole, and is it uniquely determined by the data? Motion raises a further question: does the answer depend on how quickly the pole moves relative to diffusion?

We use an integral solution: free heat diffusion plus the accumulated boundary contribution, with finite values and continuity away from the moving singular point in space-time. A minimal solution is one no larger than any other admissible nonnegative solution with the same data. This is not unrestricted uniqueness. Chapter 2 gives the precise definition. A weighted class limits the allowed spatial growth near the pole and at infinity using a positive reference function, introduced in Chapter 3.

0.6 Preview of the main results

Manuscript results · precise statements in Chapter 3

For nonzero, nonnegative, compactly supported continuous initial data and any continuous boundary trajectory, the paper proves existence of a minimal integral solution up to and including a positive threshold \(\lambda_N\). Above this threshold the paper rules out finite nonnegative integral solutions if the motion is small relative to diffusion on all short time intervals. Below the threshold, with the same motion condition, it obtains upper singularity estimates and uniqueness in a specified weighted class.

Existence at critical coupling does not settle the precise critical profile. Failure of the motion condition does not by itself imply existence above the threshold.

Exact threshold and motion condition (optional preview)

The threshold is expressed using the Gamma function, \(\Gamma(z)=\int_0^\infty r^{z-1}e^{-r}\,dr\) for \(z>0\). Chapter 6 derives the constant. For short time intervals of length at most \(h>0\), the normalized displacement compares the movement of the pole with the diffusion length.

\[\lambda_N=2\frac{\Gamma(N/4)^2}{\Gamma((N-2)/4)^2}.\tag{0.1}\]

Define the uniform normalized displacement

\[\omega(h)=\sup_{\substack{0\le s<t\le T\\t-s\le h}}\frac{|\xi(t)-\xi(s)|}{\sqrt{t-s}}.\]

The comparison condition in the research paper is \(\omega(h)\to0\) as \(h\downarrow0\). For instance, a trajectory \(\xi(t)=at e_1\) has \(\omega(h)\le |a|\sqrt h\). In contrast, \(\xi(t)=\sqrt t\,e_1\) has \(\omega(h)=1\) for \(0<h\le T\). The denominator is the diffusion scale (Chapter 5). Existence through criticality does not require this condition.

0.7 Research background

ResearchConnection to this problem
Baras–Goldstein [1]Heat equation with an interior singular potential.
Ishige–Ishiwata [2]Stationary boundary singularity. The Kato inequality determines the critical coupling.
Chern et al. [3]Moving interior Hardy-type potential. Gaussian comparison motivates the approach to the moving problem studied here.
Revised research paper [4] and revised thesis [5]A singular point moving along the boundary. The revised paper establishes existence at critical coupling for continuous trajectories.

0.8 Reading roadmap

Start with Chapters 1–2 for the physical interpretation and precise solution framework, then Chapters 3–5 for the theorem statements and their energy and scaling motivations. Chapters 6–9 prove the main results. Chapters 10–11 give probabilistic and numerical perspectives, and Chapters 12–13 develop extensions and remaining questions. Consult Appendices A–F as needed. The theorem catalogue provides a separate reference route.

Chapter 0 · Takeaways

The half-space is fixed and the inverse-distance coefficient acts only on the boundary. The threshold concerns finite integral solvability, not a numerical temperature threshold. Existence through criticality requires continuity of the path; the supercritical nonexistence theorem requires an additional displacement condition.

Check your understanding

Where does the potential act? What changes when the pole moves? Does minimality mean uniqueness?

On the boundary; only the coefficient's center changes; no.

Part I

1. Heat equation and boundary interaction

How does a positive boundary input coexist with local cooling?

Prerequisites and proof outline

The governing equation; elementary differentiation and integration.

Derive the heat equation from flux → compare boundary conditions → inspect a regularized moving coefficient.

1.1 Heat diffusion

The governing equation in §0.1 uses diffusivity one. To see why, start from conservation of heat and the direction in which heat flows. The whole-space Gaussian below isolates interior diffusion before we return to the boundary.

Let \(u(x,t)\) denote temperature and \(J(x,t)\) the heat flux. With constant thermal conductivity, Fourier’s law states that heat flows opposite to the temperature gradient. For volumetric heat capacity \(c>0\) and conductivity \(k>0\), the heat balance takes the following form.

\[J=-k\nabla u,\qquad c\,\partial_t u=-\operatorname{div}J=k\Delta u.\tag{1.1}\]

Normalizing the diffusivity \(k/c\) to one gives the heat equation used in this study. The Laplacian \(\Delta u=\sum_{j=1}^N\partial_{x_j}^2u\) is the sum of second directional derivatives in the coordinate directions. At a smooth interior local maximum, \(\Delta u\le0\), so diffusion tends to flatten peaks.

\[\partial_tu=\Delta u.\tag{1.2}\]
Example · Local diffusion

In one dimension, at an interior point with \(u(x)=1-x^2\) locally, \(u_{xx}=-2\), so the instantaneous time derivative is negative. For \(u(x)=1+x^2\), it is positive. In several dimensions the second derivatives are added: a saddle point need not have zero Laplacian. These are local calculations, not global solutions.

Example 1.1 · Gaussian initial data

In the whole space, take \(u(x,0)=\exp(-|x|^2/(4b_0))\), \(b_0>0\). The solution is

\[u(x,t)=\left(\frac{b_0}{b_0+t}\right)^{N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|x|^2}{4(b_0+t)}\right).\]

Its central value decreases, while its integral over the whole space stays constant. Chapter 2 derives this using the heat kernel.

1.2 Boundary conditions and heat balance

Imagine the same initial heat distribution in three identical intervals: a boundary held at zero temperature, an insulated boundary, and a boundary supplying heat proportionally to its temperature. These are Dirichlet, Neumann and positive Robin conditions, respectively. The outward-flux sign will distinguish them.

Let \(\nu\) be the outward unit normal of a domain \(\Omega\). The normal derivative is \(\partial_\nu u=\nabla u\cdot\nu\). In normalized units the outward component of the heat flux is \(J\cdot\nu=-\partial_\nu u\), so \(\partial_\nu u>0\) corresponds to heat entering the domain.

Boundary conditionFormulaInterpretation
Dirichlet\(u=g\)Prescribes the boundary temperature.
Neumann\(\partial_\nu u=q\)Prescribes the normal heat flux. \(q=0\) means insulation.
Robin\(\partial_\nu u=bu\)Flux proportional to the boundary temperature. Under our sign convention, \(b>0\) gives amplification.

For a smooth solution on a bounded smooth domain, assuming that differentiation under the integral is justified and the boundary flux is integrable, the divergence theorem gives

\[\frac{d}{dt}\int_\Omega u\,dx=\int_\Omega\Delta u\,dx=\int_{\partial\Omega}\partial_\nu u\,d\sigma.\tag{1.3}\]

In the half-space, one can apply the balance on truncated half-balls and pass to infinity only if the omitted spherical flux tends to zero and the volume and planar integrals converge. In particular, neither finite \(L^2\) norm nor continuity away from the singular point alone guarantees this identity. We use it here for smooth bounded-coefficient models, not as an assertion about all singular integral solutions.

Example · Prescribed flux versus feedback

If \(\partial_\nu u=q(x,t)\), the incoming flux is specified independently of the unknown. For Robin data \(\partial_\nu u=Vu\), two points with the same coefficient but temperatures 1 and 2 have fluxes \(V\) and \(2V\). This is a linear feedback law. It is not a heater that injects a fixed amount of energy regardless of temperature.

Thus, when boundary terms at infinity vanish in the half-space, a nonnegative temperature and a positive Robin coefficient contribute to increasing total heat. This does not imply that the temperature at every point must increase. In a finite domain, artificial Dirichlet boundaries may also allow heat loss.

1.3 Moving boundary coefficient

The model introduces a coefficient inversely proportional to the distance from a boundary point \(\xi(t)\). The singularity means that the coefficient diverges; the moving point is not a heat particle being transported. There is no transport term in the interior equation.

\[\partial_\nu u(x,t)=V(x,t)u(x,t),\qquad V(x,t)=\frac{\lambda}{|x-\xi(t)|}.\tag{1.4}\]

The three quantities \(V\), \(u\) and \(Vu\) must be distinguished. The coefficient is singular at the boundary point, but this does not prescribe the value of the solution there. Nor does the trajectory describe a moving material particle: the interior PDE contains no advection term. For the half-space, \(\nu=-e_N\), so the boundary condition is \(-\partial_{x_N}u=Vu\). With positive temperature this represents inward flux in normalized units.

\(\lambda>0\) is the coupling constant. The coefficient is finite for \(x\ne\xi(t)\), but grows as the distance approaches zero. In numerical experiments, one may instead use the following bounded coefficient.

\[V_\varepsilon(x,t)=\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{|x-\xi(t)|^2+\varepsilon^2}},\qquad \varepsilon>0.\tag{1.5}\]

1.4 Stationary and moving singularities

The next calculation is a regularized three-dimensional finite-box experiment, not the singular half-space theorem. The initial bump is shared between both runs, the sides and top have zero Dirichlet values, and the bottom has a bounded Robin coefficient. The introductory preset (λ = 0.85, ε = 0.20, sinusoidal motion) is selected for visible contrast; the revised thesis preset (λ = 0.55, ε = 0.20, linear motion) is available in the experiment settings. Chapter 11 gives the scheme and its limitations.

Use the same initial temperature and compare a fixed center with a moving center. Past boundary amplification also affects diffusion, so the present position of the center alone does not determine the temperature field.

Scope of the physical interpretation. A positive Robin coefficient represents boundary amplification proportional to temperature. This models temperature-dependent boundary input, not a prescribed external heat source.

Chapter 1 · Takeaways

Interior diffusion redistributes temperature. The positive Robin condition supplies an inward flux proportional to the boundary value; the moving center changes the coefficient, not the transport law. The figures solve bounded regularizations on a finite box.

Check your understanding

Does \(V>0\) force every temperature value to rise? What changes when the center moves?

Answers: no, diffusion and outer-boundary losses may dominate locally; the spatial and temporal boundary coefficient changes, while the interior PDE is still the heat equation.

Sources
Source correspondence: [5] §2.3, Chapter 9 §§9.1–9.3. The heat balance is included as physical background.

The numerical examples motivate a precise solution concept. Chapter 2 builds that concept using the reflected heat kernel.

Part I

2. Mathematical formulation

How can the equation be interpreted without differentiating at the pole?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Appendix A.1 for nonnegative integrals and limits; A.2 for Gaussian kernels; B.3 for traces.

Geometry → solution concepts → reflected kernel → boundary integral → positivity and iteration.

2.1 Domain and moving singularity

Definition 2.1 · Half-space and trajectory

Let \(N\ge3\) and \(T>0\). Define the upper half-space, its closure and its boundary by

\[\begin{aligned}\mathcal{H}&=\{x=(x',x_N)\in\mathbb R^N:x_N>0\},\\\overline{\mathcal{H}}&=\{x_N\ge0\},\\\partial \mathcal{H}&=\mathbb R^{N-1}\times\{0\}\end{aligned}\tag{2.1}\]

The trajectory \(\xi:[0,T]\to\partial \mathcal{H}\) is continuous, and \(\nu=-e_N\) is the outward unit normal. Thus \(\partial_\nu=-\partial_{x_N}\).

\[\begin{aligned}\mathcal G_\xi&=\{(\xi(t),t):0\le t\le T\},\\\Sigma_\xi&=(\partial \mathcal{H}\times(0,T))\setminus\mathcal G_\xi,\\D_\xi&=(\overline{\mathcal{H}}\times[0,T])\setminus\mathcal G_\xi.\end{aligned}\tag{2.2}\]
Problem 2.1 · Initial-boundary value problem (research paper (1.1))
\[\begin{cases}\partial_tu=\Delta u&\text{in }\mathcal{H}\times(0,T),\\[3pt]\displaystyle\partial_\nu u=\frac{\lambda}{|x-\xi(t)|}u&\text{on }\Sigma_\xi,\\[3pt]u(\cdot,0)=u_0&\text{in }\mathcal{H}.\end{cases}\tag{2.3}\]

Assume \(\lambda>0\), \(u_0\ge0\), and \(u_0\in C(\overline{\mathcal{H}}\setminus\{\xi(0)\})\). The main existence and nonexistence theorems further assume \(u_0\in C_c(\overline{\mathcal{H}})\) and \(u_0\not\equiv0\). The weighted theorem has a separate initial-data condition, to be stated in Chapter 3.

Assumptions · Three classes of initial data
SettingData and role
General formulation\(0\le u_0\in C(\overline{\mathcal{H}}\setminus\{\xi(0)\})\). This specifies the datum, not a solvability theorem.
Main results (paper Theorems 1.1–1.3)\(0\le u_0\in C_c(\overline{\mathcal{H}})\), \(u_0\not\equiv0\). Such data are bounded, integrable and square integrable. Nontriviality is essential for the stated nonexistence and strict positivity conclusions.
Weighted result (paper Theorem 1.4)\(0\le u_0\in C(\overline{\mathcal{H}}\setminus\{\xi(0)\})\) with a global bound by a positive harmonic reference profile (defined precisely in §3.4). The admissible exponents are developed in Chapters 3 and 9; this class may include singular data and does not require compact support.

These are hypotheses of different results, not interchangeable definitions. The zero datum gives the zero solution, regardless of the coupling; hence it must be excluded in nonexistence statements.

Compatibility at the initial time. A classical solution smooth up to the corner \(t=0\) may require \(\partial_\nu u_0=Vu_0\) there. Definition 2.3 below imposes an initial value and continuity away from the graph, but does not prescribe a normal derivative at \(t=0\). In particular, the integral formulation accommodates compact continuous data without a classical corner compatibility condition.

\(C_c(\overline{\mathcal{H}})\) denotes continuous functions with compact support on the closed half-space; they need not vanish on the boundary. The measure \(d\sigma\) is Lebesgue surface measure on the boundary. Continuity on \(D_\xi\) is understood in the relative topology.

Background: Sobolev spaces and boundary traces
\[H^1(\mathcal{H})=\{w\in L^2(\mathcal{H}):\partial_jw\in L^2(\mathcal{H})\ (1\le j\le N)\}.\]

Here the derivatives are weak derivatives. The Sobolev trace \(\operatorname{Tr}:H^1(\mathcal{H})\to L^2(\partial \mathcal{H})\) extends boundary values of smooth functions. The definition of integral solution uses continuous boundary values away from the singularity; identifying these with Sobolev traces requires a separate argument.

For precise definitions, the trace inequality and weak convergence, see Appendix B — Functional analysis primer.

2.2 What do we mean by a solution?

The singular boundary equation is not meaningful pointwise at the pole. Away from it, a classical solution has the indicated derivatives, but the existence theorem uses a formulation that can be justified by positive approximation. Here is the distinction before deriving the integral equation.

NotionRequired conditions
Classical solutionThe necessary derivatives exist, and the equation and boundary conditions hold pointwise.
Weak solutionAn integral identity holds against test functions, with appropriate Sobolev traces.
Integral solutionSatisfies the continuity, initial condition and finite integral equality in Definition 2.3.

Definition 2.3 below will specify the integral class exactly. A Sobolev trace is the boundary value defined by continuous extension from smooth functions in an energy norm; see Appendix B.3. No equivalence is assumed without proof.

2.3 Heat kernel and reflection

The free-space heat kernel has unit mass and is the fundamental solution describing diffusion from initial data concentrated at a point.

Derivation by Fourier transform

For smooth rapidly decreasing initial data on \(\mathbb R^N\), use the unitary convention \(\widehat f(\zeta)=(2\pi)^{-N/2}\int e^{-ix\cdot\zeta}f(x)\,dx\). Taking the spatial transform gives the scalar ODE

\[\partial_t\widehat u=-|\zeta|^2\widehat u,\qquad \widehat u(\zeta,t)=e^{-t|\zeta|^2}\widehat f(\zeta).\]

The Gaussian Fourier integral and the convolution theorem then give \(u=g_t*f\), where \(\widehat g_t=(2\pi)^{-N/2}e^{-t|\zeta|^2}\). This yields the formula below; direct differentiation verifies it without requiring Fourier theory.

\[g_t(z)=(4\pi t)^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{|z|^2}{4t}\right),\qquad t>0.\tag{2.4}\]

Derivation · Basic heat kernel properties

Direct differentiation gives

\[\partial_tg_t=\left(-\frac N{2t}+\frac{|z|^2}{4t^2}\right)g_t=\Delta g_t.\]

Gaussian integration gives \(\int_{\mathbb R^N}g_t=1\). Substitution \(z=\sqrt t\,w\) shows that \(g_t\) concentrates at the origin as \(t\downarrow0\). This is the approximate identity property.

To satisfy the Neumann condition on the half-space, reflect a source \(y=(y',y_N)\) to \(y^*=(y',-y_N)\). Adding the two free-space kernels cancels their normal derivatives at the boundary.

Definition 2.2 · Neumann heat kernel
\[G(x,y,t)=g_t(x-y)+g_t(x-y^*).\tag{2.5}\]

Proof · Derivative at the boundary

Let the source on the half-line be \(a>0\) and let \(g_t^{(1)}\) denote the one-dimensional heat kernel. Using \(\partial_xg_t^{(1)}(x-a)=-(x-a)g_t^{(1)}(x-a)/(2t)\), we obtain

\[\left.\partial_x G(x,a,t)\right|_{x=0}=\frac a{2t}g_t^{(1)}(-a)-\frac a{2t}g_t^{(1)}(a)=0.\]

The final equality follows from evenness of the Gaussian. For the half-space, the same calculation applies in the normal direction at \(x_N=0\).□

2.4 Neumann heat semigroup

Define the operator that evolves initial data \(f\) under free Neumann heat flow.

\[P_tf(x)=\int_{\mathcal{H}}G(x,y,t)f(y)\,dy,\qquad P_0f=f.\tag{2.6}\]
Lemma 2.1 · Neumann composition (research paper Lemma 2.1)

For \(a,b>0\),

\[\int_{\mathcal{H}}G(x,z,a)G(z,y,b)\,dz=G(x,y,a+b).\tag{2.7}\]

Consequently \(P_aP_b=P_{a+b}\) for functions for which the integrals are defined.

Why the initial value is recovered

For bounded continuous \(f\) on the closed half-space, extend it evenly across the plane, calling the extension \(\widetilde f\). Then \(P_tf=(g_t*\widetilde f)|_\mathcal{H}\). Fix \(x\) and split the convolution difference into \(|z|<\delta\) and \(|z|\ge\delta\).

\[|P_tf(x)-f(x)|\le \sup_{|z|<\delta}|\widetilde f(x-z)-\widetilde f(x)|+2\|f\|_\infty\int_{|w|\ge\delta/\sqrt t}g_1(w)\,dw.\]

Choose \(\delta\) by continuity and then send \(t\downarrow0\); the Gaussian tail vanishes. In \(L^2\), Minkowski's inequality and continuity of translations in \(L^2(\mathbb R^N)\) yield the same conclusion in norm. The latter translation fact follows by approximating by smooth compactly supported functions.

□

Proof sketch

Reflect the integration variable in the image term of the first kernel. Since the second kernel is even in the normal variable, the two half-space integrals combine into a whole-space Gaussian convolution. The identity \(g_a*g_b=g_{a+b}\) yields the result. The formula also holds for boundary source points, where the two terms coincide.□

The same reflection argument gives \(P_t1=1\). Pointwise initial convergence for bounded continuous data and strong initial convergence for \(L^2\) data follow from the approximate identity property.

Lemma 2.1a · L² contraction of the Neumann semigroup

For every \(f\in L^2(\mathcal{H})\) and \(t\ge0\),

\[\|P_tf\|_{L^2(\mathcal{H})}\le\|f\|_{L^2(\mathcal{H})}.\tag{2.6a}\]

Proof · Jensen's inequality

For \(t>0\), the positive symmetric kernel satisfies \(\int_{\mathcal{H}} G(x,y,t)\,dy=1\) and, by symmetry, \(\int_{\mathcal{H}} G(x,y,t)\,dx=1\). Cauchy–Schwarz with respect to the probability measure \(G(x,y,t)dy\) (equivalently Jensen's inequality) gives

\[|P_tf(x)|^2\le \int_{\mathcal{H}}G(x,y,t)|f(y)|^2\,dy.\]

Integrating in \(x\) and using Tonelli's theorem yields

\[\begin{aligned}\|P_tf\|_2^2&\le\int_{\mathcal{H}} |f(y)|^2\left(\int_{\mathcal{H}} G(x,y,t)\,dx\right)dy\\&=\|f\|_2^2.\end{aligned}\]

The case \(t=0\) is equality. The nonnegative integral on the right is finite for almost every \(x\), so the argument also establishes the bounded operator on \(L^2\).

□

Why we need this. The free heat evolution cannot increase the squared \(L^2\) norm. Combined with positivity, the approximate identity property and the sharp energy estimate, this will help recover the strong initial trace in Chapter 7. See Appendix B (preliminary) for the underlying spaces.

Example 2.1 · Evolution of Gaussian initial data

Let \(f(x)=\exp(-|x|^2/(4b_0))\), \(b_0>0\). Its even extension is \((4\pi b_0)^{N/2}g_{b_0}\), so the semigroup law gives

\[P_tf(x)=\left(\frac{b_0}{b_0+t}\right)^{N/2}e^{-|x|^2/(4(b_0+t))}.\tag{2.8}\]

Figure 1.1 also plots a section of this formula. Since these initial data are not compactly supported, this is an example of free heat evolution rather than an application of the main theorem.

2.5 Boundary integral representation

Instead of treating the singular Robin condition directly, use the Neumann heat kernel to express the boundary contribution as an integral. Fix an interior observation point \(x\in \mathcal{H}\) and time \(t>0\). Assume sufficient smoothness and decay to discard boundary terms at spatial infinity.

Step 1 · Multiply by the backward heat kernel

\[I(s)=\int_{\mathcal{H}}G(x,y,t-s)u(y,s)\,dy,\qquad 0<s<t.\]

Using \(\partial_sG=-\Delta_yG\) and \(\partial_su=\Delta u\) produces the difference of two interior integrals.

\[I'(s)=\int_{\mathcal{H}}(G\Delta u-u\Delta_yG)\,dy.\]

Step 2 · Extract the boundary term with Green’s identity

\[I'(s)=\int_{\partial \mathcal{H}}(G\partial_\nu u-u\partial_\nu G)\,d\sigma.\]

The Neumann heat kernel satisfies \(\partial_\nu G=0\). Substitute the Robin condition.

\[I'(s)=\lambda\int_{\partial \mathcal{H}}G(x,y,t-s)\frac{u(y,s)}{|y-\xi(s)|}\,d\sigma(y).\]

Step 3 · Integrate in time and use the initial condition

The limit as \(s\uparrow t\) is \(u(x,t)\) by the approximate identity property; at \(s=0\) the term is \(P_tu_0(x)\). Integrating in time yields the following equation.

Boundary integral equation (research paper (1.3))
\[\begin{aligned}u(x,t)={}&P_tu_0(x)\\&+\lambda\int_0^t\!\int_{\partial \mathcal{H}}G(x,y,t-s)\frac{u(y,s)}{|y-\xi(s)|}\,d\sigma(y)\,ds.\end{aligned}\tag{2.9}\]

The boundary operator and positivity

For a nonnegative measurable function \(v\) on the nonsingular cylinder define, allowing infinity,

\[(A_\xi v)(x,t)=\lambda\int_0^t\int_{\partial \mathcal{H}}G(x,y,t-s)\frac{v(y,s)}{|y-\xi(s)|}\,d\sigma(y)\,ds.\]

See boundary mass identity (2.10) and Definition 2.3.

We assign the integrand zero on the singular graph; this changes no surface-time integral: each time section is a singleton of surface measure zero. The kernel and coefficient are nonnegative. Thus \(0\le v\le w\) implies \(A_\xi v\le A_\xi w\). The integral equation is \(u=F+A_\xi u\), with \(F=P_\cdot u_0\). For bounded nonnegative coefficients, repeated substitution motivates the positive series \(F+AF+A^2F+\cdots\). In the singular setting the formal iterates can be infinite; existence requires a separate finiteness and regularity argument.

Example · A scalar Volterra equation

As a simplified analogue, solve \(w(t)=1+b\int_0^t w(s)\,ds\) for \(b\ge0\). Starting at \(w_0=1\), the successive iterates are \(w_k(t)=\sum_{j=0}^k(bt)^j/j!\), increasing to \(e^{bt}\). This explicit toy model illustrates positive iteration, not the singular PDE.

Exact scalar formula with b=1; the dashed curve is the limit. Play increases the iteration index k, not physical time. The analogous PDE iterates require separate finiteness and continuity estimates.

The free diffusion term is augmented by the accumulated contributions of past boundary flux to the observation point. Because the unknown boundary value \(u(y,s)\) also appears on the right-hand side, this is not merely an integral of a prescribed source.

Lemma 2.2 · Boundary mass of the heat kernel (research paper (2.1))
\[\int_{\partial \mathcal{H}}G(x,y,h)\,d\sigma(y)=\frac{e^{-x_N^2/(4h)}}{\sqrt{\pi h}},\qquad h>0.\tag{2.10}\]

On the boundary \(y=y^*\), so the kernel is twice the Gaussian. Integration over the \(N-1\) tangential dimensions gives the displayed identity. Integrating the short-time part gives \(\int_0^\eta h^{-1/2}dh=2\sqrt\eta\), which is useful for continuity estimates with bounded boundary densities.

Formal derivation. The preceding Green’s identity calculation is a formal derivation under sufficient regularity, integrability and decay. Those assumptions cannot be imposed without justification near the singularity, so the paper adopts the following definition of integral solution.

2.6 Nonnegative integral solutions and minimality

For a rigorous Green identity calculation one can first use a bounded continuous Robin coefficient and smooth solutions with adequate spatial decay, integrate on a truncated domain and over \(s\in[0,t-\eta]\), then remove the spatial and temporal cutoffs using the stated integrability and approximate identity. In the singular problem, excising a neighborhood of the moving point would introduce additional boundary terms; their disappearance is not automatic. The paper instead constructs bounded approximations and passes to the limit in the integral equation. The definition below does not assume a classical solution at the singularity.

Definition 2.3 · Nonnegative integral solution (research paper Definition 1.1)

A real-valued function \(u\in C(D_\xi)\) is a nonnegative integral solution if it is nonnegative and satisfies the following conditions.

  1. \(u(x,0)=u_0(x)\) for \(x\in\overline{\mathcal{H}}\setminus\{\xi(0)\}\).
  2. For every \((x,t)\in \mathcal{H}\times(0,T)\), (2.9) holds with finite right-hand side.

The solution \(u\) is minimal if \(u\le v\) on \(D_\xi\) for every nonnegative integral solution \(v\) with the same initial data.

Continuity is required on \(D_\xi\), the closed half-space cylinder with the singular graph removed. By contrast, the definition requires the integral equality only in the interior \(\mathcal{H}\times(0,T)\). Lemma 2.3 of the paper establishes a supersolution inequality at nonsingular boundary and terminal observations; the solution constructed in research paper [4], Theorem 1.1 satisfies equality there as well.

Example 2.2 · Minimality versus uniqueness

Minimality is an order property: if other nonnegative solutions exist, the minimal solution lies below all of them. It differs from uniqueness, which means that all solutions in the specified class coincide. The latter is established under separate weighted assumptions in this study.

Lemma 2.3 · Supersolution inequality at nonsingular observations (paper Lemma 2.3)

For compact continuous data or nonnegative continuous harmonic-weighted data off the initial pole, with \(0<\alpha<N-2\), any nonnegative integral solution \(v\) satisfies, at every \((x,t)\in D_\xi\) with \(t>0\),

\[v(x,t)\ge P_tu_0(x)+(A_\xi v)(x,t).\tag{2.9a}\]

No weight bound is required of the competing solution. Only the initial datum must belong to one of the two stated classes.

Proof · Extend the inequality by Fatou's lemma

Choose interior observations \((x_j,t_j)\to(x,t)\), with \(0<t_j<T\). The free terms converge by dominated convergence. For compact data this follows from boundedness and compact support. For weighted data, write \(p=\xi(0)\) and use \(u_0(y)\le C|y-p|^{-\alpha}\), by (9.1). On a compact neighborhood of the positive-time observation, all elapsed times are bounded below by \(t/2>0\). Both direct and reflected kernels admit the uniform majorant \(C_t\exp[-((|y-p|-R)_+)^2/(4T)]\) for a sufficiently large R, since reflection preserves distance to the boundary point p. Multiplication by \(|y-p|^{-\alpha}\) gives an integrable volume function: \(\alpha<N\) at the pole and the Gaussian controls infinity. Hence the free term converges also for this noncompact, possibly unbounded datum. The competitor's boundary values need not satisfy any weighted estimate. Extend each nonnegative boundary integrand by zero for \(s\ge t_j\). For almost every boundary space-time point with \(s<t\) and \(y\ne\xi(s)\), the kernel converges to \(G(x,y,t-s)\). Fatou's lemma therefore bounds the limit inferior of the integrals below by \(A_\xi v(x,t)\). Continuity of \(v\) on \(D_\xi\) gives (2.9a). In particular its right-hand side is finite.

□

2.7 Positive iteration and approximation

The scalar example above shows how repeatedly adding a nonnegative contribution can converge. For the actual boundary equation the iterates may instead grow without bound; the next propositions separate order and convergence from the independent question of finiteness.

Proposition 2.1 · Positive iteration and conditional minimality

Set \(F(x,t)=P_tu_0(x)\) and, on \(D_\xi\), let

\[u^{(0)}=F,\qquad u^{(k+1)}=F+A_\xi u^{(k)}.\tag{2.9b}\]

At \(t=0\) take all iterates equal to \(u_0\). The iterates are nonnegative measurable functions, allowed to take the value \(+\infty\) at positive times. They increase pointwise and are dominated by any nonnegative integral solution with the same compact datum.

Proof · Two inductions and the limit

Step 1 (monotonicity). \(u^{(1)}\ge u^{(0)}\) since \(A_\xi\) is positive. If \(u^{(k)}\ge u^{(k-1)}\), applying the order-preserving operator and adding \(F\) gives the next inequality.

\[0\le u^{(0)}\le u^{(1)}\le\cdots.\]

Step 2 (comparison). For any solution \(v\), Lemma 2.3 gives \(v\ge F\), including boundary and terminal observations. If \(u^{(k)}\le v\), then \(u^{(k+1)}\le F+A_\xi v\le v\). This is why one needs the supersolution inequality on the boundary, not just interior equality.

Step 3 (limit). Define \(S=\sup_k u^{(k)}\). Monotone convergence gives \(S=F+A_\xi S\) in the extended nonnegative sense, and the comparison gives \(S\le v\) for every such solution. If \(S\) is finite and continuous on \(D_\xi\) with the prescribed initial trace, it is an integral solution and therefore minimal.

□

Why we need this. The scalar Volterra example illustrates increasing iterates in a case where the limit is explicit. In the singular PDE the limit need not be finite; this conditional proposition is not an existence proof. Chapter 7 establishes the necessary estimates through bounded approximations, and Chapter 8 uses comparison with divergent approximations to exclude finite solutions.

Proposition 2.2 · Unified positive-series principle (supplementary)

Let \(V\ge0\) be Borel and finite almost everywhere on the boundary space-time cylinder, with arbitrary values on null sets. Let \(f\ge0\) be Borel in the volume and suppose \(F=P_\cdot f\) is finite and continuous at all positive-time observations of the closed half-space. Define the series at all positive-time points of the closed half-space, allowing infinity, and use the prescribed initial value at time zero:

\[\begin{aligned}(A_V w)(x,t)&=\int_0^t\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,t-s)V(y,s)w(y,s)\,d\sigma ds,\\ S(V,f)&=\sum_{k=0}^\infty A_V^k F.\end{aligned}\tag{2.11}\]

Products of nonnegative extended quantities use the convention \(0\cdot\infty=0\). The following statements hold.

  1. The series is measurable, is a fixed point in the extended nonnegative sense, and is increasing in both \(V\) (almost everywhere) and \(f\) (almost everywhere in volume, for positive time).
  2. Every nonnegative supersolution of the full kernel inequality dominates the series. In the compact or harmonic-weighted data classes of Lemma 2.3, if the series is finite and continuous on \(D_\xi\) with the prescribed initial value, it is the minimal integral solution.
  3. If \(V_j\uparrow V\) almost everywhere, then \(S(V_j,f)\uparrow S(V,f)\) at every positive-time observation. If merely \(V_j\to V\) almost everywhere and \((x_j,t_j)\to(x,t)\) with \(t>0\), then \(\liminf_j S(V_j,f)(x_j,t_j)\ge S(V,f)(x,t)\).
  4. With the additional domination \(V_j\le V\) almost everywhere and a finite continuous limit on \(D_\xi\), convergence is locally uniform there, provided the common free evolution recovers the initial datum continuously off the initial pole. In contrast, divergence at a positive-time observation of the limiting series forces divergence of the approximations without upper domination.
Proof · Measurability, Tonelli, monotone convergence and sequential Fatou

Extend kernels by zero for \(s\ge t\). Parameter integration of nonnegative Borel functions is measurable by Appendix B.9. Each finite partial sum is the corresponding iterate from Proposition 2.1. Positivity and monotone convergence in the sum show \(S=F+A_VS\), even if the value is infinite. If a supersolution dominates F, induction shows that it dominates every finite sum. Lemma 2.3 supplies this inequality at nonsingular boundary and terminal observations for integral competitors; assigning zero on the null graph does not alter an integral. The finite continuous series with initial trace therefore solves the equation and is minimal.

For increasing coefficients, induction and monotone convergence in each integral prove convergence term by term, followed by monotone convergence for counting measure. For general convergence write \(F_{j,k}=A_{V_j}^k F\) and \(F_k=A_V^k F\). At positive times the base case follows from continuity of F. Assume the sequential lower bound holds for term k, in particular at each fixed source point. For almost every \((y,s)\) with \(0<s<t\), the moving observation kernel tends to the limiting kernel and \(V_j(y,s)\to V(y,s)\); the inductive inequality gives the lower bound on the product (including zero coefficients by nonnegativity). Fatou proves the inequality for term k+1. This induction applies also on the boundary, where the kernel is defined at positive elapsed times. Applying Fatou to counting measure proves the assertion for the full series. For nontrivial null exceptional sets of coefficients the same set can be removed at every integration, by Tonelli.

If dominated, termwise order gives \(S_j\le S\). For a convergent sequence of observations with positive limiting time, the lower bound and continuity give convergence to \(S(x,t)\). At a nonsingular time-zero observation, use \(F\le S_j\le S\) and continuity of the initial trace. At exactly zero time all values are the common datum. A failure of uniform convergence on a compact subset would give a sequence of observations with discrepancy bounded below by a positive constant; a convergent subsequence contradicts the sequential conclusion. The divergent case follows immediately from the extended lower bound. Neither finiteness nor continuity of an arbitrary positive series is asserted. □

This proposition unifies the mechanisms used in Chapters 7–10, 12 and 13. See dominated approximation, local uniform convergence and divergence and trajectory lower semicontinuity. The latter applies the assertion at fixed observation sequences because uniform convergence of the trajectories gives coefficient convergence off a countable union of null graphs.

2.8 Why the dimension matters

Optional proof · Why dimension two is obstructed (full treatment in Chapter 12)

Suppose a finite nonnegative integral solution exists with nonzero nonnegative compact continuous initial data. Positivity of the Neumann kernel gives \(F(y,s)=P_su_0(y)>0\) for \(s>0\), and continuity extends \(u\ge F\) to the nonsingular boundary. Fix an interior observation \((x,t)\) and \(0<a<b<t\). On the compact moving boundary neighborhood \(a\le s\le b,\ |y-\xi(s)|\le r\), the continuous product \(G(x,y,t-s)F(y,s)\) has a strictly positive minimum \(c\).

\[ A_\xi u(x,t)\ge c\lambda\int_a^b\int_{0<|y-\xi(s)|<r}\frac{d\sigma(y)\,ds}{|y-\xi(s)|}=+\infty\quad(N=2).\]

Tonelli's theorem justifies this lower bound. It contradicts the finite integral equation. This is the argument of research paper [4], Proposition 6.1, not a claim about every possible generalized solution class.

In polar coordinates, the local boundary integral of the inverse-distance coefficient is, up to a constant factor, \(\int_0^r\rho^{N-3}\,d\rho\). For \(N=2\) this diverges, leading to nonexistence of finite integral solutions for nonzero, nonnegative, compactly supported continuous data (paper Proposition 6.1; see Chapter 12). However, local integrability of the coefficient for \(N\ge3\) does not by itself guarantee existence.

Chapter 12 develops the full obstruction and quantitative divergence. Local integrability alone does not decide solvability in higher dimensions.

Chapter 2 · Takeaways

The Neumann kernel encodes reflection, preserves mass and contracts the \(L^2\) norm. The integral formulation adds the history of boundary interaction. Positivity gives a comparison principle for iterates; existence additionally requires finiteness and continuity away from the graph.

Check your understanding

Why is the image term needed? Why must the comparison extend to boundary values? Does a minimal solution imply uniqueness?

Answers: to cancel the normal derivative; the boundary operator evaluates its input on the boundary; no, minimality only asserts order relative to other solutions.

Before Part II, review the three data classes, L² contraction, positive iteration and the functional analysis primer.

Sources
Source correspondence: [4] §1 (1.1)–(1.3), Definition 1.1, §2, research paper [4], Proposition 6.1; [5] §§2.1–2.7.

Chapter 3 states the existence and nonexistence results for this solution class.

Part II

3. Main theorems

For which couplings and trajectories can a finite solution exist?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Definition 2.3 and positive iteration. The harmonic profile is defined here; its derivation is deferred to Chapter 6.

Define the threshold and motion condition → state four theorems → distinguish hypotheses and solution classes.

3.1 The threshold and the motion condition

Call a coupling subcritical if it lies below the threshold, critical if equal, and supercritical if above. The constant is a mathematical boundary between regimes, not an observed peak temperature. The exact formula is a preview of Chapter 6.

We now return to the integral problem defined in Chapter 2. Throughout this chapter, assume \(N\ge3\), \(T>0\), \(\lambda>0\), and \(\xi\in C([0,T];\partial\mathcal H)\). The threshold is the constant

\[\lambda_N=2\left(\frac{\Gamma(N/4)}{\Gamma((N-2)/4)}\right)^2.\tag{3.1}\]

The Euler Gamma function is \(\Gamma(z)=\int_0^\infty r^{z-1}e^{-r}\,dr\) for \(z>0\). Its recurrence \(\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z)\) follows by integration by parts. We call \(\lambda<\lambda_N\) subcritical, equality critical, and \(\lambda>\lambda_N\) supercritical. The names refer to the sharp form bound, not to a numerical temperature threshold.

Some results require the following additional property:

Assumption (M) · Uniformly vanishing parabolic displacement
\[\omega(h)=\sup_{\substack{0\le s<t\le T\\0<t-s\le h}}\frac{|\xi(t)-\xi(s)|}{\sqrt{t-s}},\qquad \lim_{h\downarrow0}\omega(h)=0.\tag{3.2}\]

Continuity alone does not imply (M). The supremum concerns every sufficiently short time interval, not merely increments from time zero. See Chapter 5 for examples.

3.2 Global existence

Theorem 3.1 · Global existence (paper Theorem 1.1)

Suppose \(u_0\in C_c(\overline{\mathcal H})\), \(u_0\ge0\), \(u_0\not\equiv0\), and \(0<\lambda\le\lambda_N\). Then:

  1. A minimal nonnegative integral solution exists on \([0,T]\). Its integral representation is finite and valid at every \((x,t)\in D_\xi\) with \(t>0\), and \(u(x,t)>0\) there.
  2. Writing \(\|\cdot\|_2\) for the spatial \(L^2(\mathcal H)\) norm,
    \[\sup_{0\le t\le T}\|u(t)\|_2\le\|u_0\|_2,\qquad \lim_{t\downarrow0}\|u(t)-u_0\|_2=0.\tag{3.3}\]
  3. When \(\lambda<\lambda_N\), additionally \(u\in L^2(0,T;H^1(\mathcal H))\) and
    \[\|u(t)\|_2^2+2\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_N}\right)\int_0^t\|\nabla u(s)\|_2^2\,ds\le\|u_0\|_2^2\quad(0\le t\le T).\tag{3.4}\]

The critical case is included without a Hölder assumption on the trajectory. Minimality compares solutions by pointwise order; it does not claim that every solution agrees with the constructed one. The factor in front of the integrated gradient in (3.4) vanishes at equality. The theorem makes no global gradient claim there.

Proof roadmap

Approximate the singular coefficient by bounded nonnegative Robin coefficients that increase to it. Translation of the sharp Kato inequality gives a uniform spatial form estimate at each time without differentiating the trajectory. The energy bound, local boundedness and continuity away from the graph permit the limit. Positivity of the kernel yields strict positivity and the order argument in Chapter 2 establishes minimality. The detailed proof belongs to Chapter 7.

3.3 Supercritical nonexistence

Theorem 3.2 · Nonexistence (paper Theorem 1.2)

For the same compact nonnegative nonzero datum, assume \(\lambda>\lambda_N\) and (M). For every \(S\in(0,T]\), there is no finite nonnegative integral solution on \([0,S]\).

This excludes even local-in-time solvability in the specified class. It is stronger than a statement about a large temperature at the singular point: the integral formulation already excludes that point. The proof uses stationary supercritical divergence and a lower Gaussian comparison in moving coordinates. A negative quadratic form alone is not the entire nonexistence proof.

Example 3.1 · The role of the dimension

Using \(\Gamma(1)=1\) and \(\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt\pi\), we obtain \(\lambda_4=2/\pi\). For \(N=6\), \(\Gamma(3/2)=\sqrt\pi/2\) gives \(\lambda_6=\pi/2\). Thus the same coupling \(\lambda=1\) is supercritical in dimension four and subcritical in dimension six. With a Lipschitz trajectory and the stated compact data, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2 give opposite conclusions in these two dimensions.

3.4 Harmonic weights and the singularity estimate

The remaining two theorems quantify the behavior close to the moving point. A useful way to guess a spatial weight is to freeze the center at zero and seek a stationary positive function with a power-law singularity. Since a stationary heat solution is harmonic in the interior, prescribe the boundary value \(|x'|^{-\alpha}\) and extend it harmonically. Differentiation in the normal direction decreases the homogeneity degree by one, exactly as multiplication by \(|x'|^{-1}\) does. The resulting normal derivative is \(C_N(\alpha)|x'|^{-1}\Phi_\alpha\); the scalar coefficient is computed in §6.4. A stationary harmonic weight is not an assertion that the moving solution itself is harmonic. When \(C_N(\alpha)>\lambda\), the excess supplies a useful margin in the upper comparison argument. More precisely, introduce the Poisson extension of the boundary power \(|x'|^{-\alpha}\), denoted here by \(\Phi_\alpha\) (the paper uses \(H_\alpha\)). For \(x_N>0\), define

\[\Phi_\alpha(x)=\int_{\mathbb R^{N-1}}\mathcal P_{x_N}(x'-y')|y'|^{-\alpha}\,dy',\quad \mathcal P_z(w)=\frac{\Gamma(N/2)}{\pi^{N/2}}\frac{z}{(|w|^2+z^2)^{N/2}}.\tag{3.5}\]

Here \(0<\alpha<N-2\). On the boundary away from zero the profile is defined by its trace \(|x'|^{-\alpha}\). It is positive, harmonic in the interior, homogeneous of degree \(-\alpha\), and comparable to \(|x|^{-\alpha}\). The homogeneity follows directly by substituting \(y'=r w'\) in (3.5); Chapter 6 develops the remaining properties and the normal derivative identity. Its characteristic coefficient is

\[C_N(\alpha)=2\frac{\Gamma((N-1-\alpha)/2)\Gamma((\alpha+1)/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha/2)\Gamma((N-2-\alpha)/2)}.\tag{3.6}\]

The maximum occurs at \(\alpha_*=(N-2)/2\) and equals \(\lambda_N\). For \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\), the two roots of \(C_N(\alpha)=\lambda\) satisfy \(0<\alpha_-<\alpha_*<\alpha_+<N-2\). This is a result proved using the Gamma-function properties in Chapter 6; the plotted curve below is an illustration, not its proof.

Theorem 3.3 · Upper singularity estimate (paper Theorem 1.3)

Assume \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\), (M), and the compact-data assumptions of Theorem 3.1. Given any \(\eta>0\), its minimal solution satisfies

\[u(x,t)\le C_\eta |x-\xi(t)|^{-\alpha_-(\lambda)-\eta},\quad (x,t)\in D_\xi,\quad 0<|x-\xi(t)|\le1.\tag{3.7}\]

The positive constant may depend on the dimension, coupling, terminal time, trajectory, datum and \(\eta\).

This is an upper bound, not an asymptotic equality. It neither proves a matching lower bound nor establishes that the endpoint exponent \(\alpha_-\) itself is attainable. We use \(\eta\) rather than the paper's \(\varepsilon\) here to distinguish the exponent loss from numerical regularization.

Theorem 3.4 · Weighted existence and uniqueness (paper Theorem 1.4)

Suppose \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\), (M), \(\alpha_-(\lambda)<\alpha<\alpha_*\), and \(0\le u_0\in C(\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{\xi(0)\})\) satisfies

\[\sup_{x\in\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{\xi(0)\}}\frac{u_0(x)}{\Phi_\alpha(x-\xi(0))}<\infty.\]

There is exactly one nonnegative integral solution in the class

\[\sup_{(x,t)\in D_\xi}\frac{u(x,t)}{\Phi_\alpha(x-\xi(t))}<\infty.\tag{3.8}\]

This solution is also minimal among all nonnegative integral solutions for that datum.

The weight permits certain singular initial data, but imposes global control at infinity as well. Uniqueness here is restricted to the weighted class. No nonzero assumption is needed for this theorem. For a compact continuous datum, the ratio is bounded because the weight is positive and comparable to the radial power on the support.

How the upper estimate follows from weighted existence

Choose \(\alpha\in(\alpha_-,\min\{\alpha_*,\alpha_-+\eta\})\). Both constructions give a minimal solution for compact data, so they agree by comparison in both directions. The weighted estimate gives \(u\le M\Phi_\alpha\le C|x-\xi(t)|^{-\alpha}\). For distances at most one this implies (3.7). The construction itself uses a short-time contraction from the upper kernel comparison and the harmonic heat identity; it will be proved in Chapter 9.

Corollary 3.5 · Classification under (M) (paper Corollary 1.5)

For nonnegative nonzero compact continuous data and a trajectory satisfying (M), existence on \([0,T]\) is equivalent to \(0<\lambda\le\lambda_N\).

Proof. The forward implication is Theorem 3.2 by contradiction; the converse is Theorem 3.1. □

For supplementary results, see the theorem catalogue. The weighted-regime comparison distinguishes the expanded moving-pole interval and stationary endpoint theorem. The singular energy identification and critical weighted-energy bound are supplementary, not part of the original theorem statements.

Chapter 3 · Takeaways

Existence includes criticality for all continuous paths. Nonexistence and weighted results require a separate motion condition. The weighted estimate is not an exact asymptotic or a general uniqueness theorem.

Check your understanding

At critical coupling, do we obtain a uniform integrated-gradient estimate? Does failure of (M) prove supercritical existence?

No to both. The displayed estimates and classification do not imply either conclusion.

Sources
Source correspondence: research paper [4], §1, Theorems 1.1–1.4, Corollary 1.5 and §5.1; revised thesis [5], §§1.3, 3.5–3.8 and Chapter 8. Examples and plots are explanatory additions.

The exact threshold is not yet derived. Chapter 4 explains the energy competition behind it.

Part II

4. Energy and critical coupling

Can the boundary input be controlled by the dissipation in the interior?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Appendix B.1 for the squared L² norm; B.2 and B.3 for gradients and traces.

Multiply the equation by u → integrate by parts → bound the boundary form → interpret the remaining margin.

4.1 Deriving the energy identity

In this chapter first work with smooth or bounded-Robin solutions for which integration by parts is justified. Diffusion removes squared L² energy, while positive Robin input adds it. The singular limit requires Chapter 7.

The total heat discussed in Chapter 1 is an integral of \(u\). The energy method instead studies its squared \(L^2\) norm. These quantities measure different features of the field. To justify the following computation first use a bounded continuous nonnegative boundary coefficient \(b(x,t)\) with sufficiently regular solutions and sufficient decay (or the weak formulation below). Multiplying the PDE by \(u\), integrating over the half-space, and integrating by parts in space gives

\[\begin{aligned}\frac12\frac{d}{dt}\|u(t)\|_2^2&=\int_{\mathcal H}u\Delta u\,dx\\&=-\int_{\mathcal H}|\nabla u|^2\,dx+\int_{\partial\mathcal H}b|\operatorname{Tr}u|^2\,d\sigma.\end{aligned}\tag{4.1}\]

The interior term is dissipative; the positive Robin term adds to the squared norm. For the original singular coefficient this identity is initially formal, because the regularity and boundary integrability required for it are not given a priori. We will apply the justified version to regularized solutions.

Weak formulation and the energy identity

Let \(\mathscr X=H^1(\mathcal H)\). For a weak solution in \(L^2(0,T;\mathscr X)\) with time derivative in \(L^2(0,T;\mathscr X^*)\), the identity for almost every time and each \(w\in\mathscr X\) is

\[\langle u_t,w\rangle_{\mathscr X^*,\mathscr X}+\int_{\mathcal H}\nabla u\cdot\nabla w\,dx=\int_{\partial\mathcal H}b\operatorname{Tr}u\operatorname{Tr}w\,d\sigma.\]

The Hilbert-triple energy chain rule gives an \(L^2\)-continuous representative and \(\frac12(\|u\|_2^2)'=\langle u_t,u\rangle\) almost everywhere. The bounded coefficient and ordinary trace bound (B.1) make the boundary pairing meaningful. Chapter 7 will justify this regularized solution framework.

4.2 The sharp boundary inequality

Theorem 4.1 · Sharp boundary Kato inequality

For \(N\ge3\), every \(w\in H^1(\mathcal H)\), and every \(a\in\partial\mathcal H\),

\[\lambda_N\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|\operatorname{Tr}w(x)|^2}{|x-a|}\,d\sigma(x)\le\int_{\mathcal H}|\nabla w|^2\,dx.\tag{4.2}\]

The coefficient \(\lambda_N\) is optimal. The proof is developed in Chapter 6.

Define the bulk Dirichlet energy and weighted boundary integral for a test function, with a boundary center \(a\in\partial\mathcal H\) (distinct from the scalar trajectory coordinate a(t) in Figure 1.4):

\[\begin{aligned}\mathcal E[w]&=\int_{\mathcal H}|\nabla w|^2\,dx,\\B_a[w]&=\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|\operatorname{Tr}w|^2}{|x-a|}\,d\sigma,\\Q_{\lambda,a}[w]&=\mathcal E[w]-\lambda B_a[w].\end{aligned}\tag{4.3}\]

The inequality says precisely that \(B_a[w]\le \mathcal E[w]/\lambda_N\). Thus

\[Q_{\lambda,a}[w]\ge\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_N}\right)\mathcal E[w].\tag{4.4}\]

Below criticality the form controls the full gradient norm. At equality it remains nonnegative but the uniform positive gradient factor disappears. Above criticality, sharpness ensures that some test function has negative form, not that all test functions do.

4.3 Why motion does not change the energy bound

For each fixed time, put \(a=\xi(t)\) and translate tangentially, defining \(w_a(z)=w(z+a)\). This preserves the half-space and both measures, so a change of variables gives

\[\mathcal E[w_a]=\mathcal E[w],\qquad B_0[w_a]=B_a[w].\]

Applying (4.2) with center zero to \(w_a\) proves the translated inequality. No derivative of \(\xi\) occurs. This is why the existence proof does not need condition (M).

For example, take \(b_n(x,t)=\lambda(|x-\xi(t)|^2+n^{-2})^{-1/2}\) on the boundary. For the regularized weak solution \(u_n\), since \(b_n\le\lambda/|x-\xi(t)|\) almost everywhere, (4.1) and (4.2) imply

\[\|u_n(t)\|_2^2+2\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_N}\right)\int_0^t\|\nabla u_n(s)\|_2^2\,ds\le\|u_0\|_2^2.\tag{4.5}\]

For \(\lambda\le\lambda_N\) this gives a uniform bound for all approximations. The gradient control is uniform only for strict subcriticality. Finiteness and continuity of the singular limit are separate arguments; see Chapter 7.

4.4 Why the threshold is sharp

Optimality of (4.2) means that the infimum of \(\mathcal E[w]/B_a[w]\) over \(w\in H^1(\mathcal H)\) with \(B_a[w]>0\) is \(\lambda_N\). Hence for \(\lambda>\lambda_N\) we can choose a test \(w\) with \(Q_{\lambda,0}[w]<0\). Normalize \(\|w\|_2=1\) and set \(w_\rho(x)=\rho^{-N/2}w(x/\rho)\) for \(\rho>0\). Changing variables in the volume and boundary integrals gives

\[\|w_\rho\|_2=1,\qquad \mathcal E[w_\rho]=\rho^{-2}\mathcal E[w],\qquad B_0[w_\rho]=\rho^{-2}B_0[w].\tag{4.6}\]

Thus \(Q_{\lambda,0}[w_\rho]=\rho^{-2}Q_{\lambda,0}[w]\to-\infty\) as \(\rho\downarrow0\). The stationary quadratic form is unbounded below on the unit sphere. Chapter 8 will show how this leads to divergence of stationary regularizations, and how a kernel comparison transfers the result to slow trajectories. Neither scaling nor this form calculation alone establishes nonexistence for arbitrary moving coefficients.

Example 4.1 · Comparing energy margins

At \(\lambda=\lambda_N/2\), the gradient margin is \(1/2\). At \(\lambda=0.99\lambda_N\) it is only \(0.01\). The energy estimate deteriorates as criticality is approached, even though the \(L^2\) bound persists at equality. This explains why the compactness argument cannot simply assume the same integrated-gradient bound in the critical case.

Chapter 4 · Takeaways

The threshold comes from a sharp trace estimate. Translation gives uniform control for arbitrary continuous motion. Criticality loses coercivity, not the nonnegative form bound.

Check your understanding

Why is no trajectory derivative needed? Does a negative form lower bound alone prove supercritical nonexistence?

Translation is performed separately at each time; no. Sharpness and stationary divergence plus moving-kernel comparison are needed.

Sources
Source correspondence: [4] §2 (form preliminaries), §3 (existence), §4 (stationary comparison and divergence); [5] §2.9, Chapter 3, §§4.1–4.2 and Chapter 7. The illustrative energy bars are not numerical solutions.

The energy bound is independent of the speed of motion. Chapter 5 explains where motion enters the comparison proof instead.

Part II

5. Parabolic scaling

How fast does the pole move compared with diffusion?

Prerequisites and proof outline

The Gaussian kernel and condition (M).

Identify the √h diffusion length → compare normalized displacements → shift Gaussian kernels and rescale time.

5.1 The characteristic diffusion length

The heat equation is invariant under simultaneous spatial and temporal rescaling \(x\mapsto r x\), \(t\mapsto r^2t\). The free heat kernel exhibits this invariance:

\[g_h(z)=h^{-N/2}g_1(z/\sqrt h),\qquad h>0.\tag{5.1}\]

This formula identifies \(\sqrt h\) as the spatial scale over a time interval of length \(h\). To quantify it, observe that the Gaussian factors into independent one-dimensional densities with variance \(2h\). Integration by parts gives

\[\int_{\mathbb R}r^2\frac{e^{-r^2/(4h)}}{\sqrt{4\pi h}}\,dr=2h,\qquad \int_{\mathbb R^N}|z|^2g_h(z)\,dz=2Nh.\tag{5.2}\]

Indeed \(g_h'(r)=-r g_h(r)/(2h)\) in one dimension, so integrating \(-2hr g_h'(r)\) gives \(2h\int g_h=2h\). Summing over coordinates yields the second identity. The root mean square displacement is \(\sqrt{2Nh}\); the Gaussian has nonzero tails at every distance, so this is not a finite propagation radius.

5.2 Comparing the motion with diffusion

Consider two times \(s<t\). In moving coordinates the Gaussian sees the shift \(d=\xi(t)-\xi(s)\) in its argument. The exponent contains a factor \(1/(t-s)\), so the meaningful size of the shift is \(|d|/\sqrt{t-s}\), not \(|d|\) alone.

\[\frac{|\xi(t)-\xi(s)|}{\sqrt{t-s}}\le\omega(h),\qquad 0<t-s\le h.\]

Condition (M) says the largest normalized shift on all sufficiently short intervals tends to zero. A Hölder estimate \(|\xi(t)-\xi(s)|\le L|t-s|^\gamma\) with \(\gamma>1/2\) gives

\[\omega(h)\le Lh^{\gamma-1/2}\longrightarrow0.\tag{5.3}\]

At \(\gamma=1/2\) this argument gives only \(\omega(h)\le L\), not vanishing. For smaller exponents this upper estimate does not imply either vanishing or divergence for every such path. A smoother path can also satisfy a weaker Hölder bound.

Example 5.1 · A sufficient condition, not an equivalence

Near zero take \(\xi(t)=\sqrt{t}/\log(e/t)\,e_1\), extended smoothly for positive times in a sufficiently short interval. This path is not Hölder of any exponent above \(1/2\), because its ratio to \(t^\gamma\) diverges as \(t\downarrow0\). Nevertheless (M) holds: for any \(\epsilon_0>0\), its derivative satisfies \(|\xi'(t)|\le\epsilon_0/(2\sqrt t)\) sufficiently near zero. Integrating on intervals in that region gives increments at most \(\epsilon_0\sqrt{t-s}\). Away from zero the derivative is bounded, giving a normalized increment of order \(\sqrt h\). Splitting intervals that cross the chosen cutoff proves uniform vanishing. This shows why (M) is the natural, weaker hypothesis.

Proposition 5.1 · The regularity hierarchy
\[C^\gamma\ (\gamma>1/2)\ \Longrightarrow\ (M)\ \Longrightarrow\ C^{1/2}\ \Longrightarrow\ C.\tag{5.3a}\]

Here the Hölder spaces refer to trajectories on the entire compact time interval. The first implication follows from the Hölder estimate; the last is immediate. For the middle implication, choose \(h_0\in(0,T]\) with \(\omega(h_0)\le1\). For \(t-s\le h_0\), the normalized increment is at most one. For larger increments it is at most \(2\sup_{r\in[0,T]}|\xi(r)|/\sqrt{h_0}\). Thus the global half-Hölder seminorm is finite.

Why none of the implications reverses

The logarithmically corrected square-root path in Example 5.1 satisfies (M) but belongs to no Hölder class with exponent above one half. The path \(\xi(t)=\sqrt t\,e_1\) is half-Hölder on \([0,1]\) but has \(\omega(h)=1\) for \(0<h\le1\). Finally, \(\xi(t)=t^{1/4}e_1\) is continuous but not half-Hölder: the normalized increments from zero tend to infinity. The revised thesis also considers \(\xi(t)=e_1/\log(e/t)\) for \(t>0\), with \(\xi(0)=0\); it is continuous but fails every positive Hölder order at zero.

5.3 From parabolic displacement to kernel comparison

A moving coordinate frame shifts the tangential Gaussian argument by the difference of the pole positions at the source and observation times. We need estimates comparing that shifted kernel with an unshifted one, not a differential equation involving a derivative of the trajectory.

Write \(U(x,t)=u(x+\xi(t),t)\) and define the translated kernel

\[K_\xi(x,y;t,s)=G(x+\xi(t),y+\xi(s),t-s).\tag{5.4}\]

Set \(f_0(y)=u_0(y+\xi(0))\). Because each translation is parallel to the boundary, both the half-space and surface measure are unchanged. In the boundary integral at time \(s\), substitute the original source point \(Y=y+\xi(s)\); the singular denominator becomes \(|y|\). Applying the same change at time zero gives the complete moving-frame identity:

\[\begin{aligned}U(x,t)={}&\int_{\mathcal H}K_\xi(x,y;t,0)f_0(y)\,dy\\ &+\lambda\int_0^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}K_\xi(x,y;t,s)\frac{U(y,s)}{|y|}\,d\sigma(y)\,ds.\end{aligned}\tag{5.4a}\]

This is an identity for interior observations in the integral-solution formulation; no differentiability of the trajectory is used. The Fatou inequality from §2.6 transfers to nonsingular boundary observations as well. Compare the original representation in §2.5.

For tangential shifts \(d=\xi(t)-\xi(s)\), the image term is shifted by the same vector. The elementary Young inequalities

\[\begin{aligned}|z+d|^2&\le(1+\delta)|z|^2+(1+\delta^{-1})|d|^2,\\|z+d|^2&\ge(1-\delta)|z|^2-(\delta^{-1}-1)|d|^2\end{aligned}\]

hold for the upper bound when \(\delta>0\), and for the lower bound when \(0<\delta<1\). Substituting in the Gaussian and accounting for its normalization gives the two-sided estimate below. For \(0<t-s\le\tau\) and \(M=\omega(\tau)<\infty\), set

\[D_\pm=(1\mp\delta)^{-1},\qquad c_\pm=(1\mp\delta)^{-N/2}\exp\left(\pm\frac{(1\mp\delta)M^2}{4\delta}\right).\]
\[c_-G(x,y,D_-(t-s))\le K_\xi(x,y;t,s)\le c_+G(x,y,D_+(t-s)).\tag{5.5}\]

The lower comparison alone allows any \(\delta>0\) (with \(D_-\) and \(c_-\)); both comparisons simultaneously use \(0<\delta<1\). To see the lower estimate, the upper quadratic inequality bounds the exponential from below; \(D_-^{-1}=1+\delta\) and the prefactor ratio is \(D_-^{N/2}\). The upper estimate follows similarly from the lower quadratic inequality. The same bounds apply to the image Gaussian, so adding preserves them.

Derivation of the effective coupling by time substitution

On an initial interval \([0,\tau]\), let \(D>0\) be either diffusivity factor and set \(W_D(y,q)=U(y,q/D)\). With \(q=Ds\) and \(r=Dt\), we have

\[\begin{aligned}&\lambda c\int_0^t\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,D(t-s))\frac{U(y,s)}{|y|}\,d\sigma\,ds\\ &=\frac{\lambda c}{D}\int_0^r\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,r-q)\frac{W_D(y,q)}{|y|}\,d\sigma\,dq.\end{aligned}\tag{5.5a}\]

Thus in the stationary comparison the new coefficient is \(\mu=\lambda c/D\), not just \(\lambda c\). The free term is bounded separately by \(cP_{Dt}f_0\) in the corresponding direction. On a restarted interval the same substitution uses \(q=D(s-a)\) for initial time \(a\). In the weighted argument choose \(\alpha\) with \(C_N(\alpha)>\lambda\), then make \(\mu_+<C_N(\alpha)\). For the lower comparison choose \(\mu_->\lambda_N\). These are strict inequalities; the full comparison and continuation arguments are deferred to Chapters 8–9.

Time substitution in a boundary integral produces the effective factor \(\lambda c_\pm/D_\pm\). First choose \(\delta\) small enough to keep this factor on the desired side of \(\lambda_N\) when \(M=0\); then use (M) to make \(M\) sufficiently small by choosing \(\tau\). This preserves a strict subcritical or supercritical margin, but does not provide the critical existence argument. Chapters 8 and 9 use these comparisons in detail.

Chapter 5 · Takeaways

Diffusion has spatial scale \(\sqrt h\), with no sharp finite propagation radius. Hölder regularity above one half is sufficient for (M), not necessary. The uniform modulus controls shifts in the moving Neumann kernel and enables comparison while preserving strict coupling margins.

Check your understanding

What is the difference between a finite normalized displacement at a single interval and a finite uniform modulus? Why is (M) unnecessary for the energy method?

The modulus is a supremum over arbitrarily short intervals; the energy estimate uses only a translation at a fixed time.

Sources
Source correspondence: [4] §1 (1.4), §4 Gaussian comparisons and §5 upper comparison; [5] §§2.8, 5.1–5.4. The diffusion moment calculation, regularity hierarchy proof and logarithmically corrected square-root example are explanatory derivations. Figure 5.3 evaluates Lemma 4.1 of [4].

Chapter 6 computes the sharp energy constant used in the proofs.

Part III

6. Sharp Kato inequality

Why does a harmonic function determine the optimal boundary trace constant?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Appendix B.2–B.3 for Sobolev functions and traces; Appendix C.1–C.4 for Fourier transforms, distributions and Gamma identities.

Variational quotient → harmonic extension → normal derivative multiplier → characteristic coefficient → ground-state identity → logarithmic test functions.

6.1 The variational question

In Theorem 4.1, the constant controlling the boundary interaction was given without proof. We now determine it. Throughout this chapter write \(d=N-1\), \(x=(x',x_N)\), and let \(a=0\); tangential translation recovers the general boundary center.

\[\lambda_N=\inf_{\substack{w\in C_c^\infty(\overline{\mathcal H})\\ B_0[w]>0}}\frac{\mathcal E[w]}{B_0[w]},\qquad B_0[w]=\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|w|^2}{|x'|}\,d\sigma.\tag{6.1}\]

Here smooth functions on the closed half-space are restrictions of smooth compactly supported functions on the whole space. They need not vanish on the plane. To establish (6.1) with the claimed value, we must prove a lower bound for every competitor and exhibit competitors whose quotients approach it. A formal singular profile alone would do neither.

For \(w_\rho(x)=w(x/\rho)\), changing variables in the volume and surface integrals gives

\[\begin{aligned}\mathcal E[w_\rho]&=\rho^{N-2}\mathcal E[w],\\ \int_{\partial\mathcal H}|x'|^{-p}|w_\rho|^2d\sigma&=\rho^{N-1-p}\int_{\partial\mathcal H}|x'|^{-p}|w|^2d\sigma.\end{aligned}\tag{6.2}\]

The exponents coincide only for \(p=1\). For example, at \(N=4\), halving the length scale multiplies both terms with \(p=1\) by \(1/4\). The dimensionless ratio is unchanged. This identifies the critical *power*, not yet the critical coefficient.

Proof roadmap
1 · ExtendPoisson kernel and Fourier analysis2 · DifferentiateBoundary Fourier multiplier3 · ConstructPositive critical harmonic profile4 · Lower boundGround-state identity: every quotient ≥ λₙ5 · OptimalityLogarithmic cutoffs approach λₙ6 · ExtendDensity gives the sharp Sobolev inequality

The two variational steps are independent requirements. The lower bound supplies the uniform energy estimate used in Chapter 7; sharpness supplies the negative-form tests used in Chapter 8. Appendix C develops the Fourier prerequisites.

6.2 Harmonic extension and the Poisson kernel

Given a boundary function \(f\), consider the elliptic problem \(\Delta v=0\) in \(\mathcal H\), \(v(x',0)=f(x')\). Unlike the original heat equation, the extension has no time variable. It is a tool for constructing a positive function with the desired normal derivative.

Definition 6.1 · Tangential Fourier transform

For \(f\in\mathcal S(\mathbb R^d)\), the space of smooth rapidly decreasing functions (all derivatives decrease faster than any power), use the unitary convention

\[\widehat f(\zeta)=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\mathbb R^d}e^{-ix'\cdot\zeta}f(x')\,dx'.\tag{6.3}\]

The inverse has the positive sign in the exponential. Integration by parts gives \(\widehat{\partial_j f}=i\zeta_j\widehat f\), and Plancherel gives \(\int|f|^2=\int|\widehat f|^2\). Plancherel extends the transform to square-integrable functions by density; the derivative identity then holds whenever the corresponding weak derivative lies in L². See Appendix C for the precise conventions and proofs.

Transform only the tangential coordinates. The equation becomes \(\partial_{x_N}^2\widehat v-|\zeta|^2\widehat v=0\). At each nonzero frequency its decaying solution is \(e^{-x_N|\zeta|}\widehat f(\zeta)\). At zero frequency the bounded solution is constant. This motivates the following representation.

Lemma 6.1 · Poisson extension

For \(z>0\), set

\[\mathcal P_z(x')=\frac{\Gamma((d+1)/2)}{\pi^{(d+1)/2}}\frac{z}{(|x'|^2+z^2)^{(d+1)/2}},\qquad v(x',z)=(\mathcal P_z*f)(x').\tag{6.4}\]

Then \(\mathcal P_z\) has mass one, is positive, and has convolution multiplier \(e^{-z|\zeta|}\). For Schwartz data, \(v\) is harmonic and has boundary trace \(f\).

Proof

Normalization. In polar coordinates, substituting \(r=zs\) reduces the integral to a constant times \(\int_0^\infty s^{d-1}(1+s^2)^{-(d+1)/2}ds=\tfrac12 B(d/2,1/2)\). Here \(B(a,b)=\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)/\Gamma(a+b)\), obtained by changing variables in the product of two gamma integrals. With \(|S^{d-1}|=2\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(d/2)\), the stated prefactor makes the integral one.

Harmonicity and multiplier. Direct differentiation of \(z(|x'|^2+z^2)^{-(d+1)/2}\) shows its Laplacian in \(d+1\) variables vanishes. Its Fourier multiplier \(m(\zeta,z)=\int e^{-ix'\cdot\zeta}\mathcal P_z(x')dx'\) thus solves the above ODE. Differentiation and integration by parts are justified at positive heights by the integrable decay of the kernel derivatives. Positivity and unit mass imply \(|m|\le1\), and scaling \(\mathcal P_z(x')=z^{-d}\mathcal P_1(x'/z)\) gives \(m(\zeta,z)\to1\) as \(z\downarrow0\) by dominated convergence. Hence \(m=e^{-z|\zeta|}\). The same scaling proves the approximate identity property: for any fixed \(\delta>0\), the mass outside \(|x'|\le\delta\) tends to zero. This yields the trace for continuous bounded data, in particular Schwartz data. □

6.3 Dirichlet-to-Neumann map and energy

Before defining the normal derivative operator abstractly, think of a boundary wave cos(kx₁). Its bounded harmonic extension is exp(−k x_N)cos(kx₁) for k>0, so the outward derivative on the boundary multiplies the wave by k. Fourier analysis makes this observation systematic.

The outward normal is \(-e_N\), so differentiation at the plane has the opposite sign to differentiation into the domain.

Definition 6.2 · Square-root Laplacian

For Schwartz functions define \((-\Delta_{x'})^{1/2}\) by its Fourier multiplier:

\[\widehat{(-\Delta_{x'})^{1/2}f}(\zeta)=|\zeta|\widehat f(\zeta).\tag{6.5}\]

Thus the harmonic extension has outward derivative \(\partial_\nu v=(-\Delta_{x'})^{1/2}f\). Applying the multiplier twice gives the usual Laplacian with negative sign. This is the Dirichlet-to-Neumann map: it converts prescribed boundary values into normal derivatives.

Lemma 6.2 · Least-energy extension

For \(w\in C_c^\infty(\overline{\mathcal H})\) with trace \(f\), and \(v=\mathcal P_{x_N}*f\),

\[\mathcal E[v]=\int_{\mathbb R^d}|\zeta|\,|\widehat f(\zeta)|^2d\zeta,\qquad \mathcal E[w]=\mathcal E[v]+\mathcal E[w-v].\tag{6.6}\]

Proof

Use Plancherel in the tangential variables. Both the tangential and normal gradients of the extension give the squared multiplier \(|\zeta|^2 e^{-2z|\zeta|}\). Integrating their sum in \(z\) produces \(|\zeta|\), proving the first equality. To see orthogonality, set \(W=\widehat w\) and \(k=|\zeta|\). For each nonzero frequency integrate

\[\int_0^\infty\left(k^2e^{-kz}\widehat f\overline W-ke^{-kz}\widehat f\,\partial_z\overline W\right)dz=k|\widehat f|^2.\]

The integrand is the derivative of \(-ke^{-kz}\widehat f\overline W\); the lower endpoint uses \(W(\zeta,0)=\widehat f(\zeta)\). Both gradient energies are finite, so Cauchy–Schwarz permits integration over frequencies. The cross inner product of \(\nabla v\) with \(\nabla(w-v)\) is zero. Expanding the squared norm gives the result. Only gradient integrability is needed for the identity. □

Why this matters. The bulk energy needed to realize a boundary trace is measured by a fractional-order operator on the boundary. We can therefore find the special Robin profile by studying powers under this operator rather than solving a new differential equation in all spatial variables.

6.4 Singular powers and the characteristic coefficient

The boundary power is singular at zero and does not have an ordinary absolutely integrable Fourier transform. The following computation uses distributions; review Appendix C.1 if that language is unfamiliar.

We need to transform \(|x'|^{-\alpha}\), which is not a Schwartz function. It is locally integrable for \(0<\alpha<d\) and defines a tempered distribution by integration against Schwartz tests. A tempered distribution is a continuous linear functional on the Schwartz class; its Fourier transform is defined by duality with the Fourier transform of test functions. This allows the singular power to be treated without assuming an absolutely integrable Fourier integral.

Lemma 6.3 · Fourier transform of a radial power
\[\mathcal F(|x'|^{-\alpha})(\zeta)=a(d,\alpha)|\zeta|^{\alpha-d},\qquad a(d,\alpha)=2^{d/2-\alpha}\frac{\Gamma((d-\alpha)/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha/2)},\quad 0<\alpha<d.\tag{6.7}\]

Proof by Gaussian mixtures

The gamma integral \(\Gamma(s)=\int_0^\infty r^{s-1}e^{-r}dr\) for \(s>0\) implies, by substitution,

\[|x'|^{-\alpha}=\frac1{\Gamma(\alpha/2)}\int_0^\infty r^{\alpha/2-1}e^{-r|x'|^2}\,dr.\tag{6.8}\]

First restrict the integral to \([1/m,m]\). The resulting function \(f_m\) is Schwartz and increases pointwise off zero to the power. Its Fourier transform can be taken inside the truncated integral by absolute integrability. The unitary transform of \(e^{-r|x'|^2}\) is \((2r)^{-d/2}e^{-|\zeta|^2/(4r)}\). Thus at nonzero frequencies the limit of the transforms is

\[\begin{aligned}\frac{2^{-d/2}}{\Gamma(\alpha/2)}\int_0^\infty r^{(\alpha-d)/2-1}e^{-|\zeta|^2/(4r)}dr\ &=2^{d/2-\alpha}\frac{\Gamma((d-\alpha)/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha/2)}|\zeta|^{\alpha-d}.\end{aligned}\]

The last step substitutes \(s=|\zeta|^2/(4r)\). On the physical side the majorant \(|x'|^{-\alpha}|\psi(x')|\) is integrable for every Schwartz test \(\psi\); on the frequency side the majorant \(a(d,\alpha)|\zeta|^{\alpha-d}|\psi(\zeta)|\) is integrable because the radial exponent near zero is \(\alpha-1>-1\). Dominated convergence on both sides and the dual definition of the transform establish (6.7) as a distributional identity. □

In the narrower range \(0<\alpha<d-1\), multiplication by \(|\zeta|\) gives a density proportional to the transform of \(|x'|^{-\alpha-1}\). Consequently the coefficient is a quotient of the two gamma factors:

\[\begin{aligned}(-\Delta_{x'})^{1/2}|x'|^{-\alpha}&=C_N(\alpha)|x'|^{-\alpha-1},\\ C_N(\alpha)&=\frac{a(d,\alpha)}{a(d,\alpha+1)}\\&=2\frac{\Gamma((N-1-\alpha)/2)\Gamma((\alpha+1)/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha/2)\Gamma((N-2-\alpha)/2)}.\end{aligned}\tag{6.9}\]

The multiplier here is applied to an explicitly locally integrable Fourier density, so no multiplication of an arbitrary distribution by a nonsmooth function is assumed. At \(\alpha=d-1\), the limiting multiplier density is constant, whose inverse transform is a multiple of the Dirac mass at zero, not the displayed power. This explains the open exponent interval.

6.5 The positive harmonic profiles

Lemma 6.4 · Harmonic profile and boundary identity

For \(0<\alpha<N-2\), define

\[\Phi_\alpha(x',z)=\int_{\mathbb R^d}\mathcal P_z(x'-w)|w|^{-\alpha}\,dw.\tag{6.10}\]

It is positive, harmonic in \(\mathcal H\), smooth up to the plane away from zero, and

\[\begin{aligned}\Phi_\alpha(x',0)&=|x'|^{-\alpha},\quad x'\ne0,\\\partial_\nu\Phi_\alpha(x',0)&=\frac{C_N(\alpha)}{|x'|}\Phi_\alpha(x',0),\\\Phi_\alpha(\rho x)&=\rho^{-\alpha}\Phi_\alpha(x),\qquad \rho>0.\end{aligned}\tag{6.11}\]

There exist constants \(0<c\le C<\infty\) such that \(c|x|^{-\alpha}\le\Phi_\alpha(x)\le C|x|^{-\alpha}\) on \(\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{0\}\).

Proof

Near \(w=0\), the integrand is integrable since \(\alpha<d\); at infinity the Poisson tail is \(O(|w|^{-d-1})\). Positivity is immediate; differentiation under the integral at positive heights gives harmonicity. Substitution \(w=\rho v\) in (6.10) gives homogeneity. For the normal derivative, use the truncated Gaussian mixtures from Lemma 6.3. At each positive height their convolutions increase to \(\Phi_\alpha\); they are uniformly bounded at that height (split \(|w|\le1\) and its complement), so convergence holds against Schwartz tests. Their transforms converge by the same frequency majorant, now multiplied by \(e^{-z|\zeta|}\). Thus the Fourier density of the extension is \(a(d,\alpha)e^{-z|\zeta|}|\zeta|^{\alpha-d}\). At positive height its inverse transform and differentiated inverse transform are absolutely convergent. Taking \(z\downarrow0\), dominated convergence against Schwartz tests gives the distributional limit of the negative height derivative as (6.9).

Justification of the classical boundary derivative

Fix \(x'_0\ne0\). Split the boundary data into a smooth compactly supported part equal to the power on a neighborhood of \(x'_0\), and a remainder supported a positive distance away.
