The first part is Schwartz, so its extension and all derivatives converge locally up to the plane by the Fourier multiplier representation. For the separated part, on a smaller neighborhood the Poisson kernel and its derivatives remain bounded near the integration singularity and have integrable tails against the power, uniformly for heights between zero and one. Dominated convergence extends the derivatives to the plane; the separated term has zero boundary value. The continuous local limit of the normal derivative must coincide with the distributional limit there. This proves the pointwise trace and derivative formulas away from zero. Homogeneity and the positive continuous profile on the compact closed unit hemisphere give the two-sided bounds (and similarly a bound on its gradient by \(C|x|^{-\alpha-1}\)).

□

Why choose the midpoint \(\alpha_*=(N-2)/2\)? Let \(r=|x|\), \(\vartheta=x/r\), and write \(\Phi_\alpha(x)=r^{-\alpha}p_\alpha(\vartheta)\). For a cutoff \(\chi(s)\) with \(s=\log r\), the radial derivative is \(\chi'(s)/r\). In polar coordinates the weighted gradient cost factors as

\[\begin{aligned}\int_{\mathcal H}\Phi_\alpha^2|\nabla\chi(\log r)|^2dx &= A_\alpha\int_0^\infty r^{N-3-2\alpha}|\chi'(\log r)|^2dr\\&=A_\alpha\int_{\mathbb R}e^{(N-2-2\alpha)s}|\chi'(s)|^2ds,\\ A_\alpha&=\int_{S^{N-1}\cap\mathcal H}p_\alpha(\vartheta)^2d\vartheta.\end{aligned}\]

Here the angular integral is finite and positive by Lemma 6.4. At \(\alpha=\alpha_*\), the radial measure becomes \(dr/r=ds\); translating a fixed cutoff transition along the logarithmic radius does not change its cost. On the boundary the squared profile divided by distance likewise gives the radial measure \(dr/r\). Thus the critical exponent makes both terms scale invariant. This is the mechanism behind the minimizing sequence in §6.7.

Substitution in (6.9) yields

\[C_N(\alpha_*)=2\frac{\Gamma(N/4)^2}{\Gamma((N-2)/4)^2}=\lambda_N.\tag{6.12}\]
Why the characteristic coefficient has exactly one maximum

Let \(s=\alpha/2\) and \(b=(N-2-\alpha)/2\); their sum is fixed. Put \(R(s)=\Gamma(s+1/2)/\Gamma(s)\). Then \(C_N(\alpha)=2R(s)R(b)\), which is symmetric about \(\alpha_*\). Since \(R(s)=\Gamma(1/2)/B(s,1/2)\), and

\[ B(s,1/2)=\int_0^1 t^{s-1}(1-t)^{-1/2}dt,\]

Hölder's inequality for this integral shows that \(\log B(s,1/2)\) is strictly convex (equality would require two distinct powers of \(t\) to be proportional almost everywhere). Therefore \(\log R\) is strictly concave. The symmetric strictly concave function \(\log C_N\) strictly increases before the midpoint and strictly decreases after it. The endpoint limits of \(C_N\) are zero by \(s\Gamma(s)=\Gamma(s+1)\) as \(s\downarrow0\). It follows that \(C_N(\alpha)=\lambda\) has two roots if \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\), one at equality, and none in this interval above the maximum. This justifies the roots used in §3.4; Chapter 9 uses the smaller root for weighted solvability.

Example 6.1 · An exact profile in dimension four

For \(N=4\), \(\alpha_*=1\) and \(\lambda_4=2/\pi\). With \(r=|x'|\) and \(z=x_4>0\),

\[\Phi_1(r,z)=\frac{2}{\pi r}\arctan\frac r z,\qquad \Phi_1(0,z)=\frac{2}{\pi z}.\tag{6.13}\]

For \(r>0\), the radial Laplacian in three tangential dimensions is \(\partial_{rr}+2r^{-1}\partial_r\). Writing \(F=r\Phi_1\) gives \(\Delta\Phi_1=(F_{rr}+F_{zz})/r=0\), since \(\arctan(r/z)\) is harmonic in the positive quadrant. The even expansion at \(r=0\) extends harmonicity there. At the boundary \(\Phi_1(r,0)=1/r\) and \(-\partial_z\Phi_1(r,0)=2/(\pi r^2)\), as required.

Derive the explicit Poisson integral

By radial convolution in three dimensions, the spherical average of \(|x'-\rho\theta|^{-1}\) is

\[\frac12\int_{-1}^1\frac{ds}{\sqrt{r^2+\rho^2-2r\rho s}}=\frac1{\max(r,\rho)}.\]

Integrating the Poisson kernel against this average reduces the convolution to

\[\frac{4z}{\pi}\left[\frac1r\int_0^r\frac{\rho^2}{(\rho^2+z^2)^2}d\rho+\int_r^\infty\frac{\rho}{(\rho^2+z^2)^2}d\rho\right]=\frac{2}{\pi r}\arctan(r/z).\]

Use the primitives \(\arctan(\rho/z)/(2z)-\rho/[2(\rho^2+z^2)]\) and \(-1/[2(\rho^2+z^2)]\) respectively. The rational terms cancel.

6.6 Ground-state representation

Set \(\Phi_*=\Phi_{\alpha_*}\). The normal derivative of this positive harmonic function is exactly \(\lambda_N\Phi_*/|x'|\). Factoring out \(\Phi_*\) will turn the difference between bulk dissipation and boundary amplification into a nonnegative integral.

Proposition 6.1 · Ground-state identity

If \(w\in C_c^\infty(\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{0\})\) (compact support avoiding the origin), then

\[\mathcal E[w]-\lambda_N B_0[w]=\int_{\mathcal H}\Phi_*^2\left|\nabla\left(\frac{w}{\Phi_*}\right)\right|^2dx\ge0.\tag{6.14}\]

Proof

For real-valued \(w\), put \(v=w/\Phi_*\); the complex case follows by applying the result to real and imaginary parts. Expand the derivative:

\[|\nabla(\Phi_*v)|^2=\Phi_*^2|\nabla v|^2+\nabla\Phi_*\cdot\nabla(\Phi_*v^2).\]

Integrate the cross term by parts, using the compact support away from zero and \(\Delta\Phi_*=0\). Only the planar boundary term remains:

\[\int_{\partial\mathcal H}(\partial_\nu\Phi_*)\Phi_*v^2d\sigma=\lambda_N\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|w|^2}{|x'|}d\sigma.\]

Rearrange to obtain (6.14). The right side is nonnegative. □

6.7 Logarithmic annuli and sharpness

Proving an inequality gives a lower bound on the quotient. Proving sharpness additionally requires a sequence whose quotient approaches the bound. The logarithmic annuli below provide that sequence.

First extend the lower bound to smooth functions whose support includes zero. Use a radial cutoff \(\chi_\delta\) equal to zero for \(|x|\le\delta\), equal to one for \(|x|\ge2\delta\), with \(|\nabla\chi_\delta|\le C/\delta\). For fixed smooth compactly supported \(w\), the squared gradient error is \(O(\delta^{N-2})\) and the weighted trace integral error is \(O(\delta^{N-2})\), by integrating over the ball and boundary disk. They vanish since \(N\ge3\). Apply (6.14) to \(\chi_\delta w\) and let \(\delta\downarrow0\).

Optimality of the coefficient

For \(R>2\) choose a smooth radial cutoff \(\chi_R\) equal to one for \(R^{-1}\le |x|\le R\), zero for \(|x|\le(2R)^{-1}\) and \(|x|\ge2R\), with \(|\nabla\chi_R|\le C/|x|\). Such a function is obtained by composing two fixed smooth transitions with rescaled radii. Set \(w_R=\Phi_*\chi_R\). This is a smooth compactly supported test on the closed half-space, avoiding zero.

By homogeneity, \(\Phi_*^2\le C|x|^{-(N-2)}\). The right side of (6.14) is supported on two annuli of ratio two. Each contributes at most

\[C\int_a^{2a}r^{N-1}r^{-(N-2)}r^{-2}dr=C\log2.\]

On the boundary, the critical trace is exactly \(|x'|^{-(N-2)/2}\). Hence

\[B_0[w_R]\ge |S^{d-1}|\int_{1/R}^R\frac{dr}{r}=2|S^{d-1}|\log R.\]

Dividing the identity by this denominator yields

\[\frac{\mathcal E[w_R]}{B_0[w_R]}=\lambda_N+O\left(\frac1{\log R}\right)\longrightarrow\lambda_N.\tag{6.15}\]

Thus no larger constant can hold for all admissible competitors. The formal profile \(\Phi_*\) itself has divergent weighted boundary integral at both radial endpoints; the proof constructs a minimizing sequence, not an admissible extremizer. □

6.8 Extension to Sobolev functions and consequences

For \(w\in H^1(\mathcal H)\), smooth compactly supported restrictions are dense. Indeed even reflection across the plane gives an \(H^1(\mathbb R^N)\) function (the weak derivatives are the even tangential and odd normal reflections); spatial cutoff followed by convolution with a smooth approximate identity approximates it in \(H^1\), and restriction preserves convergence. By the trace estimate in Lemma B.1, the traces converge in boundary \(L^2\). A subsequence converges almost everywhere; Fatou's lemma with the nonnegative weight yields the sharp inequality for \(w\). Translating tangentially preserves both energy and trace measure, giving the result for all centers.

Theorem 6.1 · Sharp Kato inequality (proof of Theorem 4.1)
\[\lambda_N\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|\operatorname{Tr}w(x)|^2}{|x-a|}\,d\sigma(x)\le\mathcal E[w],\quad w\in H^1(\mathcal H),\ a\in\partial\mathcal H.\tag{6.17}\]

For \(N\ge3\), the constant is optimal. The minimizing sequence above consists of admissible \(H^1\) functions; translating it proves optimality at every boundary center.

Proposition 6.2 · Fractional Hardy inequality (supplementary)

For \(d=N-1\ge2\) and \(f\in\mathcal S(\mathbb R^d)\),

\[\lambda_N\int_{\mathbb R^d}\frac{|f(x')|^2}{|x'|}\,dx'\le \int_{\mathbb R^d}|\zeta|\,|\widehat f(\zeta)|^2d\zeta.\tag{6.18}\]

The coefficient is optimal on this class.

Proof

Let \(v=\mathcal P_{x_N}*f\). By tangential Plancherel and Tonelli,

\[\|v\|_{L^2(\mathcal H)}^2=\int_{\mathbb R^d}\frac{|\widehat f(\zeta)|^2}{2|\zeta|}\,d\zeta.\]

This is finite: near zero the radial weight is proportional to \(r^{d-2}\), which is integrable for \(d\ge2\), and at infinity the Fourier transform decays rapidly. The gradient energy is finite by Lemma 6.2 (its calculation applies to all Schwartz data), so \(v\in H^1(\mathcal H)\) and its trace is \(f\). Apply Theorem 6.1 and use (6.6).

For optimality take the smooth logarithmic-annulus competitors \(w_R\) of §6.7 and put \(f_R=\operatorname{Tr}w_R\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^d)\subset\mathcal S\). The minimum-energy identity gives

\[\lambda_N\le\frac{\int |\zeta||\widehat f_R|^2d\zeta}{B_0[w_R]}\le\frac{\mathcal E[w_R]}{B_0[w_R]}\longrightarrow\lambda_N.\]

Thus the sharp bulk trace inequality and the fractional boundary inequality have the same constant. □

Proposition 6.3 · Nonattainment (supplementary)

Equality in Theorem 6.1 holds for \(w\in H^1(\mathcal H)\) only when \(w=0\). In particular, the sharp quotient is an infimum, not a minimum among nonzero functions with positive boundary mass.

Proof

By translation take \(a=0\). Approximate \(w\) in \(H^1(\mathcal H)\) by smooth compactly supported restrictions \(v_j\). For each fixed \(j\), the near-origin cutoff estimate from §6.7 lets us choose a cutoff radius so that the resulting \(w_j\) avoids the origin and \(\|w_j-v_j\|_{H^1}\le1/j\). Thus \(w_j\to w\) in \(H^1\). Applying Theorem 6.1 to the differences gives

\[\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|\operatorname{Tr}(w_j-w)|^2}{|x'|}d\sigma\le\lambda_N^{-1}\mathcal E[w_j-w]\to0.\]

Hence both bulk energy and weighted boundary mass converge, so \(Q_{\lambda_N,0}[w_j]\to Q_{\lambda_N,0}[w]\). For any compact subdomain \(K\Subset\mathcal H\), the positive smooth profile and its reciprocal have bounded first derivatives on a neighborhood of \(K\). Multiplication by \(1/\Phi_*\) therefore gives local strong \(H^1\) convergence. By (6.14),

\[\int_K\Phi_*^2|\nabla(w/\Phi_*)|^2dx=\lim_j\int_K\Phi_*^2|\nabla(w_j/\Phi_*)|^2dx\le Q_{\lambda_N,0}[w].\]

Exhausting the interior by compact subdomains gives the corresponding global inequality by monotone convergence. If equality holds in Kato, the right side is zero. The weak gradient of \(w/\Phi_*\) then vanishes locally throughout the connected half-space, so the ratio is a constant almost everywhere (by the local Poincaré inequality on overlapping balls). Thus \(w=c\Phi_*\). For \(c\ne0\) the trace on each boundary annulus equals \(c|x'|^{-\alpha_*}\), by local smoothness and uniqueness of traces. Its weighted mass is \(|c|^2|S^{d-1}|\int_0^\infty dr/r=\infty\), contradicting Theorem 6.1. Hence \(c=0\). □

This result completes the justification of the energy estimate in Chapter 4. For strictly subcritical coupling it gives a positive gradient margin. At equality the form is nonnegative but the sharpness sequence rules out any uniform positive gradient margin. In the supercritical case the same sequence supplies a negative-form test. Chapter 7 will construct the minimal solution at and below criticality, while Chapter 8 will exploit the negative form. The harmonic profile is a tool for those proofs, not a claim that the time-dependent solution is equal to that profile.

The local-to-global inequality in the proof of Proposition 6.3 holds for every translated critical profile. It is used in Theorem 7.5 to pass critical weighted-gradient control to the singular solution.

Chapter 6 · Takeaways

Harmonic extension translates normal differentiation into a tangential Fourier multiplier. Gaussian mixtures compute the characteristic gamma ratio. At critical homogeneity the positive profile absorbs the singular boundary term, leaving a nonnegative weighted gradient. Logarithmic radial scales prove optimality.

Check your understanding

Why is the midpoint exponent special? Why can the formal ground state not be used directly as a compact smooth competitor? Why does an inequality at the critical value fail to supply uniform gradient control?

The exponent makes the cutoff cost proportional to an integral of dr/r. The profile is singular and its weighted trace mass diverges. The minimizing sequence makes the ratio of excess energy to bulk energy tend to zero.

Sources
Source correspondence: revised thesis [5], Chapter 3 and Appendices A.3, B.1–B.4; research paper [4], §2, §5.1 and Appendix A. The beta-integral concavity argument, exact dimension-four logarithmic cutoff illustration, fractional Hardy proposition and nonattainment proposition are supplementary derivations. See Appendix C for Fourier conventions.

With the sharp bound proved, Chapter 7 can construct solutions even when the ordinary gradient margin vanishes.

Part III

7. Proof of global existence

How can bounded approximations yield a finite continuous solution at critical coupling?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Sharp Kato inequality; Appendix B.5–B.8 for weak evolution, Sobolev gain and nonlinear testing; A.5 for compactness.

Bounded problems → uniform L² bound → local L∞ estimate → continuity off the graph → monotone limit and initial trace → minimality.

We now prove Theorem 3.1 (Theorem 1.1 of the research paper). Fix \(N\ge3\), \(T>0\), \(\xi\in C([0,T];\partial\mathcal H)\), \(0<\lambda\le\lambda_N\), and \(f:=u_0\in C_c(\overline{\mathcal H})\), \(f\ge0\), \(f\not\equiv0\). Here \(f\) is the original initial function, not a boundary profile. All estimates indexed by \(n\) below use the same datum.

The critical obstacle is a loss of coercivity, not a failure of the boundary inequality. We can still control the volume \(L^2\) norm at equality. The challenge is to turn that integral control into a finite continuous function at every nonsingular space-time point.

Proof roadmap
1 · ConstructBounded positive Volterra series2 · IdentifyWeak evolution and its energy3 · ControlUniform L²; global gradients only below threshold4 · LocalizeRobin Moser estimate off the graph5 · Recover continuityCutoffs and heat kernel estimates6 · Take the limitFinite identity, initial trace and minimality

The final three steps are needed even when a global L² limit is available. No step differentiates the path.

7.1 Bounded Robin approximations and positive iteration

Let \(b\) be a bounded, continuous, nonnegative function on \(\partial\mathcal H\times[0,T]\). Use the real Banach space \(\mathscr B_T=C_b(\overline{\mathcal H}\times[0,T])\), with norm \(\|v\|_{\mathscr B_T}=\sup_{(x,t)}|v(x,t)|\). Completeness follows because a uniformly Cauchy sequence converges uniformly to a bounded continuous function. Define the time-causal operator on this space

\[(A_bv)(x,t)=\int_0^t\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,t-s)b(y,s)v(y,s)\,d\sigma(y)\,ds.\tag{7.1}\]

At time zero define \(A_bv=0\). All operator norms in (7.3) refer to \(\mathscr B_T\); the time-slice norm in (7.2) is the spatial supremum over \(\overline{\mathcal H}\). From Lemma 2.2, with \(M_b=\|b\|_\infty\),

\[\|A_bv(t)\|_\infty\le \frac{M_b}{\sqrt\pi}\int_0^t(t-s)^{-1/2}\|v(s)\|_\infty\,ds.\tag{7.2}\]
Lemma 7.1 · Volterra series

In \(\mathscr B_T\), the unique solution of \(u_b=F+A_bu_b\), \(F(x,t)=P_tf(x)\), is

\[u_b=\sum_{k=0}^\infty A_b^kF,\qquad \|A_b^k\|\le\frac{M_b^kT^{k/2}}{\Gamma(1+k/2)}.\tag{7.3}\]

It is nonnegative, and increases when either the nonnegative datum or the coefficient increases.

Proof

1. Iteration of the time estimate. The stronger bound at time \(t\) replaces \(T\) by \(t\) in (7.3). Suppose it holds for \(k\). Applying (7.2) and substituting \(s=t\theta\) yields the integral \(t^{(k+1)/2}B(1/2,1+k/2)\). The beta identity in Appendix C.4 cancels \(\sqrt\pi\,\Gamma(1+k/2)\), giving the next bound. The case \(k=0\) is the identity operator.

2. Convergence and uniqueness. If \(a_k=M_b^kT^{k/2}/\Gamma(1+k/2)\), then \(a_{k+2}/a_k=M_b^2T/(1+k/2)\to0\) when \(M_b>0\); for \(M_b=0\) all terms after the first vanish. Both parity subseries converge. Thus the sum converges uniformly, and applying the bounded operator gives the equation. For a bounded difference \(d=A_bd\), iterating gives \(\|d\|_\infty\le a_k\|d\|_\infty\), hence \(d=0\).

3. Continuity and order. The portion of (7.1) with \(t-s<\delta\) is bounded by \(2M_b\|v\|_\infty\sqrt{\delta/\pi}\). On the complementary time range, for observations in a compact set, Gaussian decay in the boundary variable supplies an integrable majorant; moving the upper integration endpoint does not obstruct dominated convergence. The same short-time estimate handles zero time. This proves that \(A_b\) maps \(\mathscr B_T\) into itself; (7.2) also gives \(\|A_b\|\le2M_b\sqrt{T/\pi}\). The free term belongs to the same space by continuity of the even extension of the compact datum and the approximate identity. Every iterate and the uniform sum therefore belong to \(\mathscr B_T\). Every term is nonnegative for nonnegative \(f\); comparing term by term proves the order property. □

Why the Gamma factor matters. A contraction of \(A_b\) itself is not required. Causality produces a factorial-like denominator in its high powers, so bounded problems can be solved on any finite interval.

Now regularize the actual singular coefficient:

\[b_n(y,t)=\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{|y-\xi(t)|^2+n^{-2}}},\qquad u_n=F+A_{b_n}u_n,\qquad n=1,2,\ldots .\tag{7.4}\]

Each coefficient is continuous and bounded by \(\lambda n\); for \(y\ne\xi(t)\) it increases to \(V(y,t)=\lambda/|y-\xi(t)|\). Lemma 7.1 therefore gives \(0\le u_n\le u_{n+1}\) on the closed cylinder. This is pointwise order, not yet an existence proof for the singular equation.

Uniform convergence away from the pole

Write \(r=|y-\xi(t)|\). For \(r\ge d_0>0\), rationalizing the difference gives

\[0\le V-b_n=\frac{\lambda n^{-2}}{r\sqrt{r^2+n^{-2}}\,(r+\sqrt{r^2+n^{-2}})}\le\frac{\lambda}{2n^2d_0^3}.\]

This bound is uniform in both space and time on separated sets. It does not assert uniform convergence on the whole punctured boundary.

7.2 Weak evolution and the energy identity

The integral construction alone does not justify testing the equation with its solution. We identify it with a weak energy evolution. Use \(\mathscr X=H^1(\mathcal H)\) and the Hilbert triple \(\mathscr X\hookrightarrow L^2(\mathcal H)\hookrightarrow\mathscr X^*\). These are dense continuous embeddings; the second sends an \(L^2\) function to its pairing with \(\mathscr X\). See Appendix B.5 and B.6.

\[a_t(v,w)=\int_{\mathcal H}\nabla v\cdot\nabla w\,dx-\int_{\partial\mathcal H}b(y,t)\operatorname{Tr}v\operatorname{Tr}w\,d\sigma.\tag{7.5}\]

The negative sign is the outward-normal convention for positive Robin amplification. By the elementary trace inequality, for \(\delta>0\),

\[a_t(w,w)\ge(1-M_b\delta)\|\nabla w\|_2^2-M_b\delta^{-1}\|w\|_2^2.\]

Choose \(\delta=1/[2(1+M_b)]\). Adding \(c_0\|w\|_2^2\), with \(c_0=M_b\delta^{-1}+1/2\), gives at least \(\tfrac12\|w\|_{H^1}^2\). The ordinary trace bound and Cauchy–Schwarz also bound \(|a_t(v,w)|\) by \(C\|v\|_{H^1}\|w\|_{H^1}\); measurability follows from that of \(b\). Thus the hypotheses of the nonautonomous form theorem are met. It gives a unique weak evolution \(v\) with

\[\begin{gathered}v\in L^2(0,T;\mathscr X)\cap C([0,T];L^2(\mathcal H)),\qquad v_t\in L^2(0,T;\mathscr X^*),\\ v(0)=f,\qquad \langle v_t,w\rangle_{\mathscr X^*,\mathscr X}+a_t(v,w)=0\quad(w\in\mathscr X)\quad\text{a.e. }t.\end{gathered}\tag{7.6}\]
Two constructions, one solution
Positive seriesContinuous bounded values and comparisonWeak evolutionSobolev regularity and legal energy testingVolume identityBoundary flux → bulk via a time-integrable normTrace identityRecover boundary values in space-time L²

The weak solution's volume representative alone does not identify its flux. We first recover the boundary Volterra equation, then compare its convergent series with the continuous construction.

Two kernel operators
\[\begin{aligned}S_h &:L^2(\partial\mathcal H)\longrightarrow L^2(\mathcal H),&\quad\|S_h\|&\le Ch^{-1/4},\\ R_h &:L^2(\partial\mathcal H)\longrightarrow L^2(\partial\mathcal H),&\quad\|R_h\|&\le(\pi h)^{-1/2}.\end{aligned}\]

Here \(R_h\psi=(S_h\psi)(\cdot,0)\) is defined by the explicit kernel formula (7.7), not by assuming Sobolev regularity. The first estimate allows Cauchy–Schwarz in time at a fixed observation time; the second uses an integrable time kernel in the boundary space-time norm. The proof below identifies the latter with the Sobolev trace of the weak evolution.

Proof of the identification: weak evolution = bounded integral solution

Step 1 · Boundary-to-volume estimates

Write \(S_h\psi(x)=\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,h)\psi(y)\,d\sigma(y)\). For boundary sources, the reflection formula factors as

\[S_h\psi(x',z)=\frac{e^{-z^2/(4h)}}{\sqrt{\pi h}}\,(g_h^{(N-1)}*\psi)(x'),\qquad z\ge0.\tag{7.7}\]

The superscript denotes dimension of the Gaussian. Tangential convolution is an \(L^2\) contraction. Taking the normal squared integral gives \(\|S_h\|_{L^2(\partial\mathcal H)\to L^2(\mathcal H)}\le C h^{-1/4}\), since \(\int_0^\infty e^{-z^2/(2h)}dz=C\sqrt h\). At height zero, the explicit operator \(R_h\) is tangential convolution multiplied by \((\pi h)^{-1/2}\); its operator norm is at most \((\pi h)^{-1/2}\). By symmetry and duality, \(\|\operatorname{Tr}P_hf\|_{L^2(\partial\mathcal H)}\le Ch^{-1/4}\|f\|_2\). This free trace is square integrable in time.

Step 2 · Backward testing

For any \(g\in C_c^\infty(\mathcal H)\) and fixed \(t>0\), use \(P_{t-s}g\) as a time-dependent test in (7.6). The even extension of \(g\) is smooth, so this test satisfies the homogeneous Neumann heat equation backward in time and has the requisite energy regularity through \(s=t\). The dual pairing rule cancels the two bulk gradient terms. Integration from zero to \(t\) gives

\[(v(t),g)_2=(P_tf,g)_2+\int_0^t\big(b(\cdot,s)\operatorname{Tr}v(s),\operatorname{Tr}P_{t-s}g\big)_{L^2(\partial\mathcal H)}ds.\]

Because \(\psi=b\operatorname{Tr}v\in L^2(0,T;L^2(\partial\mathcal H))\) and \(h^{-1/4}\in L^2(0,T)\), the integral \(\int_0^t S_{t-s}\psi(s)ds\) exists in \(L^2(\mathcal H)\) for every \(t\). Density of interior tests identifies

\[v(t)=P_tf+\int_0^t S_{t-s}\psi(s)ds\quad\text{in }L^2(\mathcal H).\tag{7.8}\]

Step 3 · Recover the trace, not just the volume representative

Set \(Z=L^2(0,T;L^2(\partial\mathcal H))\). At height \(z\), (7.7) introduces a factor \(e^{-z^2/(4h)}\) in the time convolution. As \(z\downarrow0\), the difference from the boundary operator has time-kernel majorant \((\pi h)^{-1/2}|1-e^{-z^2/(4h)}|\), whose \(L^1(0,T)\) norm tends to zero by dominated convergence. Young's convolution inequality in time gives convergence in \(Z\). The free term converges in the same space: normal Gaussian integration gives a height-uniform bound \(Ch^{-1/4}\|f\|_2\), and at each positive time its slices converge in tangential \(L^2\) by Gaussian continuity. For almost every height (7.8) agrees with the Sobolev slice by Fubini. Moreover \(\|v(\cdot,z,\cdot)-\operatorname{Tr}v\|_Z\le\sqrt z\|\partial_Nv\|_{L^2(\mathcal H\times(0,T))}\) along such slices, by the fundamental theorem on normal lines. Therefore, writing \(\tau=\operatorname{Tr}v\), the equality in \(Z\) is

\[\tau(t)= (P_tf)(\cdot,0)+\int_0^t R_{t-s}[b(\cdot,s)\tau(s)]\,ds.\]

The equality holds for almost every time as an equality of boundary \(L^2\) functions. The boundary value of the free term is given by its Gaussian kernel at positive times.

Step 4 · Identify both constructions

Let the boundary operator on \(Z\) be the right-hand integral of (7.1) restricted to \(x\in\partial\mathcal H\). Its \(k\)-fold norm is bounded by the same Gamma expression as (7.3): iterating the positive time majorant gives a convolution kernel \(M_b^kh^{k/2-1}/\Gamma(k/2)\) for \(k\ge1\), whose \(L^1(0,T)\) norm is the bound. Hence the trace equation has a unique solution in \(Z\), obtained by its series with free trace \(\operatorname{Tr}P_\cdot f\). The same partial sums converge uniformly to the continuous boundary values of \(u_b\). On each compact boundary-time patch both limits agree almost everywhere (convergence in measure is unique). Substituting the identified flux into (7.8) shows that \(u_b(t)=v(t)\) as volume functions for every time, and that their traces agree almost everywhere in boundary space-time. Thus the bounded continuous representative inherits the weak energy regularity. □

The Hilbert-triple chain rule from Appendix B.6 justifies testing with \(v(t)\): the derivative of \(\tfrac12\|v(t)\|_2^2\) is \(\langle v_t,v\rangle\) almost everywhere. Integrating and using the identification gives, for every \(0\le t\le T\),

\[\frac12\|u_b(t)\|_2^2+\int_0^t\|\nabla u_b(s)\|_2^2ds=\frac12\|f\|_2^2+\int_0^t\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H} b\,|\operatorname{Tr}u_b|^2d\sigma ds.\tag{7.9}\]

7.3 Uniform energy control at and below criticality

Apply (7.9) with \(b=b_n\). At each time the translated sharp Kato inequality controls the singular weight, and \(b_n\le V\) almost everywhere on the boundary. This yields the estimate

Proposition 7.2 · Uniform approximation estimate
\[\boxed{\|u_n(t)\|_2^2+2\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda_N}\right)\int_0^t\|\nabla u_n(s)\|_2^2ds\le\|f\|_2^2.}\tag{7.10}\]

It holds for every \(n\) and every \(t\in[0,T]\); no derivative of the moving center enters the inequality.

Subcritical. The positive coefficient of the gradient gives a uniform bound in \(L^2(0,T;H^1(\mathcal H))\). Critical. That coefficient is zero, so estimate (7.10) supplies a uniform volume norm; it does not imply that the limit belongs to the global energy space.

Retained control at criticality

At \(\lambda=\lambda_N\), the exact bounded energy identity and \(b_n\le\lambda_N/|y-\xi(s)|\) give

\[\boxed{\|u_n(t)\|_2^2+2\int_0^t Q_{\lambda_N,\xi(s)}[u_n(s)]\,ds\le\|f\|_2^2.}\]

Indeed the regularized form equals the singular critical form plus \(\int_{\partial\mathcal H}(V-b_n)|\operatorname{Tr}u_n|^2\,d\sigma\), a nonnegative correction. By Chapter 6 the critical form is itself nonnegative. This controls its time integral uniformly in \(n\), but does not control the two large terms \(\mathcal E[u_n]\) and \(\lambda_N B_{\xi(s)}[u_n]\) separately. We do not claim that the limiting solution is in global \(H^1\) or pass this form to the limit without further analysis.

Set \(u=\sup_nu_n\) initially with extended values. At each fixed \(t\), monotone convergence applied to \(u_n^2\) shows that \(\|u(t)\|_2\le\|f\|_2\) and \(u(t)\) is finite almost everywhere in the volume. Because \(0\le u-u_n\le u\) almost everywhere, dominated convergence gives strong \(L^2\) convergence at each time, and, by the common norm bound, also in \(L^2(0,T;L^2(\mathcal H))\). For strict coupling, a weakly convergent subsequence in the global energy space has this same limit. Restricting weak convergence to \((0,t)\) and applying lower semicontinuity proves the gradient estimate for the limit at every \(t\).

Example 7.1 · Why the endpoint is genuinely different

Let \(0<\rho<1/4\) and choose a radial cutoff \(\chi_\rho\) equal to one on \(2\rho\le|x|\le1\), zero for \(|x|\le\rho\) or \(|x|\ge2\), with \(|\nabla\chi_\rho|\le C/|x|\). Put \(w_\rho=\Phi_*\chi_\rho\). Homogeneity gives \(\|w_\rho\|_2^2\le C\int_0^2r\,dr\), while \(B_0[w_\rho]\ge |\mathbb S^{N-2}|\log(1/(2\rho))\). The critical ground-state identity bounds \(Q_{\lambda_N,0}[w_\rho]\) by a constant because each cutoff transition has a fixed logarithmic width. Therefore \(\mathcal E[w_\rho]=Q_{\lambda_N,0}[w_\rho]+\lambda_N B_0[w_\rho]\to\infty\). Even bounded critical form energy and volume norm cannot control the full gradient. These test functions are not actual heat solutions.

Why we need a local argument. The preceding convergence concerns volume equivalence classes. It does not recover a boundary value, pointwise finiteness or continuity. The next two sections supply precisely those missing properties.

7.4 Local boundedness by boundary Moser iteration

A uniform L² estimate controls an integral, not the maximum at any individual point. Even smooth functions can form high, narrow peaks with bounded L² norm. We therefore need a local estimate in a cylinder separated from the pole.

We work on a backward parabolic cylinder \(Q_R=(B_R(x_0)\cap\mathcal H)\times(t_0-R^2,t_0]\), with \(x_0\in\overline{\mathcal H}\). A cylinder can meet the flat boundary; its test functions need not vanish there. The key is that on a neighborhood separated from the moving pole, the coefficients \(b_n\) admit a bound independent of \(n\).

Lemma 7.3 · Local boundedness (paper Lemma 3.1)

Suppose \(w\ge0\) is bounded and belongs locally to \(L^2\) in time with spatial \(H^1\) regularity up to the flat boundary, on smaller spatial balls and compact time intervals strictly inside \((t_0-R^2,t_0)\). Assume for all nonnegative smooth tests \(\varphi\) supported inside the ball and the open time interval (restricted to the closed half-space),

\[\iint_{Q_R}(-w\varphi_t+\nabla w\cdot\nabla\varphi)\,dx\,dt\le B\int_{t_0-R^2}^{t_0}\!\int_{B_R(x_0)\cap\partial\mathcal H}w\varphi\,d\sigma\,dt,\qquad B\ge0.\tag{7.11}\]

For \(0<r<R\),

\[\|w\|_{L^\infty(Q_r)}\le C(N,B,R,r)\|w\|_{L^2(Q_R)}.\tag{7.12}\]

The constant does not depend on the individual bound of \(w\).

Step 1 · Power testing

Take \(p\ge2\), and a cutoff \(\theta\) equal to one on \(Q_r\), supported spatially strictly inside \(B_R(x_0)\), and zero close to the initial cylinder time. Choose \(|\nabla\theta|\le C/(R-r)\) and \(|\theta_t|\le C/(R-r)^2\); such cutoffs follow from the spatial gap and \(R^2-r^2\ge(R-r)^2\). Write \(v=w^{p/2}\) and use \(\theta^2w^{p-1}\) in (7.11).

Justification of the nonlinear test

See Appendix B.8 for the time-averaging and approximation lemmas. Average the subsolution inequality over a short forward time interval of length \(\ell\), using only times strictly inside the cylinder. The average \(w_\ell\) is nonnegative and bounded by the same individual bound as \(w\), has a local \(L^2\) time derivative, and satisfies the same inequality with boundary upper term \(B\operatorname{Tr}w_\ell\). Sobolev approximation and a cutoff at the upper time allow the test \(\theta^2w_\ell^{p-1}\). For \(F(s)=s^{p-1}\) the function and derivative are bounded on the actual range (for \(p\ge2\)); thus the spatial chain rule is valid. Time averages converge strongly in local \(L^2(H^1)\) and their traces converge in local space-time \(L^2\). Along a subsequence, the difference of gradients of the powers equals \(F'(w_\ell)\nabla(w_\ell-w)+(F'(w_\ell)-F'(w))\nabla w\); the first term tends to zero in \(L^2\) and the second by dominated convergence. The upper-time primitives converge for almost every upper time. Taking the essential supremum and exhausting the open interval justifies the integrated power estimate up to the cylinder top. The resulting constants are independent of the individual bound.

Before multiplication by \(p\), the time contribution is \(p^{-1}\frac{d}{dt}\int\theta^2v^2-2p^{-1}\int\theta\theta_t v^2\). The gradient contribution is

\[\frac{4(p-1)}{p^2}\int\theta^2|\nabla v|^2+\frac4p\int\theta v\nabla v\cdot\nabla\theta.\]

Young's inequality absorbs the mixed term into half the principal term plus a cutoff cost. Multiplying by \(p\), using \(p\ge2\), and estimating \(|\nabla(\theta v)|^2\) by twice each square yields absolute constants \(c,C>0\) such that

\[\frac{d}{dt}\int(\theta v)^2+c\int|\nabla(\theta v)|^2\le C\int(|\nabla\theta|^2+|\theta\theta_t|)v^2+pB\int_{\partial\mathcal H}(\theta v)^2.\tag{7.13}\]

Step 2 · Absorb the Robin contribution

Apply (B.1) to \(\theta v\), extended by zero beyond its spatial support, with \(\delta=c/[2(1+pB)]\). Then \(pB\delta\le c/2\), while \(pB\delta^{-1}\le C(1+p^2B^2)\). Integrate from the lower cylinder time, where the cutoff vanishes, and take the essential supremum of the first term. We obtain

\[\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{s}\|\theta v(s)\|_2^2+\iint_{Q_R}|\nabla(\theta v)|^2\le C\big[1+(R-r)^{-2}+p^2B^2\big]\iint_{Q_R}w^p.\tag{7.14}\]

Step 3 · Gain an integrability exponent

Put \(\kappa=1+2/N\). For \(g=\theta v\), even reflection and the Euclidean Sobolev inequality (see B.7) imply \(\|g\|_{2N/(N-2)}\le C_N\|\nabla g\|_2\). Hölder interpolation at each time gives \(\|g\|_{2\kappa}^{2\kappa}\le\|g\|_2^{4/N}\|g\|_{2N/(N-2)}^2\). Integrating in time and using (7.14), then taking the \(p\kappa\)-th root, yields

\[\|w\|_{L^{p\kappa}(Q_r)}\le\big[C_N(1+(R-r)^{-2}+p^2B^2)\big]^{1/p}\|w\|_{L^p(Q_R)}.\tag{7.15}\]

Step 4 · Iterate to the essential supremum

Use \(p_j=2\kappa^j\), \(R_j=r+(R-r)2^{-j}\). Apply (7.15) from \(Q_{R_j}\) to \(Q_{R_{j+1}}\). Because \(R_j-R_{j+1}=(R-r)2^{-j-1}\), the logarithm of the product of all multiplicative costs is bounded by \(C(N,B,R,r)\sum_{j\ge0}(1+j)/p_j<\infty\). To see the mechanism, let \(q_\kappa=1/\kappa\). The exact summability factor is \(\sum_{j\ge0}(j+1)/p_j=1/[2(1-q_\kappa)^2]\). In a simple model with \(\kappa=2\), factors \(4^{(j+1)/p_j}\) have product \(4^2=16\), even though the unpowered costs diverge. In the actual iteration, \(p_j^2B^2\) and the squared inverse radius gap both grow at most geometrically, so taking logarithms gives a linear-in-\(j\) bound divided by \(p_j\). The exact numerical value of the PDE constant is not asserted. Every norm on \(Q_r\) is bounded by the same product times \(\|w\|_{L^2(Q_R)}\). Since the cylinder has finite measure, the limit of its \(L^{p_j}\) norms is its essential supremum. This proves (7.12). □

7.5 Applying the local estimate, including time zero

On a compact set \(K\subset D_\xi\), continuity of the trajectory implies

\[d_K=\min_{(x,t)\in K}|x-\xi(t)|>0.\]

Choose \(\rho>0\) so that \(2\rho<d_K/4\) and \(|\xi(s)-\xi(t)|<d_K/4\) for \(|s-t|\le4\rho^2\) in \([0,T]\). Uniform continuity on the compact time interval guarantees such a radius. The triangle inequality gives \(|y-\xi(s)|\ge d_K/2\) whenever \(|y-x|\le2\rho\) and the stated time constraint holds. Hence all \(b_n\) are bounded there by \(B=2\lambda/d_K\).

For a positive-time observation choose the backward cylinder of radius \(\rho\) centered at \(x\) with top time \(\min(t+\rho^2/8,T)\). Its cylinder of radius \(\rho/2\) contains the observation in the relative closed space-time region. If this cylinder reaches negative times, extend the subsolution as follows.

Initial-time extension
\[M_0=\|f\|_\infty,\qquad w_n(x,s)=\begin{cases}\max\{u_n(x,s),M_0\},&s\ge0,\\ M_0,&s<0.\end{cases}\tag{7.16}\]

In any such cylinder with positive-time boundary bound \(b_n\le B\), the extended function satisfies (7.11).

Proof of the extension

Approximate \(r\mapsto\max(r,M_0)\) by smooth convex functions \(F_\delta\) lying above \(r\) for \(r\ge0\), with \(0\le F_\delta'\le1\), derivative zero below \(M_0\), and uniform convergence on bounded intervals. For example, integrate a smooth increasing transition from zero to one over \([M_0,M_0+\delta]\), starting with constant \(M_0+\delta\). Use the time-averaged weak equation and the nonnegative test \(F_\delta'(u_{n,\ell})\varphi\). The same strong local convergence of averages and traces as in the nonlinear-test justification permits passage \(\ell\downarrow0\) for fixed \(n,\delta\); the averaged Robin flux converges strongly in local \(L^2\) since \(b_n\) is bounded. The convexity contribution \(-F_\delta''(u_n)|\nabla u_n|^2\le0\) can be discarded. On the plane the flux is at most \(B F_\delta(u_n)\), since \(F_\delta'(u_n) b_nu_n\le B F_\delta(u_n)\). Passing \(\delta\downarrow0\) using bounded convergence and the Sobolev chain rule gives the subsolution for \(\max(u_n,M_0)\). The latter tends to \(M_0\) locally in \(L^2\) as \(s\downarrow0\), because \(u_n(s)\to f\) strongly and \(f\le M_0\). Let \(z_n=\max(u_n,M_0)\) on positive times and let \(\varphi\ge0\) be supported inside the extended cylinder, vanishing near its time endpoints. Integrate the positive-time inequality first with a cutoff which rises from zero to one just after time zero. Taking that cutoff to the initial face using the local strong trace gives

\[\int_{s>0}(-z_n\varphi_s+\nabla z_n\cdot\nabla\varphi)\,dx\,ds\le B\int_{s>0,\partial\mathcal H}z_n\varphi\,d\sigma\,ds+\int_{\mathcal H}M_0\varphi(x,0)\,dx.\]

On the negative-time piece, the gradient is zero and \(\int_{s<0}-M_0\varphi_s\,dx\,ds=-\int M_0\varphi(x,0)dx\). Adding cancels both interface terms exactly. The negative-time Robin upper bound is also valid because its right-hand side is nonnegative. The extended function therefore obeys the same subsolution inequality throughout the cylinder. This is a proof device, not an extension of the original physical evolution. □

The uniform volume estimate (7.10) controls the space-time \(L^2\) norm on the positive-time part of any chosen cylinder; truncation adds at most the constant contribution \(M_0^2\) times its finite measure, and the negative-time part is also bounded in that way. Lemma 7.3 gives a pointwise bound on the smaller cylinder after using continuity of each \(u_n\) to pass from essential to ordinary values, including its boundary and terminal face. The constants can be chosen using \(d_K,\rho,N,T,f\), not \(n\). Thus

\[\sup_n\sup_{(x,t)\in K}u_n(x,t)<\infty\qquad(K\subset D_\xi\text{ compact}).\tag{7.17}\]

The limit is now finite at every nonsingular observation, not merely almost everywhere. Condition (M) is unnecessary: the only motion estimate used was uniform continuity.

7.6 Continuity off the graph and local uniform convergence

A locally bounded monotone limit need not be continuous. Uniform local continuity supplies the extra compactness needed to identify the integral solution, including boundary observations.

Local boundedness does not imply continuity of a pointwise increasing limit. We prove equicontinuity of \(u_n\) by localized kernel representations, rather than differentiating the moving coefficient.

Step 1 · Localize in space

Near a fixed nonsingular observation, take nested spatial neighborhoods and a time interval \([a,b]\) with closures away from the graph and bounds from (7.17). Choose \(a=0\) for an observation at zero, and \(a<t\) for positive-time observations. A smooth spatial cutoff \(\chi\) is one around the smaller neighborhood and supported in the larger, with \(\partial_\nu\chi=0\). For a boundary observation take radial cutoffs centered on the plane; for an interior one use balls lying strictly inside the half-space. Set \(v_n=\chi u_n\). In the weak equation, the product rule gives

\[(\partial_t-\Delta)v_n=-2\operatorname{div}(u_n\nabla\chi)+u_n\Delta\chi,\qquad\partial_\nu v_n=\chi b_nu_n.\tag{7.18}\]

The volume divergence source has zero normal component on the plane. Moving its derivative onto the kernel avoids needing uniform bounds on \(\nabla u_n\) for this continuity argument.

Step 2 · Write the representation

\[\begin{aligned}v_n(x,t)={}&P_{t-a}v_n(a)(x)\\ &+\int_a^t\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,t-s)\chi(y)b_n(y,s)u_n(y,s)\,d\sigma ds\\ &+\int_a^t\!\int_{\mathcal H}\big[2\nabla_yG(x,y,t-s)\cdot\nabla\chi(y)\\ &\hspace{95pt}+G(x,y,t-s)\Delta\chi(y)\big]u_n(y,s)\,dy ds.\end{aligned}\tag{7.19}\]

Test first against smooth compactly supported interior functions by the backward Neumann method of §7.2. The derivative term is integrated by parts against the kernel with no boundary term. This identifies (7.19) in duality. On the smaller neighborhood, all sources have uniformly bounded densities on fixed compact spatial supports, and the derivative sources are spatially separated from observations. The estimates below yield a continuous representative of this formula; its volume equality with \(v_n\) then extends to the plane by continuity.

Step 3 · Split elapsed time

Let \(h=t-s\). For recent times \(0<h<\delta\), the boundary term is bounded in absolute value by \(C\sqrt\delta\), using (2.10). For the volume term differentiate the Gaussian directly:

\[|\nabla g_h(z)|=\frac{|z|}{2h}g_h(z)\le C_Nh^{-(N+1)/2}e^{-|z|^2/(8h)}.\]

Positive distance from the cutoff derivative supports, also for their reflected images, gives the uniform bound

\[C\int_0^\delta\left(h^{-N/2}+h^{-(N+1)/2}\right)e^{-c/h}\,dh\longrightarrow0\qquad(\delta\downarrow0),\tag{7.20}\]

where \(c>0\) depends on spatial separation. The substitution \(z=c/h\) converts each integral to an exponentially decaying tail. No uncontrolled gradient appears. For elapsed times \(h\ge\delta\), the kernels and their derivatives are uniformly continuous in observations on the fixed compact ranges and uniformly bounded. In comparing observations, the change of time indicator affects an interval of length at most \(|t-t'|\) and contributes at most \(C_\delta|t-t'|\). Thus fixing \(\delta\) first and then making observation differences small controls the separated-time terms. Finally take \(\delta\) small to make the recent portions uniformly negligible.

Step 4 · Free term and the limit

For \(t\ge a+\epsilon_0\) the free terms are equicontinuous by the kernel's smoothness and the uniform compactly supported bound on \(v_n(a)\). If \(a=0\), the free term is independent of \(n\): \(P_t(\chi f)\) is continuous up to time zero by the approximate identity (even reflection). This proves equicontinuity locally at all nonsingular observations, including \(t=0,T\). The finite pointwise limit is continuous: the common local modulus passes to the limit. On any compact \(K\subset D_\xi\), Dini's theorem gives uniform convergence: for any tolerance, the relatively open sets on which the continuous error is smaller than that tolerance form an increasing cover of the compact set, hence a finite subcover and then a single index suffice. □

7.7 Strong initial trace

Two different traces. Continuity on the nonsingular closed cylinder recovers the pointwise datum including nonsingular boundary points; strong convergence in volume \(L^2\) is a separate statement and does not assign values on the plane.

At \(t=0\), every bounded approximation equals \(f\), so local uniform convergence gives the correct pointwise initial values off \(\xi(0)\). For the global norm we need a different argument: \(u\ge P_tf\ge0\) by the positive series, and \(\|u(t)\|_2\le\|f\|_2\). Hence

\[\begin{aligned}\|u(t)-P_tf\|_2^2&=\|u(t)\|_2^2+\|P_tf\|_2^2-2(u(t),P_tf)_2\\&\le\|f\|_2^2-\|P_tf\|_2^2\longrightarrow0.\end{aligned}\tag{7.21}\]

The inequality uses \((u(t),P_tf)_2\ge\|P_tf\|_2^2\). The last limit follows because even extension to the full space and Gaussian approximation give strong continuity \(P_tf\to f\) in \(L^2\) (first for smooth compact data, then by density and contraction). Adding \(\|P_tf-f\|_2\) proves \(\lim_{t\downarrow0}\|u(t)-f\|_2=0\).

Example 7.2 · Why an L² limit is not a boundary limit

Choose a smooth compactly supported function \(\psi\) on the closed half-space with \(\psi(0)=1\). Then \(\psi_j(x)=\psi(jx)\) has \(\|\psi_j\|_2=j^{-N/2}\|\psi\|_2\to0\), but \(\psi_j(0)=1\) at every index. A volume norm cannot determine behavior on a set of zero volume measure. This is why §7.6 is indispensable even after the norm convergence in §7.3.

7.8 Passing to the limit and proving minimality

Step 1 · Finite integral identity

For fixed \((x,t)\in D_\xi\), \(t>0\), define boundary integrands arbitrarily as zero on the singular graph for integration. The graph is closed relative to the cylinder by continuity of the trajectory, hence measurable. It has boundary space-time measure zero by Tonelli's theorem: at each time the exceptional boundary set is a singleton. Away from it, \(b_nu_n\uparrow Vu\), so monotone convergence applies directly to the nonnegative integrands in (7.4). Since the left-hand sides converge to a finite value,

\[u(x,t)=P_tf(x)+\lambda\int_0^t\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,t-s)\frac{u(y,s)}{|y-\xi(s)|}\,d\sigma ds<\infty.\tag{7.22}\]

In particular equality holds also at nonsingular boundary points and at terminal time, stronger than the interior equality in Definition 2.3. The limiting boundary density is locally integrable even across the graph for times strictly below \(T\): on a compact boundary-time source set with times at most \(b<T\), evaluate (7.22) at an interior point and a later time \(t>b\). The kernel has a strictly positive minimum there, so its finite weighted integral bounds the unweighted flux integral.

Step 2 · Recover the local weak equation

For compact cylinders off the graph, the \(p=2\) version of (7.14) bounds the gradients on smaller cylinders independently of \(n\). With the local uniform convergence, any weak limit of these gradients is the distributional gradient of \(u\) (test against compact smooth functions and integrate by parts). Thus \(u\) has local energy regularity there. Weak continuity of the trace map and uniform convergence of the boundary values identify its trace with the continuous boundary values. Also \(b_n\to V\) uniformly on compact boundary patches separated from the pole, so \(b_nu_n\to Vu\) uniformly there. Passing to the limit in the weak equation gives the local Robin identity away from the graph, for tests with positive interior times. This does not assert a classical solution at the singularity.

Step 3 · Compare with every competitor

Let \(v\) be any finite nonnegative integral solution for the same datum. The boundary and terminal supersolution inequality (Lemma 2.3) and \(b_n\le V\) imply \(v\ge F+A_{b_n}v\) on nonsingular positive-time observations. By positivity, induction gives

\[v\ge\sum_{k=0}^j A_{b_n}^kF\quad\text{for every }j,n.\]

Taking \(j\to\infty\) gives \(v\ge u_n\) by the bounded series construction; then \(n\to\infty\) gives \(v\ge u\). At zero time the initial values agree. This proves minimality. Since \(f\ge0\) is continuous and nonzero, it is positive on a volume subset of positive measure, hence \(u\ge P_tf>0\) for all nonsingular positive-time observations.

Conclusion

The finite integral identity, continuity, initial values and minimality establish the first assertion of Theorem 3.1. The global norm estimate and strong initial trace give the second. Strict subcriticality gives the gradient estimate by the weak compactness argument of §7.3. At criticality no global gradient bound or unrestricted uniqueness has been proved. Since \(T\) is arbitrary, the result holds on every finite time interval of a continuous trajectory; minimal solutions for successive horizons agree by the same approximation construction. □

7.9 The singular energy solution and critical weighted energy

Theorem 7.4 · Singular weak/integral identification (supplementary)

Let \(N\ge3\), \(T>0\), \(0\le\lambda<\lambda_N\), \(\xi\in C([0,T];\partial\mathcal H)\), and \(0\le f\in C_c(\overline{\mathcal H})\). Set \(\mathscr X=H^1(\mathcal H)\), \(Y=L^2(\mathcal H)\) and \(\mathcal W=L^2(0,T;\mathscr X)\cap H^1(0,T;\mathscr X^*)\), using the pivot inclusion \(Y\hookrightarrow\mathscr X^*\). The minimal integral solution is the continuous off-graph representative of the unique weak energy solution in \(\mathcal W\), with initial value f in Y. Its boundary values agree with the Sobolev trace almost everywhere. This does not assert that every integral competitor belongs to \(\mathcal W\).

\[\langle u_t,w\rangle_{\mathscr X^*,\mathscr X}+\int_{\mathcal H}\nabla u\cdot\nabla w\,dx-\int_{\partial\mathcal H}V\operatorname{Tr}u\operatorname{Tr}w\,d\sigma=0\tag{7.23}\]

The identity holds in \(\mathscr X^*\) for almost every time.

Proof · Uniform dual estimates and weak passage to the singular form

Put \(c=1-\lambda/\lambda_N>0\). For each t the singular bilinear form \(a_t(v,w)=\int\nabla v\cdot\nabla w-\int V\operatorname{Tr}v\operatorname{Tr}w\) is bounded on \(\mathscr X\) by translated Kato and weighted Cauchy–Schwarz. The same bound applies to \(a_{n,t}\) with the bounded coefficients of §7.1. Measurability on fixed pairs follows by truncating V, since the absolute boundary integral is bounded by \((\lambda/\lambda_N)\|\nabla v\|_2\|\nabla w\|_2\). Furthermore \(a_t(w,w)+c\|w\|_2^2\ge c\|w\|_{\mathscr X}^2\). Appendix B.6 therefore supplies a unique weak evolution with datum f.

The approximants \(u_n\) belong to \(\mathcal W\) by §7.2; §7.3 bounds them uniformly in \(L^2(0,T;\mathscr X)\), and their equations give \(\|(u_n)_t\|_{L^2\mathscr X^*}\le(1+\lambda/\lambda_N)\|u_n\|_{L^2\mathscr X}\). They converge strongly in \(L^2(0,T;Y)\) by monotone and dominated convergence, so a weakly convergent subsequence in \(\mathcal W\) has limit u. The continuous evaluation map \(\mathcal W\to Y\) at time zero is bounded by the Hilbert-triple estimate; hence u has trace f.

For a test \(w\in L^2(0,T;\mathscr X)\), write the boundary difference as the fixed singular form acting on \(u_n-u\), plus the error with coefficient \(b_n-V\) acting on \(u_n,w\). The former tends to zero by weak convergence and the uniform form bound. The absolute value of the latter is at most

\[\left(\int_0^T\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}V|\operatorname{Tr}u_n|^2\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_0^T\!\int_{\partial\mathcal H}(V-b_n)|\operatorname{Tr}w|^2\right)^{1/2}.\]

The first factor is uniformly bounded by Kato; the second tends to zero by dominated convergence, with integrable majorant from Kato for w. The weak derivative and bulk terms pass by weak convergence. This proves (7.23); uniqueness follows from the Hilbert-triple chain rule applied to the difference and nonnegativity of the form. At each fixed time the approximants converge strongly in Y as in §7.3; weak evaluation of the subsequence in \(\mathcal W\) identifies the continuous Y representative at every time, not merely almost everywhere in time. On compact nonsingular boundary patches, the continuous representative and the Sobolev trace agree almost everywhere by the slice estimate of §2.6 (for almost every time), using local continuity up to the plane. For \(\lambda=0\) the free Neumann solution gives the same conclusion. □

Theorem 7.5 · Limiting critical weighted-energy estimate (supplementary)

Under the compact-data hypotheses of Theorem 3.1 at \(\lambda=\lambda_N\), set \(\Psi_s(x)=\Phi_{\alpha_*}(x-\xi(s))\). Then for every \(t\in[0,T]\),

\[\boxed{\|u(t)\|_2^2+2\int_0^t\!\int_{\mathcal H}\Psi_s^2\left|\nabla_x\left(u(x,s)/\Psi_s(x)\right)\right|^2dx\,ds\le\|f\|_2^2.}\tag{7.24}\]

The gradient is the local weak spatial gradient in the open half-space, defined for almost every time. No time derivative of \(\Psi_s\) or of \(\xi\) is required. The integral is not defined as a difference of potentially infinite ordinary energies.

Proof · Ground-state inequality, local weak compactness and exhaustion

For fixed s and any \(w\in H^1(\mathcal H)\), the translated form of Proposition 6.3's approximation argument gives

\[\int_{\mathcal H}\Psi_s^2|\nabla(w/\Psi_s)|^2\,dx\le Q_{\lambda_N,\xi(s)}[w].\]

Indeed, approximate w by smooth compactly supported functions avoiding the boundary pole (the cutoff cost for smooth tests tends to zero in dimension N≥3). Kato applied to the differences gives convergence of weighted traces in their weighted L² norm. The ordinary gradients converge in L², so the form values converge. On every compact interior region the reciprocal profile is smooth with bounded derivatives, and the transformed gradients converge locally in L². Exhaustion proves the inequality. Translation is an isometry and no uniform-in-s approximation choice is needed.

For the bounded approximations the retained critical estimate in §7.3 thus gives \(\|u_n(t)\|_2^2+2\int_0^t\int\Psi_s^2|\nabla(u_n/\Psi_s)|^2\le\|f\|_2^2\). The profile is jointly continuous in x,s in the open half-space, with continuous spatial derivatives there, since the path is continuous. On each bounded interior subdomain \(K\Subset\mathcal H\), \(\Psi_s\) and its reciprocal are bounded uniformly for all s, as are their spatial derivatives. The global strong \(L^2(0,T;Y)\) convergence established in §7.3 implies strong convergence of \(u_n/\Psi_s\) in \(L^2(K\times(0,t))\). The estimate supplies a bounded sequence of spatial gradients there. Weak compactness identifies any weak gradient limit with the distributional spatial gradient of \(u/\Psi_s\), by testing against smooth compactly supported space-time functions and integrating only in space. Multiplication by the bounded \(\Psi_s\) preserves weak convergence, and weak lower semicontinuity gives the desired local bound.

For every fixed t, take the lower limit of the combined inequality using strong convergence \(u_n(t)\to u(t)\) in Y. Exhaust K by the sets \(\{|x|<j,\ x_N>1/j\}\) (or a nested interior exhaustion); local distributional gradients agree on overlaps. Monotone convergence for the nonnegative squared weighted gradient yields (7.24). The argument works for each t independently, and the gradient on the full cylinder restricts to each shorter cylinder. At t=0 the claim is the initial equality. □

This is a bound in a weighted gradient space, not a proof of global unweighted \(H^1\) regularity. For its implications and limitations regarding logarithmic profiles see Proposition 13.7 and Open question 13.2. Weak energy-class uniqueness at criticality is conditional on membership in that class; see Proposition 13.9.

Chapter 7 · Takeaways

The positive series supplies bounded solutions. Identification with weak evolution permits energy testing. The exact translated Kato inequality controls every regularization at the endpoint; Moser iteration and localized heat kernel estimates recover local boundedness and continuity without a global gradient bound. Positivity then makes monotone convergence and minimality possible.

Check your understanding
  1. Why cannot strong volume convergence replace the continuity argument?
  2. Which estimate remains uniform at critical coupling?
  3. Why is no Hölder condition on the trajectory needed?
  4. Which step uses nonnegativity of the competitors?

Answers: volume norms do not determine boundary values; the volume L² bound survives; continuity separates compact nonsingular neighborhoods from the pole; positivity permits iteration beneath every competitor and monotone convergence of the flux.

Sources
Source correspondence: research paper [4], §3 (Lemma 3.1, equations (3.1)–(3.4) and proof of Theorem 1.1); revised thesis [5], Chapter 4 and Appendix A. The retained critical form estimate follows from the manuscript energy identity; the exact dimension-four concentration family, additional scalar and geometric figures, and expanded testing and trace arguments are supplementary derivations. Appendix B.6–B.8 develops standard supporting functional analysis used by the manuscripts. The proof is stated for the same compact-data class as Theorem 3.1.

Existence is established through criticality. Chapter 8 shows why the supercritical case behaves differently under slow motion.

Part III

8. Proof of supercritical nonexistence

Why does a negative form value lead to complete divergence, rather than just some growth?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Sharpness, Gaussian comparison, and Appendix D.1–D.3 for spectral measures, forms and positive semigroups.

Negative test energy → concentration → spectral Jensen → propagation by positive kernels → moving-frame comparison → contradiction.

We prove Theorem 3.2 (research paper Theorem 1.2). Let \(N\ge3\), \(T>0\), \(u_0\in C_c(\overline{\mathcal H})\), \(u_0\ge0\), \(u_0\not\equiv0\), and \(\xi\in C([0,T];\partial\mathcal H)\). Assume \(\lambda>\lambda_N\) and the uniform motion condition (M), namely \(\omega(h)\to0\) as \(h\downarrow0\). We will rule out a finite nonnegative integral solution on every \([0,S]\), \(0<S\le T\).

In Chapter 7 the form inequality gave an upper estimate for bounded approximations. Here the sign reverses for suitable test functions. This does not yet prove nonexistence: a test function with negative energy need not describe the temperature of a particular solution. The missing link is a positive semigroup argument that turns concentration into divergence for every nonzero nonnegative datum and every interior observation.

Proof dependencies
1 · Negative formSharpness of the Kato constant2 · ConcentrationUnit L² mass, energy of order −ρ⁻²3 · AmplifyBounded Robin semigroup and spectral Jensen4 · PropagateComplete stationary divergence5 · CompareMoving kernel, slower stationary clock6 · ContradictPositive iterates dominate every bounded approximation

Steps 1–4 are stationary. Steps 5–6 use only trajectory increments. Neither part requires a singular stationary semigroup.

8.1 Negative quadratic forms

At the origin, write the form of Theorem 4.1 as

\[Q_{\mu,0}[w]=\mathcal E[w]-\mu B_0[w],\qquad B_0[w]=\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|\operatorname{Tr}w(y)|^2}{|y|}\,d\sigma(y).\tag{8.1}\]

Here \(\mu\) denotes a stationary coupling, which will later be obtained from the moving coupling \(\lambda\). The form is finite on \(H^1(\mathcal H)\) by the Kato inequality. At or below the threshold it is nonnegative. Above the threshold, the optimality proved in §6.7 ensures a real smooth compactly supported function on the closed half-space, with positive boundary mass, whose quotient \(\mathcal E[w]/B_0[w]\) is less than \(\mu\). Thus its form is negative.

Proposition 8.1 · Negative test function

If \(\mu>\lambda_N\), there exist \(a>0\) and a nonnegative, continuous, bounded, compactly supported \(\varphi\in H^1(\mathcal H)\) with \(\|\varphi\|_2=1\) and \(Q_{\mu,0}[\varphi]=-a\).

Proof. Take a real test function supplied by sharpness, replace it by its absolute value, and divide by its positive volume norm. The absolute value preserves the squared gradient integral for real Sobolev functions (the gradient is zero almost everywhere on the zero level set), the trace is the absolute value of the trace, and the boundary mass and volume norm are unchanged. See Appendix B.8. This gives the stated properties without requiring the absolute value to be smooth. □
Negative energy is not an eigenvalue

For a lower-bounded self-adjoint operator associated with a closed form, a negative form value for a unit vector implies that the spectrum meets the negative real axis: if the spectrum were nonnegative then every spectral first moment would be nonnegative. It does not imply the existence of an eigenvector. A continuous-spectrum example is given in Appendix D.1. The singular form above is unbounded below after dilation; we do not construct a self-adjoint singular generator or assert any spectral type for it. The actual proof uses only the bounded-coefficient operators.

8.2 Concentration by scaling

Compress this test function about the origin but preserve its L² norm. For \(0<\rho\le1\), define

\[\varphi_\rho(x)=\rho^{-N/2}\varphi(x/\rho).\tag{8.2}\]
Scaling calculation. The half-space is invariant under dilation. With \(x=\rho z\), the volume Jacobian is \(\rho^N\). With \(y=\rho z\) on the plane the boundary Jacobian is \(\rho^{N-1}\), while the inverse-distance coefficient contributes \(\rho^{-1}\). Hence
\[\begin{aligned}\|\varphi_\rho\|_2^2&=\|\varphi\|_2^2=1,\\ \mathcal E[\varphi_\rho]&=\rho^{-2}\mathcal E[\varphi],\\ B_0[\varphi_\rho]&=\rho^{-2}B_0[\varphi],\\ Q_{\mu,0}[\varphi_\rho]&=-a\rho^{-2}.\end{aligned}\tag{8.3}\]
The singular form is therefore unbounded below on the unit sphere of L². In particular one cannot define a semibounded operator for the entire singular form by the bounded-coefficient construction of Chapter 7. This conclusion by itself is not a proof about integral solutions.
Example 8.1 · An explicit concentrating family (supplementary)

In dimension four let \(w(x)=(1-|x|)_+\) on \(\overline{\mathcal H}\). The hemisphere area is \(\pi^2\), and the unit sphere in the boundary has area \(4\pi\). Radial integration gives

\[\|w\|_2^2=\pi^2\int_0^1 r^3(1-r)^2\,dr=\frac{\pi^2}{60},\quad \mathcal E[w]=\frac{\pi^2}{4},\quad B_0[w]=\frac{\pi}{3}.\]

For \(\varphi=\sqrt{60}\,w/\pi\), the normalized energies are \(15\) and \(20/\pi\). At \(\mu=3\), the form value is \(15-60/\pi<0\). This simple tent is not a minimizing sequence and only gives a negative value for \(\mu>3\pi/4\); Proposition 8.1 covers every supercritical coupling using sharpness instead.

We must work with bounded coefficients before using spectral theory. For an integer \(m\ge1\), put

\[V_m(y)=\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{|y|^2+m^{-2}}},\qquad q_m[w]=\mathcal E[w]-\int_{\partial\mathcal H}V_m|\operatorname{Tr}w|^2\,d\sigma.\tag{8.4}\]

For each fixed \(\rho\), monotone convergence of the nonnegative boundary integrands gives \(q_m[\varphi_\rho]\downarrow Q_{\mu,0}[\varphi_\rho]\). The limiting boundary integral is finite by Kato. Consequently, there exists \(m_\rho\) such that

\[q_m[\varphi_\rho]\le-\frac{a}{2\rho^2},\qquad m\ge m_\rho.\tag{8.5}\]

The cutoff index depends on the compression scale. We do not let \(\rho\) tend to zero at a fixed \(m\).

Proposition 8.1a · Two independent length scales (supplementary)

For the unit test function in Proposition 8.1 and \(\rho>0\), the regularized form obeys

\[q_m[\varphi_\rho]=\rho^{-2}\left[\mathcal E[\varphi]-\mu\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{|\operatorname{Tr}\varphi(y)|^2}{\sqrt{|y|^2+(m\rho)^{-2}}}\,d\sigma(y)\right].\tag{8.5a}\]

In particular, as \(m\rho\to\infty\), the expression in brackets tends to \(-a\). At fixed \(m\), however, \(q_m[\varphi_\rho]\to+\infty\) as \(\rho\downarrow0\).

Proof. In the boundary term substitute \(y=\rho z\). The trace square contributes \(\rho^{-N}\), the surface element contributes \(\rho^{N-1}\), and the denominator factors as \(\rho\sqrt{|z|^2+(m\rho)^{-2}}\). This yields (8.5a) with the bulk scaling from (8.3). The bracket converges to \(Q_{\mu,0}[\varphi]=-a\) by dominated convergence, with majorant \(\mu |\operatorname{Tr}\varphi(z)|^2/|z|\), integrable by Kato. It follows that some finite \(K_*\) has the property that \(m\rho\ge K_*\) implies (8.5). Conversely, the regularized coefficient is bounded by \(\mu m\). The trace square integral scales like \(\rho^{-1}\), so

\[q_m[\varphi_\rho]\ge\rho^{-2}\mathcal E[\varphi]-\mu m\rho^{-1}\|\operatorname{Tr}\varphi\|_2^2\longrightarrow+\infty\quad(\rho\downarrow0,\ m\text{ fixed}).\tag{8.5b}\]

Here \(\mathcal E[\varphi]>0\): a zero-gradient function is constant on the connected half-space, and a nonzero constant is not in L². Thus the quadratic inverse-length term dominates the linear one. □

Interpretation. The concentration length \(\rho\) and the regularization length \(m^{-1}\) must be compared. If the regularization length is much smaller than the test-function support, the rescaled potential is close to the singular one. If the test function is compressed inside a fixed regularization length, the potential looks bounded and cannot offset the increasing gradient energy. This is why the proof chooses \(\rho\) first and only then \(m\) large enough; a single joint path with \(m\rho\to\infty\) also suffices for the negative-form estimate, but is not needed for the quantified divergence proof.

8.3 Spectral amplification for bounded Robin problems

A negative form value alone does not prove nonexistence. Think first of a scalar eigenmode with generator −A: an eigenvalue −a of A produces exp(at). The general proof uses an average over spectral values rather than assuming an eigenfunction exists.

For any bounded continuous stationary \(V\ge0\) on the boundary, use the real symmetric form \(q_V\) of (8.4) with \(V\) in place of \(V_m\). The trace estimate of §7.2, with a sufficiently small trace tolerance, proves

\[c\|w\|_{H^1}^2\le q_V[w]+C\|w\|_2^2\le C'\|w\|_{H^1}^2\tag{8.6}\]

for suitable positive constants, possibly depending on \(V\). Thus the form is dense, closed and bounded below. The representation theorem (proved from the bounded spectral theorem in Appendix D.2) yields a self-adjoint operator \(A_V\) with

\[\begin{aligned}D(A_V)&=\{w\in H^1(\mathcal H):\exists g\in L^2(\mathcal H),\\ &\qquad q_V(w,v)=\langle g,v\rangle\ \forall v\in H^1(\mathcal H)\},\\ A_Vw&=g.\end{aligned}\tag{8.7}\]

The form is polarized in the usual way. Integration by parts on smooth domain elements gives \(A_V=-\Delta\) and \(\partial_\nu w=Vw\); the sign of the boundary term is essential. Define the heat semigroup \(S_V(t)=e^{-tA_V}\) through spectral calculus. Its weak evolution equals that of Chapter 7, as shown in Appendix D.3. In particular it gives the bounded integral solution for compact continuous data. Positivity and free-flow domination, \(S_V(t)g\ge P_tg\) for \(g\ge0\), extend to all nonnegative L² data by approximation in L². We use this order in the next section.

Lemma 8.1 · Spectral Jensen inequality

For a unit vector \(v\) in the form domain of a lower-bounded self-adjoint operator \(A\), with quadratic form \(q\), and \(h>0\),

\[\langle v,e^{-hA}v\rangle\ge \exp(-h q[v]).\tag{8.8}\]

Proof. The spectral projection measure \(E_A\) determines a probability measure \(\mathfrak m_v(J)=\|E_A(J)v\|_2^2\) for Borel sets \(J\subset\mathbb R\). Because \(\|v\|_2=1\), its total mass is one. Choose a shift \(C\) so that \(A+C\ge1\). The form-domain identity from Appendix D.2 gives

\[\int (\ell+C)\,d\mathfrak m_v(\ell)=\|(A+C)^{1/2}v\|_2^2=q[v]+C.\]

In particular \(\ell\) is integrable since it is bounded below and has finite shifted first moment. Spectral calculus yields \(\langle v,e^{-hA}v\rangle=\int e^{-h\ell}d\mathfrak m_v\), finite since the spectrum is bounded below. Jensen's inequality applied to the convex exponential gives (8.8). This uses only membership in the form domain, not \(D(A)\), and does not assume a discrete spectrum. □

Apply the lemma to \(A_{V_m}\) and \(v=\varphi_\rho\). Equation (8.5) gives, for every fixed \(\rho\) and sufficiently large \(m\),

\[\langle\varphi_\rho,S_{V_m}(h)\varphi_\rho\rangle\ge\exp\left(\frac{ha}{2\rho^2}\right).\tag{8.9}\]

This is a large pairing for specially chosen input and output. How does it force growth for the prescribed datum and at any interior point? The positivity of heat flow supplies both connections.

8.4 Complete stationary divergence

Amplification of a localized test function must be transported to the observation point. The positive kernel spreads the initial heat toward the test region, permits the spectral estimate, and spreads the result outward.

Proposition 8.2 · Stationary bounded-approximation divergence

Let \(\mu>\lambda_N\), \(f_0\in C_c(\overline{\mathcal H})\), \(f_0\ge0\), \(f_0\not\equiv0\). For \(V_m\) in (8.4), let \(w_m(x,t)=S_{V_m}(t)f_0(x)\) denote its continuous bounded integral representative. Then for every \(x\in\mathcal H\) and \(t>0\),

\[w_m(x,t)\uparrow+\infty\quad(m\to\infty).\tag{8.10}\]
Three equal time intervals — spread, amplify, propagate
First · t/3Free heat gives a positive lower bound on the test support.Middle · t/3Spectral Jensen gives exponential amplification of a pairing.Last · t/3A positive kernel transports the lower bound to x.

Schematic only: the three actual operators are bounded Robin semigroups. Replacing the first and last by free heat provides lower bounds, not an equality. The middle panel illustrates amplification of a pairing, not a temperature profile or pointwise estimate.

Proof. Fix \(x\in\mathcal H\), \(t>0\), and write \(h=t/3\). Let \(K\) be a closed half-ball containing the supports of all \(\varphi_\rho\), \(0<\rho\le1\), and put \(M_\varphi=\|\varphi\|_\infty>0\).

Step 1 · Spread the datum

By strict positivity of the Neumann kernel and nontriviality of the datum, \(P_hf_0>0\) throughout the closed half-space. Its minimum \(c\) on \(K\) is positive. Since \(\varphi_\rho\le\rho^{-N/2}M_\varphi\), positivity and free-flow domination imply

\[S_{V_m}(h)f_0\ge P_hf_0\ge \frac{c\rho^{N/2}}{M_\varphi}\varphi_\rho.\tag{8.11}\]

Step 2 · Amplify and propagate

The semigroup identity and preservation of order give an L² inequality

\[S_{V_m}(t)f_0\ge\frac{c\rho^{N/2}}{M_\varphi}P_h S_{V_m}(h)\varphi_\rho.\]

The right side has a continuous representative because \(P_h\) at positive time maps L² to continuous functions (Cauchy–Schwarz and L² continuity of translated Gaussians). The left has the continuous bounded representative from Chapter 7. Thus the a.e. inequality holds pointwise in the interior. For fixed \(x,h\), the kernel \(G(x,y,h)\) has a strictly positive minimum \(c_x\) on \(K\). Since \(S_{V_m}(h)\varphi_\rho\ge0\),

\[\begin{aligned}w_m(x,t)&\ge\frac{cc_x\rho^{N/2}}{M_\varphi}\int_K S_{V_m}(h)\varphi_\rho(y)\,dy\\&\ge\frac{cc_x\rho^N}{M_\varphi^2}\langle\varphi_\rho,S_{V_m}(h)\varphi_\rho\rangle\\&\ge\frac{cc_x}{M_\varphi^2}\rho^N e^{ha/(2\rho^2)}.\end{aligned}\tag{8.12}\]

The middle inequality uses the pointwise bound on \(\varphi_\rho\) and its support in \(K\); the last uses (8.9), for \(m\ge m_\rho\). The constants \(c,c_x,M_\varphi\) are independent of both indices.

Step 3 · Take limits in the correct order

Increasing \(m\) increases the coefficient and hence the solution by positive iteration (Lemma 7.1). For each fixed \(\rho\), (8.12) holds for every sufficiently large \(m\). Now \(N\log\rho+ha/(2\rho^2)\to+\infty\), since \(\rho^2|\log\rho|\to0\). Given any finite target, first choose \(\rho\) to make the right side exceed it, then choose \(m\ge m_\rho\). This proves (8.10). □

What has been proved? Every bounded Robin problem separately has a finite solution. Their increasing sequence diverges at each positive time and every interior point; no singular limit is assumed to be continuous or to satisfy a PDE. The original stationary result of Ishige and Ishiwata uses different truncations and logarithmic testing; the spectral proof here follows the revised research paper and thesis for the full half-space approximations. See Appendix D.4 for the logarithmic identity.

8.5 Lower Gaussian comparison in moving coordinates

Return to the moving problem with coupling \(\lambda\). If a finite integral solution exists on \([0,S]\), write \(U(x,t)=u(x+\xi(t),t)\) and \(f_0(x)=u_0(x+\xi(0))\). The moving integral equation was derived in (5.4a). For \(0<\tau\le S\), set \(M=\omega(\tau)\). This bound controls every pair of times in the comparison window, not just the displacement from time zero.

Lemma 8.2 · Lower kernel comparison

Assume \(M<\infty\). For any \(\delta>0\), let

\[D_-=(1+\delta)^{-1},\qquad c_-=(1+\delta)^{-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{(1+\delta)M^2}{4\delta}\right).\tag{8.13}\]

For \(x,y\in\overline{\mathcal H}\), \(0\le s<t\le\tau\),

\[K_\xi(x,y;t,s)\ge c_-G(x,y,D_-(t-s)).\tag{8.14}\]

Proof. Put \(h=t-s\), \(d_\xi=\xi(t)-\xi(s)\), and \(z=x-y\). Expanding the square and using \(2z\cdot d_\xi\le\delta|z|^2+\delta^{-1}|d_\xi|^2\) yields

\[|z+d_\xi|^2\le(1+\delta)|z|^2+(1+\delta^{-1})|d_\xi|^2.\]

Divide by \(4h\), use \(|d_\xi|^2/h\le M^2\), and exponentiate with a negative sign. Comparing the normalizations of \(g_h\) and \(g_{D_-h}\) supplies \(D_-^{N/2}\). The same computation holds for \(z=x-y^*\) since tangential translation commutes with reflection. Summing both lower bounds proves (8.14). Unlike the upper comparison, this direction permits every \(\delta>0\). □

Figure 5.3 already shows the Gaussian comparison itself. Here the narrower kernel runs at a slower time \(D_-t\), with a multiplicative loss \(c_-\). Both losses matter in the boundary integral.

8.6 Keeping the effective coupling supercritical

Substitute \(r=D_-t\), \(q=D_-s\) in a stationary boundary integral: \(ds=dq/D_-\). The coefficient after comparison is therefore

\[\mu_-:=\frac{c_-\lambda}{D_-}=\lambda(1+\delta)^{1-N/2}\exp\left(-\frac{(1+\delta)M^2}{4\delta}\right).\tag{8.15}\]

For fixed \(\delta>0\), condition (M) implies \(\mu_-\to\lambda(1+\delta)^{1-N/2}\) as \(\tau\downarrow0\). Since \(\lambda>\lambda_N\), choose \(\delta\) first so the latter is strictly larger than \(\lambda_N\); then choose \(\tau\le S\) small enough that \(\mu_->\lambda_N\). Equivalently, with \(R=\lambda/\lambda_N>1\), choose \(0<\delta<R^{1/(N/2-1)}-1\) and require

\[M^2<\frac{4\delta}{1+\delta}\left[\log R-(N/2-1)\log(1+\delta)\right].\tag{8.16}\]

The right side is positive by the first choice; the motion condition ensures the second. This sequential choice is important. Choosing \(\delta\) too small at a fixed nonzero \(M\) would increase the exponential penalty instead.

Example 8.2 · A constant-velocity trajectory (supplementary)

For \(N=4\), \(\lambda=2\lambda_4\), \(\xi(t)=vte_1\), \(|v|=1\), take \(\delta=1/4\). With \(\tau\le T\), \(M^2=\tau\); hence \(\mu_-/\lambda_4=1.6\,e^{-1.25\tau}\). Any \(0<\tau<\log(1.6)/1.25\approx0.376\) satisfies the strict comparison, for instance \(\tau=0.1\) if the interval permits. The argument works on arbitrarily short positive intervals.

Sequential choice of parameters

Write \(R=\lambda/\lambda_N\). Choose \(0<\delta<R^{1/(N/2-1)}-1\) while ignoring motion; this leaves a positive logarithmic margin. Then shrink \(\tau\) until (8.16) holds for the actual \(M=\omega(\tau)\). For constant velocity, this is an explicit upper bound on the window length. For a general trajectory satisfying (M), the limit supplies such a window but need not give a computable rate.

8.7 Positive iteration and contradiction

Fix the short window just chosen, abbreviate \(c=c_-\), \(D=D_-\), \(\mu=\mu_->\lambda_N\), and suppose a finite nonnegative integral solution exists. Define for \(0<t\le\tau\) and \(x\ne0\) on the closed half-space

\[\begin{aligned}F_\xi(x,t)&=\int_{\mathcal H}K_\xi(x,y;t,0)f_0(y)\,dy,\\ (A^{\rm mov}W)(x,t)&=\lambda\int_0^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}K_\xi(x,y;t,s)\frac{W(y,s)}{|y|}\,d\sigma\,ds.\end{aligned}\tag{8.17}\]

At time zero use \(F_\xi(x,0)=f_0(x)\). At the pole we may assign arbitrary nonnegative values to integrands, since its space-time section has measure zero. From the integral equation and Lemma 2.3, \(U\ge F_\xi+A^{\rm mov}U\) at every nonsingular positive-time observation, including the plane and terminal time. This boundary inequality is required because the iteration uses boundary values of the previous iterate.

Hypothetical finite solution\(U(x,t)\)
≥
Moving iterates\(U^{(j)}(x,t)\)
≥
Stationary iterates\(c\,v^{(j)}(x,Dt)\)
≥
Bounded Robin iterates at fixed m\(c\sum_{k=0}^j A_{V_m}^k(P_\cdot f_0)(x,Dt)\)
↓ first \(j\to\infty\), with m fixed
\(U(x,t)\ge c\,w_m(x,Dt)\)
↓ then \(m\to\infty\)
Contradiction: stationary divergence\(U(x,t)\ge +\infty\)
Proof of Theorem 3.2.

Step 1 · Moving iterates remain below the putative solution

\[U^{(0)}=F_\xi,\qquad U^{(j+1)}=F_\xi+A^{\rm mov}U^{(j)}.\]

Positivity and the preceding supersolution inequality imply inductively \(0\le U^{(j)}\le U\) off the pole; consequently each iterate is finite there. Measurability follows from integration of nonnegative measurable kernels. At time zero the iterates agree with the datum.

Step 2 · Compare to stationary iterates

For \(0\le r\le D\tau\) set \(v^{(0)}(x,r)=P_rf_0(x)\) and, initially allowing extended nonnegative values,

\[v^{(j+1)}(x,r)=P_rf_0(x)+\mu\int_0^r\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,r-q)\frac{v^{(j)}(y,q)}{|y|}\,d\sigma\,dq.\tag{8.18}\]

The lower kernel bound gives \(U^{(0)}(x,t)\ge c v^{(0)}(x,Dt)\). If the inequality holds for \(j\), positivity gives

\[\begin{aligned}U^{(j+1)}(x,t)&\ge cP_{Dt}f_0(x)+c^2\lambda\int_0^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,D(t-s))\frac{v^{(j)}(y,Ds)}{|y|}\,d\sigma\,ds\\&=c\left[P_{Dt}f_0(x)+\frac{c\lambda}{D}\int_0^{Dt}\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,Dt-q)\frac{v^{(j)}(y,q)}{|y|}\,d\sigma\,dq\right]\\&=c v^{(j+1)}(x,Dt).\end{aligned}\tag{8.19}\]

Here the second line uses \(q=Ds\). The comparison holds for all j and all nonsingular observations, and in particular proves finiteness of the stationary iterates off zero. No stationary singular solution has been constructed.

Step 3 · Compare with each bounded approximation

Fix \(m\). Since \(V_m\le\mu/|y|\) off the pole, the bounded iterates \(\sum_{k=0}^j A_{V_m}^k(P_\cdot f_0)\) lie below \(v^{(j)}\) by induction. For fixed m the bounded Volterra series converges by Lemma 7.1. Thus letting \(j\to\infty\) gives

\[U(x,t)\ge c\,w_m(x,Dt)\qquad\text{for every }m.\tag{8.20}\]

Fix an interior \(x\) and \(0<t<\tau\). Proposition 8.2 applies at time \(Dt>0\), because \(\mu>\lambda_N\) and the translated datum is compact continuous, nonnegative and nonzero. The right side tends to infinity as m increases, while the left side is finite. This contradiction rules out the assumed solution on every positive initial interval. □

Why each hypothesis matters

Sharpness produces a negative form precisely above the threshold. Nonnegative nonzero data allow positive heat propagation, and nonnegative solutions allow order comparison. Condition (M) guarantees a short interval with surviving supercritical coupling. This proof does not settle all rough trajectories, does not establish sign-changing nonexistence, and does not imply that bounded regularized problems become infinite at finite m. At criticality the comparison has no strict margin; existence was proved independently in Chapter 7.

Stationary divergence and the final moving comparison are applications of the extended nonnegative positive-series principle; no finite singular stationary semigroup is presupposed.

Chapter 8 · Takeaways

Negative unit-mass form values scale like −ρ⁻². Bounded Robin semigroups convert these values into exponential lower bounds. Positivity transports the amplification from arbitrary nontrivial nonnegative data to every interior point. A lower Gaussian comparison and a rescaled stationary clock preserve supercriticality for sufficiently short windows, and positive iteration transfers the stationary obstruction to the moving problem.

Check your understanding
  1. Why is a negative form value not sufficient on its own?
  2. Why must m increase after ρ is fixed?
  3. Why does the effective coupling contain 1/D?
  4. Why does the induction need boundary values?

Answers: spectral amplification and propagation are additional steps; fixing the concentration scale and then increasing m makes the regularization error small; more generally a sufficiently large product mρ suffices; the time substitution changes the integration measure; the boundary operator integrates previous iterates on the plane.

Sources
Source correspondence: research paper [4], §4 and Appendix B (bounded Robin semigroups, spectral Jensen and stationary divergence); revised thesis [5], Chapter 7, Appendix D and Chapter 5 (moving coordinates). The explicit tent example, rearranged proof roadmap, quantitative comparison condition (8.16), the two-scale Proposition 8.1a, spectral-domain examples and analytical figures are explanatory additions; the source uses ε for the concentration scale, which is written ρ here. The cited stationary result is [2]; the full-half-space proof above uses the regularizations in the supplied manuscripts instead of the original mixed-boundary truncations.

Nonexistence identifies the threshold but says little about the precise growth of an existing solution. Chapter 9 uses harmonic barriers to obtain that information.

Part III

9. Weighted solvability and upper singularity estimates

What additional information does a harmonic weight give beyond existence?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Harmonic profiles; upper Gaussian comparison; Appendix B.9 for complete normed spaces and contraction.
