Choose an admissible power → harmonic heat identity → weighted bound and contraction → local continuity → restart preserving history → uniqueness and upper bound.

Chapter 7 constructs the minimal solution for compact continuous data even at critical coupling. It does not determine its pointwise growth near the pole. Here we prove Theorem 3.4 first and then Theorem 3.3. Throughout, assume \(N\ge3\), \(T>0\), \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\), and \(\xi\in C([0,T];\partial\mathcal H)\) satisfying (M). The strict coupling gap will absorb the losses in the upper Gaussian comparison. Unlike Chapter 7, the method admits data that may be unbounded near the initial singular point and need not be in L².

Proof dependencies
1 · Choose a weightCharacteristic coefficient from Chapter 62 · Heat identityHarmonic profile gives an exact boundary integral3 · ContractUpper comparison, strict margin and Banach space4 · Recover continuityUniform recent-time bounds and dominated convergence5 · ContinueRestart and preserve the entire history6 · IdentifyMinimal positive series and weighted uniqueness7 · Bound the poleChoose exponents approaching the smaller root

9.1 Characteristic roots and the choice of weight

Recall the Poisson extension \(\Phi_\alpha=\mathcal P_{x_N}*|\cdot|^{-\alpha}\) from Lemma 6.4. Its trace is \(|x'|^{-\alpha}\), and its outward normal derivative is \(C_N(\alpha)|x'|^{-\alpha-1}\). It is homogeneous of degree \(-\alpha\), with two positive constants \(a_\alpha,A_\alpha\) such that

\[a_\alpha|x|^{-\alpha}\le\Phi_\alpha(x)\le A_\alpha|x|^{-\alpha}\quad(x\in\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{0\}).\tag{9.1}\]

The inequalities follow by taking the minimum and maximum on the compact closed unit hemisphere; positivity and continuity there were established in Chapter 6. The coefficient in the normal derivative is

\[C_N(\alpha)=2\frac{\Gamma((N-1-\alpha)/2)\Gamma((\alpha+1)/2)}{\Gamma(\alpha/2)\Gamma((N-2-\alpha)/2)},\qquad 0<\alpha<N-2.\tag{9.2}\]
Proposition 9.1 · Two characteristic roots

For \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\), the equation \(C_N(\alpha)=\lambda\) has exactly two roots \(\alpha_-(\lambda),\alpha_+(\lambda)\). They obey \(0<\alpha_-<\alpha_*<\alpha_+<N-2\), \(\alpha_*=(N-2)/2\) and \(\alpha_-+\alpha_+=N-2\). The coefficient exceeds λ strictly between the roots.

Proof. Put \(d_0=N-2\) and \(L(a)=\log\Gamma((a+1)/2)-\log\Gamma(a/2)\). Then \(\log C_N(\alpha)=\log2+L(\alpha)+L(d_0-\alpha)\). As proved in Chapter 6, \(L\) is strictly concave (equivalently use \(L''(a)=[\psi'((a+1)/2)-\psi'(a/2)]/4<0\) since the trigamma series decreases). The sum is symmetric and strictly concave; it strictly increases up to its midpoint and decreases thereafter. The Gamma pole at zero makes both endpoint limits of \(C_N\) zero, and its midpoint value is \(\lambda_N\) by (6.12). Continuity and strict monotonicity give the roots and the sign assertion. □

We take \(\alpha\in(\alpha_-,\alpha_*)\), so \(\mu=C_N(\alpha)>\lambda\). A profile with the exact smaller-root exponent has no strict margin; the chosen interval also restricts the allowed singular growth to the smaller-exponent side of critical homogeneity. The manuscript theorem uses the smaller-exponent interval. Sections 9.3–9.7 prove the same conclusions for every \(\alpha\in(\alpha_-,\alpha_+)\); see the separately stated supplementary theorem. The larger root is not used to infer the asymptotic of the solution.

Example 9.1 · Explicit roots in dimension four

When \(N=4\), Gamma recurrence gives \(C_4(1/2)=C_4(3/2)=1/2\). At \(\lambda=1/2\), the admissible interval in the theorem is therefore \((1/2,1)\). For any weight strictly inside it the coefficient exceeds 1/2. At \(\lambda=2/\pi\), the two roots merge and this interval is empty. This is a limitation of the weighted comparison proof, not a nonexistence assertion.

9.2 The harmonic heat identity

To control the moving boundary integral, we need more than the elliptic relation for \(\Phi_\alpha\). Its exact heat representation provides a finite, quantitative bound on the entire boundary integral. The following statement holds for the wider exponent range \(0<\alpha<N-2\), independently of (M).

Lemma 9.1 · Harmonic heat representation (paper Lemma 5.1)

Let \(0<\alpha<N-2\), \(\mu=C_N(\alpha)\), and \(x\in\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{0\}\). For every \(t>0\),

\[\Phi_\alpha(x)=P_t\Phi_\alpha(x)+\mu\int_0^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,t-s)\frac{\Phi_\alpha(y)}{|y|}\,d\sigma(y)\,ds.\tag{9.3}\]

Both terms are finite; in particular \(0<P_t\Phi_\alpha\le\Phi_\alpha\).

Proof. Fix \(0<\vartheta<t\) and remove the half-ball of radius \(r\) from the half-ball of radius \(R\), with \(0<r<R\). Write \(G_s(y)=G(x,y,t-s)\). Integrating \(\partial_s G_s=-\Delta_y G_s\) against the harmonic profile and using Green's second identity on this region yields
\[\frac{d}{ds}\int_{\mathcal H\cap\{r<|y|<R\}}G_s\Phi_\alpha\,dy=\int_{\partial(\mathcal H\cap\{r<|y|<R\})}(G_s\partial_\nu\Phi_\alpha-\Phi_\alpha\partial_\nu G_s)\,d\sigma.\tag{9.4}\]

Here the normal in the integral is the outward normal of the truncated region. On the planar part \(\partial_\nu G_s=0\), and the profile's normal derivative is \(\mu\Phi_\alpha/|y|\). Integrate from \(s=0\) to \(t-\vartheta\).

Technical proof · Removal of spatial and time cutoffs

Homogeneity and smoothness on the unit hemisphere yield \(|\nabla\Phi_\alpha(y)|\le C|y|^{-\alpha-1}\). On the inner hemisphere the two surface integrals are bounded by \(C_\vartheta(r^{N-2-\alpha}+r^{N-1-\alpha})\). Both vanish as \(r\downarrow0\), uniformly in \(s\in[0,t-\vartheta]\), because the kernel and its first derivatives are bounded on this elapsed-time range. On the outer hemisphere the same power factors multiply a Gaussian bounded by \(C_\vartheta e^{-cR^2}\) for large R (a derivative polynomial can be absorbed in the Gaussian). These vanish as \(R\to\infty\). The planar singular density is integrable near zero:

\[\int_{\partial\mathcal H\cap B_1}\frac{\Phi_\alpha(y)}{|y|}\,d\sigma=|\mathbb S^{N-2}|\int_0^1 v^{N-3-\alpha}\,dv=\frac{|\mathbb S^{N-2}|}{N-2-\alpha}.\tag{9.5}\]

The volume density is locally integrable since \(\alpha<N\). Gaussian decay provides integrable majorants at infinity for every fixed positive elapsed-time cutoff. Therefore the two truncated volume terms converge by dominated convergence, and the planar term by monotone convergence (or domination). This gives \(P_\vartheta\Phi_\alpha(x)=P_t\Phi_\alpha(x)+\mu\int_0^{t-\vartheta}\int G_s\Phi_\alpha/|y|\).

For x on the plane away from zero, the same identity follows by approaching it from the interior at fixed \(\vartheta\): the separated-time majorants just described work uniformly on compact observation sets. As \(\vartheta\downarrow0\), \(P_\vartheta\Phi_\alpha\to\Phi_\alpha\) locally uniformly away from zero. To justify the approximate identity for the unbounded datum, separate a small ball about zero, disjoint from the observation compact set. Its contribution is bounded by \(C\vartheta^{-N/2}e^{-c/\vartheta}\int_{B_r\cap\mathcal H}|y|^{-\alpha}dy\to0\). Off this ball the datum is bounded, and the Gaussian approximate identity gives uniform convergence using local uniform continuity. The boundary integral increases to its full value. Its finiteness follows from the finite left-hand limit. Equivalently, on recent times the source near zero has Gaussian separation and the bounded source away from zero has kernel mass \((\pi h)^{-1/2}\). This proves (9.3) at every stated point.

The conclusion is a decomposition of the fixed harmonic profile into free diffusion and a nonnegative boundary contribution. It does not assert that the moving solution is harmonic. □

Example 9.2 · An exact decomposition on the normal axis (supplementary)

In dimension four the critical profile \(\Phi_1(0,z)=2/(\pi z)\) is known from (6.13). For this stationary profile and \(z>0\), the tangential three-dimensional Gaussian integral of \(|y|^{-2}\) is \(1/(2h)\), by radial integration. The Neumann boundary kernel consequently gives

\[\frac{2}{\pi}\int_0^t\frac{e^{-z^2/(4h)}}{2\sqrt\pi\,h^{3/2}}\,dh=\frac{2}{\pi z}\operatorname{erfc}\!\left(\frac{z}{2\sqrt t}\right).\tag{9.6}\]

Using (9.3), the free fraction of the profile is \(\operatorname{erf}(z/(2\sqrt t))\) and the boundary fraction is its complement. The substitution \(v=z/(2\sqrt h)\) proves (9.6). This example illustrates the identity at a critical stationary exponent; it is not an application of the strict weighted theorem to critical coupling.

Technical remark · What happens at α = N−2?

At the excluded endpoint the Poisson extension is \(\Phi_{N-2}(x)=|x|^{2-N}\). To see this directly, put \(p_z=\mathcal P_z\) and \(\kappa_N=(N-2)|\mathbb S^{N-1}|/2\). Integration in the height variable gives \(\kappa_N\int_z^\infty p_s(x')\,ds=(|x'|^2+z^2)^{-(N-2)/2}\). At z=0 this is the boundary power (away from zero). The Poisson semigroup identity \(p_z*p_s=p_{z+s}\) follows from the multiplier in Chapter 6; Tonelli permits integrating it in s from zero to infinity. Thus the Poisson extension of the boundary power is exactly the displayed radial function.

Its outward normal derivative at height z is \((N-2)z(|x'|^2+z^2)^{-N/2}\). This is \(\kappa_N\mathcal P_z(x')\), with \(\kappa_N=(N-2)|\mathbb S^{N-1}|/2\). The normalized Poisson kernel is an approximate identity, so the normal trace converges in distributions to \(\kappa_N\delta_0\), although it tends pointwise to zero off the pole. On the inner hemisphere of radius r the term \(G_s\partial_\nu\Phi\) has limit \(\kappa_N G(x,0,t-s)\), because the normal points toward the origin and \(\partial_\nu\Phi=(N-2)r^{1-N}\); the other term is O(r). For the endpoint Green formula this gives \[\Phi_{N-2}(x)=P_t\Phi_{N-2}(x)+\kappa_N\int_0^tG(x,0,h)\,dh,\quad x\ne0.\] Remove the outer and time cutoffs as in Lemma 9.1; the point-source time integral is finite by Gaussian separation. This is not (9.3) with zero coefficient. The missing flux is concentrated at a single boundary point. This supplements the endpoint observation in the research paper Appendix A; see Lemma 6.4 for the strict-range normal trace.

9.3 Weighted data and a complete function space

The intended estimate is u ≤ M times the positive harmonic profile centered at the pole. Dividing by that profile gives a bounded quotient. This is both a near-pole growth restriction and a far-field decay restriction, not an ordinary maximum norm on u.

Let \(I=[a,b]\subset[0,T]\) and \(D_0(I)=(\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{0\})\times I\). In moving coordinates, the initial value at a restart time a is \(f_a(x)=u(x+\xi(a),a)\); for a=0 use the prescribed \(u_0\). Assume \(f_a\) is continuous off zero and \(0\le f_a\le M_a\Phi_\alpha\). The bound simultaneously restricts the allowed singularity and the behavior far from the origin.

Definition 9.1 · Weighted space
\[\begin{aligned}X_\alpha(I)&=\{W:D_0(I)\to\mathbb R\text{ Borel}:\|W\|_{X_\alpha(I)}<\infty\},\\ \|W\|_{X_\alpha(I)}&=\sup_{(x,t)\in D_0(I)}\frac{|W(x,t)|}{\Phi_\alpha(x)}.\end{aligned}\tag{9.7}\]

Use the pointwise supremum, not the essential supremum: boundary values are essential to the operator. The space includes signed functions so that differences of candidates belong to it.

Completeness. Division by the positive continuous weight is an isometric linear bijection from \(X_\alpha(I)\) to the bounded Borel functions on \(D_0(I)\) with supremum norm. A Cauchy sequence there has a uniform limit, and uniform (indeed pointwise) limits of real Borel functions are Borel. This is a Banach space. Continuity is deliberately not required at this stage; it will follow from the kernel estimates in §9.5.
Example 9.3 · Which data fit the weight?

\(\Phi_\alpha\) itself has norm one, whereas the constant function one is not in the space, since the weight decays at infinity. A continuous radial cutoff \(\chi\) equal to one for \(|x|\le1\) and zero for \(|x|\ge2\) isolates local singularity: \(\chi\Phi_\beta/\Phi_\alpha\asymp |x|^{\alpha-\beta}\) near zero. For \(0<\beta<N-2\), the cutoff datum belongs to the weight class exactly when \(\beta\le\alpha\). A bounded compact continuous datum is dominated by every such weight. For the uncut profile, the two-sided comparison (9.1) gives \(\Phi_\beta/\Phi_\alpha\asymp r^{\alpha-\beta}\) globally. Boundedness near zero requires \(\beta\le\alpha\); boundedness at infinity requires \(\beta\ge\alpha\). Hence \(\Phi_\beta\in X_\alpha\) exactly when \(\beta=\alpha\). The cutoff removes only the restriction at infinity. In particular global weight classes are not nested in general. The uncut profile has infinite L² mass at infinity for \(\alpha<\alpha_*\), so the theorem is not merely an application of Chapter 7.

9.4 Upper comparison and the weighted contraction

Choose \(\alpha_-<\alpha<\alpha_+\) (the original theorem uses the smaller interval) and put \(\mu=C_N(\alpha)>\lambda\). From (5.5), for \(0<\delta<1\), \(M=\omega(\tau)\), let

\[D=D_+=(1-\delta)^{-1},\qquad c=c_+=(1-\delta)^{-N/2}\exp\!\left(\frac{(1-\delta)M^2}{4\delta}\right).\tag{9.8}\]

The upper comparison \(K_\xi(x,y;t,s)\le cG(x,y,D(t-s))\) is valid whenever \(0<t-s\le\tau\). The quantity that matters is the effective coupling \(\mu_+=c\lambda/D\). For fixed δ its limit as \(\tau\downarrow0\) is \(\lambda(1-\delta)^{1-N/2}\); this tends to λ as δ decreases to zero. First choose δ so this limit is strictly below μ; next choose τ using (M). The result is a single pair of constants and a length that work on every window of length at most τ, including windows starting at positive times.

\[q_\alpha:=\frac{c\lambda}{D\mu}=\frac{\lambda}{\mu}(1-\delta)^{1-N/2}\exp\!\left(\frac{(1-\delta)\omega(\tau)^2}{4\delta}\right)<1.\tag{9.9}\]
Why the margin is strict

Choosing δ too small while the displacement is fixed can enlarge the exponential penalty. The order of choice is important. At \(\alpha=\alpha_-\) we have μ=λ and \(q_\alpha\ge1\), so this estimate does not prove contraction. At critical coupling there is no μ above λ among the profiles. Chapter 7 obtains critical existence by a different argument.

For a window \(I=[a,b]\) of length at most τ, define \(\mathcal A_I\) and its free term \(F_a\) by

\[\begin{aligned}(\mathcal A_I W)(x,t)&=\lambda\int_a^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}K_\xi(x,y;t,s)\frac{W(y,s)}{|y|}\,d\sigma(y)\,ds,\\ F_a(x,t)&=\int_{\mathcal H}K_\xi(x,y;t,a) f_a(y)\,dy\quad(t>a),\\ F_a(x,a)&=f_a(x),\qquad (\mathcal A_I W)(x,a)=0.\end{aligned}\tag{9.10}\]

The source at y=0 and the time diagonal may be assigned zero in the integrand; they are null sets. Borel measurability of the kernel, datum and candidate, together with parameter integration for nonnegative Borel functions, gives Borel outputs once finiteness is verified. Treat positive and negative parts separately for a signed candidate.

Proposition 9.2 · Local weighted contraction

Under (9.9), \(\mathcal A_I\) is a positive bounded linear operator on \(X_\alpha(I)\) of norm at most \(q_\alpha\). The map \(W\mapsto F_a+\mathcal A_I W\) has a unique fixed point there (see Appendix B.9), and it is nonnegative with \(\|U\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\le cM_a/(1-q_\alpha)\).

Proof. Apply upper comparison to the stationary weight in (9.10); change the time variable to \(r=D(s-a)\), so \(ds=dr/D\). Lemma 9.1 gives
\[\begin{aligned}\mathcal A_I\Phi_\alpha(x,t)&\le\frac{c\lambda}{D}\int_0^{D(t-a)}\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,D(t-a)-r)\frac{\Phi_\alpha(y)}{|y|}\,d\sigma(y)dr\\ &=q_\alpha[\Phi_\alpha(x)-P_{D(t-a)}\Phi_\alpha(x)]\le q_\alpha\Phi_\alpha(x).\end{aligned}\tag{9.11}\]

Because \(|W|\le\|W\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\Phi_\alpha\), positivity gives \(|\mathcal A_I W|\le\mathcal A_I|W|\le q_\alpha\|W\|\Phi_\alpha\). This proves absolute integrability for signed W, boundedness and the norm estimate. Similarly \(0\le F_a\le cM_a P_{D(t-a)}\Phi_\alpha\le cM_a\Phi_\alpha\) for \(t>a\); the inequality holds at a since \(c\ge1\).

The geometric series in the complete space yields

\[U=\sum_{k=0}^\infty\mathcal A_I^kF_a,\qquad \|U\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\le\frac{cM_a}{1-q_\alpha}.\tag{9.12}\]

Indeed the series converges absolutely in norm; applying the bounded operator to its partial sums and taking the norm limit verifies the fixed point. If U and W are two fixed points, \(\|U-W\|\le q_\alpha\|U-W\|\) forces equality. Positivity of each term proves nonnegativity, and \(U(x,a)=f_a(x)\). For the partial sum through order j the norm error is bounded by \(cM_a q_\alpha^{j+1}/(1-q_\alpha)\). This argument is the contraction mapping theorem in its linear affine form. □

Supplementary Proposition 9.3 · Sharper harmonic-barrier estimate

With the same assumptions and notation as Proposition 9.2, the positive solution satisfies \(\|U\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\le cM_a\). If \(S_j=\sum_{k=0}^j\mathcal A_I^kF_a\), then \(\|U-S_j\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\le cM_aq_\alpha^{j+1}\).

Proof. Let \(W=cM_a\Phi_\alpha\), constant in moving time. From (9.11) and the undiscarded free-term estimate, for t>a, writing \(P=P_{D(t-a)}\),
\[F_a+\mathcal A_I W\le cM_a[P\Phi_\alpha+q_\alpha(\Phi_\alpha-P\Phi_\alpha)]\le cM_a\Phi_\alpha=W.\tag{9.12a}\]

The last step uses \(0\le P\Phi_\alpha\le\Phi_\alpha\) and \(q_\alpha<1\). At t=a, the inequality holds since \(f_a\le M_a\Phi_\alpha\) and c≥1. By induction the positive iterates from zero stay below W. Proposition 9.2 proves norm convergence to U, so the inequality survives pointwise. Iterating the fixed point identity gives \(U-S_j=\mathcal A_I^{j+1}U\), whence the asserted sharper remainder. These estimates complement, rather than replace as a source quotation, the manuscript bounds (9.12). □

9.5 Continuity of the weighted heat potentials

A Borel fixed point is not automatically an integral solution in Definition 2.3. We must recover continuity up to the nonsingular boundary, initial time and terminal time. The argument differs from Chapter 7: here a global spatial weight supplies the dominating densities instead of a local Moser bound.

Lemma 9.2 · Weighted heat-potential continuity (paper §5.3)

Assume only continuity of ξ, \(0<\alpha<N-2\), and a Borel boundary density \(J\) with \(|J(y,s)|\le L_J|y|^{-\alpha-1}\). Then \(\int_a^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}K_\xi J\) is absolutely convergent and continuous off zero on \(D_0(I)\), with value zero at a. If \(f_a\) is continuous away from zero and \(|f_a|\le M_a\Phi_\alpha\), its moving free evolution is continuous on the same set including its initial value.

Proof · Boundary term. Fix a compact spatial observation set K in the closed half-space avoiding zero, and put \(4r=\min_{x\in K}|x|>0\). For sufficiently small \(h=t-s>0\), uniform continuity of ξ gives \(|\xi(t)-\xi(s)|<r\). For \(|y|<r\), \(|x-y+\xi(t)-\xi(s)|\ge2r\). Because y is on the plane its reflection is itself. Thus the kernel there is at most \(C_Nh^{-N/2}e^{-r^2/h}\), and the density is integrable by (9.5). For \(|y|\ge r\) the density is bounded; tangential translation does not change the boundary mass estimate (2.10).
\[\sup_{x\in K,t\in I}\int_{\max(a,t-\vartheta)}^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}|K_\xi J|\,d\sigma ds\le C\int_0^\vartheta(h^{-N/2}e^{-r^2/h}+h^{-1/2})\,dh\longrightarrow0.\tag{9.13}\]

The first integrand is integrable near zero by the substitution \(v=r^2/h\), and the second has integral \(2\sqrt\vartheta\). Constants depend on K, the datum bound and exponent, not the observation or the source time. For elapsed times at least \(\vartheta>0\), define \(R_K=\max_K|x|+2\max_{[0,T]}|\xi|\). A joint dominating function in (y,s) is

\[C_\vartheta |y|^{-\alpha-1}\exp\!\left(-\frac{((|y|-R_K)_+)^2}{4T}\right)\mathbf1_{[a,b]}(s).\tag{9.14}\]

It is integrable on boundary space-time because \(\alpha<N-2\) and the tail is Gaussian. Include the indicator \(\mathbf1_{s\le t-\vartheta}\) when integrating. For a converging observation sequence the kernels converge pointwise for every s except the moving cutoff time, a null set; the density need not be continuous in s. Dominated convergence proves continuity of the separated-time term. The uniform bound (9.13) then proves continuity of the full term, including its locally uniform vanishing as t approaches a and continuity at b.

Free term. For elapsed time at least \(\vartheta\), replace the power in (9.14) by \(|y|^{-\alpha}\) and integrate over volume. Both direct and reflected terms satisfy this bound since reflection preserves |y|. It is integrable because α<N. For h=t−a tending to zero, the portion from \(|y|<r\) tends uniformly to zero by Gaussian separation and local integrability of the volume power. On the complement the datum is bounded. The kernel has unit volume mass and concentrates at x uniformly for x in K as h→0 and ξ(t)−ξ(a)→0. More explicitly, both Gaussian terms have exponentially small mass on \(|y-x|\ge d\) for any d>0 once the tangential increment is smaller than d/2: for x,y in the closed half-space \(|x-y^*|\ge|x-y|\). Split the remaining integral into \(|y-x|<d\), controlled by uniform continuity of the datum on a compact neighborhood away from zero, and its complement, controlled by the Gaussian tail. Subtracting \(f_a(x)\) uses mass one; the integral of that bounded value over the small pole neighborhood also vanishes. This proves uniform convergence to f_a on K, including observations on the plane. □

For the fixed point in (9.12), its boundary density \(J=\lambda U/|y|\) satisfies the hypothesis because the trace of the weight is exactly \(|y|^{-\alpha}\). Applying the lemma to \(F_a+\mathcal A_IU\) shows that U is continuous. The same lemma extends any weighted integral solution's interior equation to every nonsingular observation at positive time, by continuity on both sides. This is stronger than the Fatou inequality alone and will be used in uniqueness.

9.6 Continuation on arbitrary finite intervals

The global trajectory modulus permits a common τ in (9.9). Partition the full interval \(0=t_0<t_1<\cdots<t_J=T\) so every window has length at most τ. On the first window take \(f_0(x)=u_0(x+\xi(0))\); for later windows use the moving-coordinate terminal value of the preceding one. In each case the datum is continuous away from zero and satisfies a finite weight bound. Let \(M_j=\sup_{x\ne0} f_{t_j}(x)/\Phi_\alpha(x)\) with x in the closed half-space. Then with \(L_\alpha=c/(1-q_\alpha)\),

\[\|U\|_{X_\alpha([t_j,t_{j+1}])}\le L_\alpha M_j,\qquad M_{j+1}\le L_\alpha M_j,\qquad M_j\le L_\alpha^j M_0.\tag{9.15}\]

Since \(L_\alpha\ge1\), the concatenation is bounded by \(L_\alpha^J M_0\Phi_\alpha\) on all windows. The values match at their shared endpoints and Lemma 9.2 gives continuity there. The bound can be large, but is finite for any fixed T with finitely many windows; it is not a statement of uniform-in-time boundedness on an infinite interval.

Supplementary consequence · Sharper continuation

By Proposition 9.3 on every window, \(M_{j+1}\le cM_j\) and hence \(M_j\le c^jM_0\), while \(\|U\|_{X_\alpha([t_j,t_{j+1}])}\le c^{j+1}M_0\). Therefore the global norm is at most \(c^JM_0\). This follows by finite induction, with the same uniform c; it does not assert a uniform bound as T tends to infinity. The original estimate (9.15) is retained for comparison.

Recovering the original integral equation

Window solutions alone do not establish the full equation: we must show that their restarts encode earlier boundary contributions. Return to original coordinates \(u(x,t)=U(x-\xi(t),t)\). Suppose the full identity holds through a partition point a and put \(g=u(\cdot,a)\) (the value at its singular point is immaterial in volume integrals). For \(t>a\), use the Neumann composition identity, including planar source y:

\[\int_{\mathcal H}G(x,z,t-a)G(z,y,a-s)\,dz=G(x,y,t-s),\quad s<a<t.\tag{9.16}\]

This follows by even reflection and Gaussian convolution; when y is on the plane it also follows directly from \(G(z,y,h)=2g_h(z-y)\), or by continuous extension of the composition identity. For the latter, fixed positive elapsed times give an integrable Gaussian majorant in z. Both kernels are nonnegative. Inserting the identity at a into \(P_{t-a}g\) and interchanging integrals by Tonelli yields

\[P_{t-a}g(x)=P_tu_0(x)+\lambda\int_0^a\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,t-s)\frac{u(y,s)}{|y-\xi(s)|}\,d\sigma(y)ds.\tag{9.17}\]

The left side is finite by the local free-term estimate after translation. The local window equation adds precisely the part from a to t. Starting with a=0, induction proves the global equation at every nonsingular positive-time observation, including terminal and intermediate partition points. Positivity, Tonelli and the finite weight bound justify the history decomposition; no differentiability of ξ or Markov property for an unknown singular evolution is assumed.

9.7 Minimality and uniqueness in the weighted class

We now identify the constructed solution with the full positive Volterra series. This step proves minimality even against competitors that need not be in the weight space.

Proof of Theorem 3.4 (paper Theorem 1.4)

Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.4, the preceding construction gives a nonnegative continuous integral solution u, with a global finite bound relative to the moving weight.

Use the full original-frame operator \(A_\xi\) and define the nonnegative series (pointwise extended-valued initially)

\[S(x,t)=\sum_{k=0}^\infty A_\xi^k(P_\cdot u_0)(x,t).\tag{9.18}\]

At time zero define the sum to equal u₀. Because u is a positive fixed point off the graph, induction bounds every partial sum by u. Hence S is finite there, Borel, and satisfies the same global weight bound. Monotone convergence applied to the positive integrands gives \(S=P_\cdot u_0+A_\xi S\), with finite right side. At this point we do not assume continuity of S: its weighted bound and Lemma 9.2 imply continuity in the moving frame, including the initial trace, by applying that lemma to its full-interval integral representation (the lemma does not need (M) or a short interval).

On the first window S and u have the same datum and satisfy the same contraction equation, so their moving versions coincide. At each subsequent restart use (9.17) with S in place of u. The composition formula and positivity give this identity even for a Borel fixed point with the global weight bound, and the terminal equality provides the same datum for the next window. Induction proves S=u everywhere on \(D_\xi\).

For another nonnegative integral solution v in the stated weighted class, Lemma 9.2 extends its interior identity to nonsingular boundary and terminal points. Its free term and boundary integral are continuous there; equality on the dense interior therefore implies equality throughout. The same restart argument then gives equality with u by local uniqueness and induction. For an arbitrary finite nonnegative integral competitor v with the same datum (without the weight bound), Lemma 2.3 gives \(v\ge P_\cdot u_0+A_\xi v\) at nonsingular observations. Positivity implies \(v\ge\sum_{k=0}^j A_\xi^k(P_\cdot u_0)\) for every j; passage to the increasing limit gives v≥S=u. This proves minimality, including equality at the prescribed initial points. □

Two different conclusions
Uniqueness in XαContraction: any two weighted fixed points coincideMinimality among all nonnegative integral solutionsPositive comparison: every competitor lies above the constructed one

Minimality gives a lower ordering, not an upper ordering for an arbitrary competitor. No general uniqueness theorem outside the weight class is proved. If the nonnegative datum is nonzero, strict positivity at positive time follows from the strictly positive free kernel; if the datum is zero, the minimal weighted solution is zero.

Supplementary Theorem 9.1 · Full characteristic-coefficient interval

Let N≥3, T>0, \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\) and ξ be continuous with (M). For any \(\alpha\in(\alpha_-(\lambda),\alpha_+(\lambda))\), suppose \(u_0\ge0\) is continuous on the closed half-space away from the initial pole and \(M_0=\sup_{x\ne\xi(0)}u_0(x)/\Phi_\alpha(x-\xi(0))<\infty\). There is exactly one nonnegative integral solution in the corresponding global moving weight class; it is minimal among all nonnegative integral solutions with this datum. The sharper global bound is \(c^JM_0\) for the uniform admissible partition above.

Proof. Proposition 9.1 gives \(0<\alpha<N-2\) and \(\mu=C_N(\alpha)>\lambda\). The two-sided profile estimates and Lemma 9.1 hold on this entire strict range, including the midpoint. Choose δ then τ as in (9.9), using only the strict gap and (M). The complete signed weighted space, the bounded positive operator and the free term estimates in §9.3–9.4 depend only on those inequalities. Proposition 9.2 gives a unique local fixed point; Proposition 9.3 gives the stronger endpoint bound. Lemma 9.2 requires only \(0<\alpha<N-2\) and continuity of ξ, so it supplies continuity at the endpoints. The finite partition, history identity (9.17), and positive series (9.18) use no additional restriction on α. They yield a continuous full-interval integral solution, and the same local contraction on each window proves weighted uniqueness. The extended Lemma 2.3 applies to these data, with no weight restriction on the competitor; iteration therefore gives minimality. The proof of the sharper continuation bound is the finite induction just given. □

This extension is a supplementary consequence of the manuscript argument, not a restatement of Theorem 3.4. It includes the midpoint at strictly subcritical λ, but excludes both roots and gives no weighted uniqueness result at critical coupling. The classes for different exponents are not automatically nested; if a datum belongs to several, the corresponding minimal solutions coincide by mutual minimality.

9.8 Upper singularity estimates

Return to the compact-data hypotheses of Theorem 3.1 and the strict subcritical condition with (M). For any admissible exponent, the compact continuous initial translate is bounded by a finite multiple of \(\Phi_\alpha\) by (9.1) on its compact support (including near the pole). The minimal solution from Theorem 3.4 equals the minimal solution of Theorem 3.1: each is a nonnegative integral solution, so each is below the other. The same reasoning identifies all constructions obtained with different admissible weights.

Proof of Theorem 3.3 (paper Theorem 1.3)

Given \(\eta>0\), select \(\alpha\in(\alpha_-,\min\{\alpha_*,\alpha_-+\eta\})\), a nonempty interval because both upper endpoints exceed \(\alpha_-\). The global estimate (9.15) and (9.1) give, with \(r=|x-\xi(t)|\),

\[u(x,t)\le C\Phi_\alpha(x-\xi(t))\le C A_\alpha r^{-\alpha}\le C_\eta r^{-\alpha_-(\lambda)-\eta},\qquad 0<r\le1.\tag{9.19}\]

This holds on the entire nonsingular domain \(D_\xi\), including initial, boundary and terminal observations. The constants depend on N, λ, T, ξ, u₀ and η through the profile bound and finite continuation estimate. □

Observe that \(r^{-\alpha}\le r^{-\alpha_--\eta}\) for \(r\le1\), not generally for large r. One cannot simply let η tend to zero in (9.19): the constants, comparison lengths and contraction margins depend on the chosen weight. The upper bound neither proves that the solution diverges near the pole nor yields a matching lower bound. In particular, an exact singular asymptotic at the smaller root and a critical singular asymptotic are not established by this proof.

9.9 Consolidated harmonic-weight regimes

All rows below assume \(N\ge3\), a nonnegative datum continuous off its initial pole, and \(f\le M\Phi_\alpha(\cdot-\xi(0))\) globally. Each statement concerns a finite time horizon. The word minimal means comparison with all nonnegative integral competitors.

Regime and sourceAllowed exponentsConclusion
Original Theorem 3.4, strict subcriticality and (M)\(\alpha_-<\alpha<\alpha_*\)Existence, minimality, uniqueness within the specified weight
Supplementary Theorem 9.1, same motion and coupling\(\alpha_-<\alpha<\alpha_+\)Same conclusion on the full strict coefficient-margin interval
Supplementary Theorem 13.8, stationary pole, strict subcriticality\(\alpha_-\le\alpha\le\alpha_+\)Existence and minimality, including endpoints; no endpoint uniqueness claimed
Same stationary theorem, critical coupling\(\alpha=\alpha_*\)Minimal solution bounded by \(M\Phi_*\); not a two-sided asymptotic
Zero coupling, either motion regimeNo coefficient restriction; free term must be finite and recover datumFree heat evolution; the pole plays no dynamical role

More generally Theorem 13.8 requires \(0<\alpha<N-2\) and \(0\le\lambda\le C_N(\alpha)\). For moving poles at critical coupling compact-data existence still holds by Theorem 3.1, but a global critical harmonic weight bound has not been established here. The strict weighted contraction and the stationary supersolution proof are different mechanisms. Theorems 3.4 and 9.1 do not claim uniqueness among unrestricted solutions.

Chapter 9 · Takeaways

A strict characteristic-coefficient margin produces a weighted contraction. The harmonic heat identity controls the boundary operator, the weighted kernel estimates recover continuity without differentiating the trajectory, and Tonelli plus composition preserve the entire past across a finite partition. The full positive series is minimal; contraction proves uniqueness only within the weighted class. Arbitrarily close admissible weights yield the upper singularity estimate for the compact-data minimal solution.

Check your understanding
  1. Why is the root itself not the weight used in the contraction?
  2. Why must α be less than N−2 for the heat identity?
  3. Why is a pointwise supremum used in Xα?
  4. Why is the history formula needed at each restart?
  5. Why does the upper estimate not prove the exact exponent?

Answers: the root leaves no margin; the planar singular density would cease to be locally integrable at the endpoint; boundary values enter the integral; it recovers earlier forcing rather than forgetting it; the constants need not remain bounded as the exponent loss tends to zero and no matching lower estimate has been proved.

Sources
Source correspondence: research paper [4], §5, Theorems 1.3–1.4, Lemma 5.1; revised thesis [5], Chapter 6 §§6.5–6.7 and Chapter 8 §§8.1–8.5. The root and profile calculations reuse Chapter 6 of this exposition. The normal-axis example, endpoint heat identity, sharper harmonic barrier and continuation estimates, full characteristic-interval theorem, and expanded functional-analysis arguments are supplementary derivations; the original theorem statements and manuscript estimates are retained. The source uses Hα and ε for the harmonic profile and exponent loss; the website uses Φα and η.

Chapters 10–11 develop stochastic and numerical descriptions of the equation.

Part IV

10. Probabilistic interpretation

Why do reflected random paths and boundary weights represent the same diffusion?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Neumann kernel; Appendix E.1–E.4 for conditioning, Itô calculus, reflection and sampling.

Brownian diffusion → reflection → regulator → boundary occupation → backward-time weight → exact expectation → regularized experiments and singular limit.

The same boundary interaction has three mathematical descriptions. In the PDE, positive outward normal derivative corresponds to inward heat flux. In the energy method, boundary amplification competes with the Dirichlet energy. In the probabilistic picture, a reflected diffusing path acquires a multiplicative weight when it contacts the plane. These are descriptions of the same linear evolution, not a model of literal heat particles reproducing.

Logical dependencies
1 · Brownian scalingGaussian increments generate Δ2 · ReflectionSkorokhod map and Neumann kernel3 · Local timeRegulator and boundary occupation4 · WeightItô cancellation and backward time5 · Integral solutionStrong Markov property and Volterra uniqueness6 · Singular limitMonotone approximation; Chapters 7–9 supply finiteness

10.1 Brownian motion and the heat generator

We use the probability notation in Appendix E. On a filtered probability space \((\Omega_{\rm prob},\mathcal F,(\mathcal F_s)_{s\ge0},\mathbb P)\), an \(N\)-dimensional standard Brownian motion \(W\) starts at zero, has continuous paths and independent increments with law \(W_{s+h}-W_s\sim\mathcal N(0,hI_N)\). Each coordinate has variance \(h\), not \(2h\). The diffusion associated with our heat equation is

\[Y_s=x+\sqrt2 W_s,\qquad \mathbb E_x f(Y_s)=\int_{\mathbb R^N}g_s(x-y)f(y)\,dy.\tag{10.1}\]

For \(f\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^N)\), a second-order Taylor expansion has zero expected linear term and second-order expectation \(h\Delta f(x)\), because the covariance of \(\sqrt2W_h\) is \(2h I_N\). Bounded third derivatives give a remainder of order \(h^{3/2}\) in expectation. Dividing by h proves that the infinitesimal generator on these tests is \(\Delta\). The law of \(a^{-1/2}W_{as}\) equals that of W by the increment and continuity properties. Consequently the root-mean-square displacement of Y over time h is \(\sqrt{2Nh}\), the scale in Chapter 5.

10.2 Reflection and the Skorokhod map

Let \(x\in\overline{\mathcal H}\). Tangential coordinates are unchanged by reflection. For the normal driving path \(Z_s=x_N+\sqrt2W_s^N\), define

\[L_s=\max\{0,-\min_{0\le r\le s}Z_r\},\quad X_s^N=Z_s+L_s,\quad X_s'=x'+\sqrt2W_s'.\tag{10.2}\]

The continuous regulator \(L\) is nondecreasing, starts at zero and satisfies \(\int_0^s\mathbf1_{\{X_r^N>0\}}\,dL_r=0\). Indeed a new negative running minimum can occur only when the corrected path is zero. Conversely any continuous nondecreasing correction K with these properties must satisfy \(K_s\ge L_s\): nonnegativity gives \(K_r\ge -Z_r\) and monotonicity gives the bound at s. If K exceeded L, on the open set where K>L the corrected path would be strictly positive, so K could not increase there; continuity and monotonicity of L preclude such an excursion. Thus (10.2) is the unique Skorokhod reflection map.

The map restarts at each time: after s, the new regulator increments are obtained by reflecting the path starting at \(X_s^N\) driven by \(\sqrt2(W^N_{s+r}-W^N_s)\). By independence of Brownian increments (and the strong Markov property at stopping times), the reflected process is Markov, with the same restart law also at finite stopping times.

Example 10.1 · Reflection without randomness

For \(Z_s=1-2s\) on [0,1], the regulator is \(L_s=(2s-1)_+\) and the reflected path is \(X_s^N=(1-2s)_+\). Once the driver passes below the plane, the regulator compensates exactly. This deterministic example illustrates the map; it is not Brownian motion.

Recovering the Neumann kernel

Write \(g_s^{(1)}(b)=(4\pi s)^{-1/2}e^{-b^2/(4s)}\). For a normal start a≥0 the reflection principle gives the joint endpoint and crossing law: for \(m\le\min(a,b)\),

\[\mathbb P(\min_{r\le s}Z_r\le m,\ Z_s\in db)=g_s^{(1)}(a+b-2m)\,db.\]
Derive the reflected density from this law

The no-crossing part at a positive height b has density \(g_s^{(1)}(b-a)-g_s^{(1)}(b+a)\). On paths with minimum m<0 the reflected height is z=b−m. Differentiate the crossing formula in m at fixed b, then substitute b=z+m (Jacobian one). The crossing contribution at z>0 is \(\int_{-\infty}^0 -2(g_s^{(1)})'(a+z-m)\,dm=2g_s^{(1)}(a+z)\). The two densities sum to \(g_s^{(1)}(z-a)+g_s^{(1)}(z+a)\), which integrates to one on z≥0. The density of the pair and the no-crossing contribution account for all mass; the endpoint at zero has probability zero.

Combining the normal law with the independent tangential Gaussian gives exactly the method-of-images kernel \(G\) of Chapter 2. In particular

\[\mathbb E_x[f(X_s)]=P_sf(x)=\int_{\mathcal H}G(x,y,s)f(y)\,dy.\tag{10.3}\]

The notation \(|x_N+\sqrt2B_s|\) is another realization in law of the reflected normal process (its folded Gaussian transition law follows from symmetry and independence of increments); it is not the same pathwise driver W as in (10.2). We use (10.2) to track the regulator.

10.3 Boundary local time and the occupation identity

Counting sampled contacts with a boundary would depend on the time grid. Instead the regulator accumulates the normal push needed to keep a Brownian path in the half-space. Its normalization is what makes the occupation identity match the heat kernel.

In this chapter boundary local time means the regulator L of (10.2). This fixes a normalization that varies across references. The path equation is \(dX_s=\sqrt2\,dW_s+e_N\,dL_s\); ordinary time spent exactly on the plane is zero almost surely, because \(\mathbb P_x(X_s^N=0)=0\) for s>0 and Tonelli applies. Nonetheless L can increase. For smooth functions Itô's formula gives

\[df(X_s)=\sqrt2\nabla f(X_s)\cdot dW_s+\Delta f(X_s)\,ds+\partial_N f(X_s)\,dL_s.\tag{10.4}\]

The quadratic variation of the Brownian part contributes \(\Delta f\); the finite-variation regulator has no quadratic variation or mixed quadratic variation. If \(\partial_N f=0\) on the boundary, the last term vanishes: this is the stochastic meaning of the Neumann condition.

Lemma 10.1 · Boundary occupation identity

For any nonnegative Borel function \(k:\partial\mathcal H\times[0,t]\to[0,\infty]\),

\[\mathbb E_x\int_0^t k(X_s,s)\,dL_s=\int_0^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,s)k(y,s)\,d\sigma(y)\,ds.\tag{10.5}\]

Both sides may be infinite; the argument of k is on the plane on the support of dL.

Proof. Let \(F_\varepsilon(0)=0\) and \(F_\varepsilon'(r)=\min(r/\varepsilon,1)\) for r≥0. Its derivative is Lipschitz, and \(F_\varepsilon''=\varepsilon^{-1}\mathbf1_{(0,\varepsilon)}\) almost everywhere. Smooth approximation of the derivative, or the piecewise-C² extension of Itô's formula, applies: the finitely many exceptional heights have zero time occupation by (10.3). Since \(F_\varepsilon'(0)=0\), (10.4) yields

\[L_s^{\varepsilon}:=\frac1\varepsilon\int_0^s\mathbf1_{\{0<X_r^N<\varepsilon\}}\,dr=F_\varepsilon(X_s^N)-F_\varepsilon(x_N)-\sqrt2\int_0^sF_\varepsilon'(X_r^N)\,dW_r^N.\]

Subtract (10.2). The two deterministic errors have absolute value at most ε in total. Doob's L² inequality and the Itô isometry bound the squared supremum of the stochastic difference on [0,t] by \(8\int_0^t\mathbb P_x(X_r^N<\varepsilon)dr\le C\varepsilon\sqrt t\), using the normal density bound \((\pi r)^{-1/2}\). Thus \(L^\varepsilon\to L\) uniformly in probability, and even in mean square of the supremum.

For bounded continuous k, extend k to the half-space by evaluating its tangential argument. The pathwise function \(s\mapsto k((X_s',0),s)\) is continuous. Uniform convergence in probability of the increasing integrators implies convergence of the Stieltjes integrals in probability: along any subsequence select an almost surely uniformly convergent subsubsequence; approximate the continuous integrand by step functions and use convergence of total masses. The absolute integrals are bounded by \(\|k\|_\infty L_t^\varepsilon\), uniformly integrable by the mean-square estimate. Expectations converge.

Before taking the limit, (10.3) and Fubini express the expectation as

\[\int_0^t\int_{\mathbb R^{N-1}}\frac1\varepsilon\int_0^\varepsilon G(x,(y',r),s)\,dr\ k((y',0),s)\,dy'\,ds.\]

At each fixed s>0 the tangential Gaussian dominates the integrand, and integrating that Gaussian gives a bound \(\|k\|_\infty(\pi s)^{-1/2}\) independent of ε. Dominated convergence first in space, then time, proves (10.5) for bounded continuous k. The two sides define finite Borel measures when k is an indicator (total mass finite by the same estimate); equality on bounded continuous functions determines the measures. Simple-function approximation and monotone convergence now establish the assertion for nonnegative Borel k. □

At the boundary start \(x_N=0\), the reflection principle gives \(L_t\overset{d}=|\mathcal N(0,2t)|\), so \(\mathbb E_0L_t=2\sqrt{t/\pi}\). The same value follows from integrating the boundary mass in (2.10). This checks the factor in (10.5).

10.4 Feynman–Kac representation for bounded Robin coefficients

There are two clocks: stochastic elapsed time s starts at the observation, while the physical time runs backward from t to 0. Thus a moving coefficient is sampled at t−s. The initial datum is evaluated at the path endpoint.

Consider bounded continuous \(V\ge0\) on \(\partial\mathcal H\times[0,T]\) and bounded nonnegative continuous initial datum f on the closed half-space. The equation is \(u_t=\Delta u\), \(\partial_\nu u=Vu\), \(u(\cdot,0)=f\). For a fixed target point (x,t), follow a reflected path starting from x. Define

\[A_s=\int_0^s V((X_r',0),t-r)\,dL_r,\qquad 0\le s\le t.\tag{10.6}\]

Here s denotes stochastic elapsed time. The physical time of the PDE is t−s: the path starts at the observation time t, and the terminal factor at s=t samples the initial datum. The path is not the prescribed boundary trajectory ξ. The argument t−r cannot be replaced by r for a general time-dependent V.

Theorem 10.1 · Bounded Robin Feynman–Kac formula

Under the preceding hypotheses the unique bounded continuous integral solution is

\[u(x,t)=\mathbb E_x\left[f(X_t)\exp\left(\int_0^t V((X_s',0),t-s)\,dL_s\right)\right].\tag{10.7}\]

In particular, \(V=0\) recovers the Neumann evolution.

Formal mechanism, made rigorous for bounded classical solutions. For a solution smooth enough for Itô's formula up to the plane and continuous at time zero, apply the product rule to \(M_s=e^{A_s}u(X_s,t-s)\). The result is

\[\begin{aligned}dM_s={}&\sqrt2e^{A_s}\nabla u(X_s,t-s)\cdot dW_s\\&+e^{A_s}(\Delta u-u_t)(X_s,t-s)\,ds\\&+e^{A_s}(\partial_Nu+Vu)(X_s,t-s)\,dL_s.\end{aligned}\]

The PDE cancels the ordinary-time term. Since \(\nu=-e_N\), the Robin condition cancels the local-time term. M is therefore a local martingale. Stop before leaving compact sets and before PDE time reaches zero, then pass to the endpoints. All stopped values are dominated by \(\|u\|_\infty e^{\|V\|_\infty L_t}\), integrable because \(L_t\le\sqrt2\sup_{r\le t}|W_r^N|\) has a Gaussian tail. Dominated convergence removes localization, giving (10.7). This calculation explains why the exponent is positive for amplification. It does not presume classical regularity for the continuous coefficients used below.

10.5 Derivation for bounded integral solutions

We now prove Theorem 10.1 without a classical-solution assumption. Call the expectation in (10.7) \(F(x,t)\), with \(F(x,0)=f(x)\). The Skorokhod map on a canonical continuous Brownian path is measurable jointly in the starting point, path and time: the running minimum may be evaluated using rational times and the endpoint. On each path the integrand in A is continuous and L increasing, so Riemann–Stieltjes sums on uniform partitions of [0,t] converge; these sums are jointly Borel. Parameter integration (Appendix B.9) makes F Borel. The Gaussian-tail bound makes F uniformly bounded for t≤T and every starting point x.

Proof of the integral identity. The finite-variation chain rule gives \(e^{A_t}=1+\int_0^t e^{A_t-A_s}V((X_s',0),t-s)\,dL_s\). Multiplying by f at the terminal point and taking expectations, the first term is \(P_tf\). The second involves a random Stieltjes measure; conditioning inside it at deterministic times without justification would be insufficient.

Why the strong Markov argument is valid under dL

Extend the reflected process for all s≥0. For ℓ≥0 define \(\tau_\ell=\inf\{s:L_s>\ell\}\), allowing infinity. This is a stopping time for a right-continuous filtration. For a continuous increasing L the generalized-inverse change of variables is \(\int_0^t H_s\,dL_s=\int_0^\infty\mathbf1_{\{\tau_\ell<t\}}H_{\tau_\ell}\,d\ell\) for nonnegative Borel path integrands H, with equality up to level endpoints of zero Lebesgue measure. It follows first for intervals by their L-masses, then for Borel functions by measure uniqueness. On \(\tau_\ell<t\), the restarted process and regulator increments have the reflected law starting at \(X_{\tau_\ell}\) by the strong Markov property and (10.2). The remaining physical horizon is \(t-\tau_\ell\), measurable at the stopping time. Thus the conditional expectation of \(f(X_t)e^{A_t-A_{\tau_\ell}}\) given the stopped past is \(F(X_{\tau_\ell},t-\tau_\ell)\); the assertion with a random remaining horizon follows from the independent future Brownian path and joint Borel measurability of the functional. Tonelli justifies integrating this identity in ℓ. Changing back gives the expectation of \(\int_0^t V((X_s',0),t-s)F(X_s,t-s)\,dL_s\).

Apply (10.5) to this last expression and change the deterministic integration variable from s to r=t−s. We obtain

\[F(x,t)=P_tf(x)+\int_0^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}G(x,y,t-r)V(y,r)F(y,r)\,d\sigma(y)\,dr.\tag{10.8}\]

All terms are finite by boundedness and the integrable boundary mass. Although F was initially only Borel, its boundary potential is continuous by the recent-time split (bounded density) and dominated convergence in the separated time region; \(P_tf\) is locally continuous including its initial trace by Gaussian concentration. The bounded Volterra argument of Chapter 7 applies without compact support for bounded continuous f: its supremum-norm series estimate uses only \(\|f\|_\infty\) and \(\|V\|_\infty\). Consequently (10.8) identifies F with the unique bounded integral solution. □

Inverse-regulator stopping times: geometric explanation

For continuous adapted nondecreasing L, \(\{\tau_\ell<s\}=\{L_s>\ell\}\) for the strict inverse (with s>0); right continuity of the filtration gives the stopping-time property. The pushforward of Lebesgue measure on levels \([0,L_t)\) by this inverse is dL, verified first on time intervals from their regulator increments, then by uniqueness of measures. Flat portions contribute zero level measure. This justifies the change of variables used before strong Markov conditioning.

10.6 An exactly solvable constant-coefficient model

Let \(f\equiv1\), \(V=b\ge0\) constant. This bounded-data example is not a compact-support example of Theorem 3.1. With \(z=x_N\), the reflection principle gives the law \(L_t\overset d=(|Z|-z)_+\), where \(Z\sim\mathcal N(0,2t)\). Completing the square in the half-normal integral yields

\[u(z,t)=\operatorname{erf}\!\left(\frac{z}{2\sqrt t}\right)+e^{b^2t-bz}\operatorname{erfc}\!\left(\frac{z}{2\sqrt t}-b\sqrt t\right)\quad(t>0).\tag{10.9}\]
Derivation and boundary check

Split the integral at |Z|=z. The first probability is erf(z/(2√t)); in the remaining integral multiply \(e^{b(\ell-z)}\) by \((\pi t)^{-1/2}e^{-\ell^2/(4t)}\) for ℓ>z. Completing the square gives the second term. At z=0 this is \(2e^{b^2t}\Phi_{\mathrm G}(b\sqrt{2t})\), where \(\Phi_{\mathrm G}\) denotes the standard normal distribution function (distinct from the harmonic profile \(\Phi_\alpha\)). The formula equals one at b=0, and its derivative with respect to b at zero and z=0 is \(2\sqrt{t/\pi}\). Differentiating with respect to z, the two Gaussian derivative terms cancel because \(b^2t-bz-(z/(2\sqrt t)-b\sqrt t)^2=-z^2/(4t)\), leaving \(u_z(0,t)=-bu(0,t)\), the required Robin sign. At t=0 the continuous initial value is one.

Supplementary proposition 10.1 · Exact sampling variance

Let \(m_b(z,t)=\mathbb E_z e^{bL_t}\), given by (10.9). Gaussian maximum tails make every finite exponential moment finite. For independent copies \(Y_i=e^{bL_t^{(i)}}\), expanding the square and using independence gives

\[\operatorname{Var}(Y_1)=m_{2b}(z,t)-m_b(z,t)^2,\qquad \operatorname{Var}\!\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^nY_i\right)=\frac{m_{2b}-m_b^2}{n}.\]

Cross-covariances vanish. The theoretical standard error is not a guaranteed error interval. At b=0 it vanishes exactly; at strong amplification a finite sample can miss the dominant tail.

10.7 Moving coefficients: a microscopic laboratory

To explore the time-dependent mechanism, take a bounded regularization in N=3,

\[V_\varepsilon(y,r)=\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{(y_1-a(r))^2+y_2^2+\varepsilon^2}},\qquad a(r)=v r.\]

The following experiment estimates a solution value at one observation point \(x=(0,0,z)\) using independent reflected paths and f≡1. We display the projection onto the \((x_1,x_N)\) plane, but compute the weight using both tangential coordinates. At a fixed terminal time t=1, the moving coefficient travels along the boundary in reverse physical order as the stochastic path advances. The accumulated weight at intermediate s is \(e^{A_s}\), not the value \(u(x,s)\). Only the completed expectation corresponds to \(u(x,t)\).

Supplementary proposition 10.2 · Stieltjes quadrature error

Let ξ be Lipschitz with constant v and ε>0. Write \(A_t^\Delta\) for the right-endpoint Stieltjes sum on a partition of mesh at most Δ, evaluating V on the boundary plane. Differentiation gives

\[\sup_y|\nabla_y V_\varepsilon|=\lambda\sup_{r\ge0}\frac{r}{(r^2+\varepsilon^2)^{3/2}}=\frac{2\lambda}{3\sqrt3\,\varepsilon^2}.\]

The maximum occurs at \(r=\varepsilon/\sqrt2\). At each time in a partition interval the difference from its endpoint potential is at most this constant times \(\omega_{X'}(\Delta)+v\Delta\), where \(\omega_{X'}(h)=\sup_{|s-r|\le h}|X'_s-X'_r|\) on [0,t]. Integrating against positive dL and summing yields

\[|A_t-A_t^\Delta|\le\frac{2\lambda L_t}{3\sqrt3\,\varepsilon^2}\big(\omega_{X'}(\Delta)+v\Delta\big).\tag{10.11}\]

Continuity gives almost-sure convergence for fixed ε. For bounded f, \(|f(X_t)e^{A_t^\Delta}|\le\|f\|_\infty e^{(\lambda/\varepsilon)L_t}\); the Gaussian maximum tail supplies an integrable dominator in every finite Lᵖ. The convergence is not uniform in ε. For continuous ξ replace vΔ by its ordinary continuity modulus.

10.8 Monotone regularization and scope of the interpretation

For the singular model write \(V(y,r)=\lambda/|y-\xi(r)|\) off the graph, with value +∞ on it. For \(m\ge1\), \(V_m=\min(m,V)\) is bounded continuous on the plane-time cylinder, including the pole when assigned the value m. The soft coefficients of Chapter 7 also increase to V. Define the singular path action as the monotone limit of actions for V_m. For nonnegative bounded continuous f, monotone convergence gives

\[\lim_{m\to\infty}u_m(x,t)=\mathbb E_x\left[f(X_t)\exp\left(\int_0^t\frac{\lambda}{|X_s-\xi(t-s)|}\,dL_s\right)\right]\in[0,+\infty].\tag{10.10}\]

On f(X_t)=0 the product is defined to be zero, by the monotone limit even if the action is infinite. The graph has zero expected regulator occupation: apply (10.5) to the indicator \(y=\xi(t-s)\); each planar singleton has measure zero. Hence the regulator measure assigns zero mass to graph contacts almost surely. This does not imply that the inverse-distance action is finite: unbounded values near a measure-zero set may have a divergent integral.

Identification with the compact-data minimal solution

Under Theorem 3.1's hypotheses, let A be the full singular positive boundary operator and \(A_m\) its bounded-coefficient version. For every fixed k, \(A_m^k(P_\cdot f)\uparrow A^k(P_\cdot f)\) by induction and monotone convergence, with extended values allowed. The bounded Volterra solution is the sum of these terms. The supremum over m commutes with the sum of nonnegative terms by monotone convergence on counting measure. Thus every such increasing regularization has limit \(\sum_{k\ge0}A^k(P_\cdot f)\). For the Chapter 7 regularization its limit u is a finite continuous integral solution off the graph, and the series is ≤u by positivity. Conversely each regularized series is bounded by the full series, so u is ≤ that series. They agree, including nonsingular boundary and terminal observations. This proves identification for the compact-data regime \(0<\lambda\le\lambda_N\), with continuous ξ. It uses the analytical existence theorem and does not independently prove it.

The bounded Feynman–Kac theorem uses bounded f; the extension to weighted data requires the separate monotone argument below. The stochastic path X and the prescribed singularity path ξ have separate roles. A randomly chosen ξ would require specifying the random environment and the applicable trajectory hypotheses; simulating Brownian motion for X does not justify applying condition (M) to ξ. The spectral nonexistence theorem and the Kato inequality remain the analytical results of Chapters 6–8. Neither a large empirical weight nor a finite sample mean decides singular solvability.

Supplementary proposition 10.3 · Finite singular action

Let N≥3, λ≥0, ξ continuous, and \((x,t)\in D_\xi\) with t>0. Then \(\mathbb E_x A_t<\infty\) and \(A_t<\infty\) almost surely. Here A is the monotone limit of truncated actions in (10.10), zero for λ=0.

Proof

By monotone convergence and the occupation identity,

\[\mathbb E_x A_t=\lambda\int_0^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}\frac{G(x,y,s)}{|y-\xi(t-s)|}\,d\sigma(y)\,ds.\]

Set \(r=|x-\xi(t)|/4>0\). Choose s₀>0 so that \(|\xi(t-s)-\xi(t)|<r\) for s≤s₀. For \(|y-\xi(t-s)|<r\) the distance to x is at least 2r. Since y is planar, both Gaussian terms coincide; the contribution is bounded by \(C s^{-N/2}e^{-r^2/s}\int_0^r a^{N-3}da\). The radial integral is finite for N≥3. Outside the ball the inverse distance is at most 1/r, and boundary kernel mass is at most \((\pi s)^{-1/2}\). Both time bounds are integrable. For s≥s₀ the same translated split gives a uniform Gaussian prefactor on the near part and the kernel mass on the far part. Thus the expected action is finite; a nonnegative random variable of finite mean is finite almost surely.

Scope. Finite action does not imply finite exponential moment: an exponential random variable of rate one has finite mean but infinite expectation of its exponential. The threshold concerns the datum-weighted exponential expectation. This proposition is not an existence proof.

Supplementary theorem 10.4 · Weighted-data Feynman–Kac identity

Under Chapter 9's weighted solvability hypotheses, including the supplementary full interval \(\alpha_-<\alpha<\alpha_+\), let f be nonnegative continuous off p=ξ(0), dominated by \(M\Phi_\alpha(\cdot-p)\). Then at every positive-time nonsingular observation the minimal solution satisfies

\[u(x,t)=\mathbb E_x[f(X_t)e^{A_t}]<\infty.\tag{10.12}\]
Proof by two monotone approximations

For integers j≥1 set R=|y−p|, \(\chi_j(y)=\min(1,(jR-1)_+)\min(1,(j-R)_+)\), and \(f_j=\chi_j f\) with value zero at p. These are bounded continuous compactly supported data, vanish near p and increase to f off p. At t>0 the volume transition density implies \(X_t=p\) has probability zero. Assign f value zero there.

For each bounded V_m, bounded-data Feynman–Kac identifies \(\mathbb E_x[f_j(X_t)e^{A_t^{(m)}}]\) with \(\sum_{k\ge0}A_m^k(P_\cdot f_j)(x,t)\), where A_m denotes the boundary integral operator, distinct from the action \(A_t^{(m)}\). All terms increase in both indices. Monotone convergence in the free term and inductively in every kernel integration gives \(\sup_{j,m}A_m^k(P_\cdot f_j)=A_\xi^k(P_\cdot f)\); the diagonal j=m is cofinal. Monotone convergence on counting measure interchanges sum and limit, and monotone convergence on path space gives the desired expectation. The full positive series equals the finite weighted minimal solution by §9.7, also at nonsingular boundary and terminal observations. At t=0 the initial value is recovered directly. No unweighted exponential bound or unrestricted uniqueness follows.

Corollary 10.5 · Stationary endpoint Feynman–Kac representation (supplementary)

Under all hypotheses of Theorem 13.8, for every positive-time nonsingular observation, the stationary minimal solution has the finite representation

\[u(x,t)=\mathbb E_x\!\left[f(X_t)\exp\!\left(\int_0^t\frac{\lambda}{|X_s|}\,dL_s\right)\right]\le M\Phi_\alpha(x).\tag{10.13}\]

In particular this includes \(\lambda=C_N(\alpha)\), and hence critical coupling with \(\alpha=\alpha_*\). The pole can be translated to any fixed boundary point.

Proof · Cofinal data and coefficient approximations

Choose the nested compact cutoffs of Theorem 10.4 so that \(f_j\uparrow f\) off the initial pole, with f assigned zero there. Take the bounded nonnegative continuous coefficients \(V_m\uparrow V\) off the graph, for example the soft inverse-distance regularization; if \(\lambda=0\), take all coefficients zero. For every j,m the bounded Robin formula identifies the expectation with the positive series. For each finite series term, monotone convergence of the volume integral and then the boundary integrations gives convergence to the corresponding singular term. The diagonal j=m is cofinal in the two monotone indices, and monotone convergence on counting measure and path space identifies the singular series with the expectation. Theorem 13.8 constructs its minimal solution by the stationary harmonic supersolution, so the series is finite and satisfies the claimed bound. The volume transition density makes the assigned value of f at the initial pole irrelevant for t>0; the regulator occupation identity removes the null boundary graph. Initial values are those already established by Theorem 13.8. No strict contraction or unweighted exponential moment bound is required. □

Chapter 10 · Takeaways

The generator Δ fixes the diffusion variance. Reflection produces a regulator supported on boundary contacts, not a count of hits. Its occupation identity matches the Neumann boundary kernel. The positive exponential weights derive from Robin flux, with reversed physical time. Monotone convergence defines an extended expectation; finiteness and identification with a minimal singular solution require the earlier PDE analysis.

Check your understanding

Why is the increment scale √2 rather than 1? Why is the regulator not the same as time spent on the plane? Why is t−s used in V? Why does graph occupation zero not imply finite action? Why is a completed path average different from a density of unweighted endpoints?

Answers. The generator is Δ; the plane has zero ordinary time occupation but the reflection correction increases there; the backward PDE time cancels the time derivative in Itô's formula; unbounded functions can have divergent integrals near null sets; the Robin solution uses the weighted terminal datum, and the starting point of the expectation is the observation.

Source correspondence: revised thesis [5], Appendix C §§C.1–C.7; research paper [4], §§2–3 and 5 for the kernel, bounded Volterra construction and solution classes. The research paper does not present this local-time representation. The reflection-density derivation, general-height constant-coefficient formula, exact expected slab formula and sampling algorithms are supplementary explanations. Propositions 10.1–10.3 and Theorem 10.4 are supplementary derivations; the paired refinement and spatial Monte Carlo field are additional experiments. Standard stochastic-calculus results used as inputs are listed in Appendix E.

The probability formula provides an alternative description, not an independent singular existence proof. Chapter 11 compares numerical approximations with exact benchmarks.

Part IV

11. Numerical investigations

What can a finite grid reliably tell us about the regularized PDE?

Prerequisites and proof outline

Regularization, heat balance, and Appendix F for numerical error and reproducibility.

Define the finite box → derive one update → positivity and stability → heat budgets → refinement and domain sensitivity → matched stochastic benchmark.

The numerical model uses a finite box and a bounded Robin coefficient. It illustrates diffusion and amplification but cannot establish a singular threshold. We first reproduce Chapter 9 of the revised thesis [5], then give supplementary stability and convergence arguments and a matched probabilistic benchmark.

11.1 The computational model

The theory uses an infinite half-space and a singular boundary coefficient; the experiments use a finite box, a bounded coefficient and discrete nodes. These changes are mathematically distinct: domain truncation, regularization and discretization. The resulting temperatures are not direct evaluations of a singular solution.

\[\begin{aligned}\Omega_R&=(-R,R)^2\times(0,R),\\ u_t&=\Delta u&&\text{in }\Omega_R,\\ -u_z&=V_\varepsilon u&&\text{on }\Gamma_0,\\ u&=0&&\text{on }\Gamma_D,\\ V_\varepsilon(x,y,t)&=\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{(x-a(t))^2+y^2+\varepsilon^2}}.\end{aligned}\]

The bottom face is \(\Gamma_0\); the top and sides are \(\Gamma_D\). At their intersections the discrete Dirichlet value is zero. The original settings are \(R=2,\lambda=0.55,\varepsilon=0.20\), \(a(t)=0\) or \(1.5t\), and \(T=0.28\). The initial grid field is

\[u_0(x,y,z)=\exp\left(-\frac{(x-0.15)^2+y^2}{0.5^2}-\frac{(z-0.45)^2}{0.35^2}\right),\]

with outer Dirichlet nodes overwritten by zero. The grid is uniform, \(\Delta x=\Delta y=\Delta z=\ell=0.1\), and \(\Delta t=0.0007\) (400 steps). Both runs start with the same field. The moving center alters amplification, not advection.

Why the manuscript datum is not classically compatible at time zero

The unmodified bump satisfies \(\partial_z u_0(x,y,0)=2(0.45)u_0(x,y,0)/(0.35)^2>0\), whereas the Robin condition requires \(\partial_z u_0=-V_\varepsilon u_0\le0\). Moreover, the Gaussian is positive on the lateral and top faces; overwriting the grid values does not make its continuous restriction compatible. Thus a smooth solution with bounded derivatives all the way to time zero cannot be assumed for this experiment. The numerical reproduction is retained as specified. The conditional error estimates below require separate regularity and compatible data.

Synchronized difference section and snapshot gallery

Vertical section y=0, signed moving minus stationary, common time with Figure 11.1. Colors use a symmetric scale at the selected time.

11.2 Finite differences and the Robin ghost point

Write \(u^n_{ijk}\) for the approximation at time \(n\Delta t\), and \(r=\Delta t/\ell^2\). The six neighbors of an interior node give the seven-point explicit update

\[u^{n+1}_{ijk}=(1-6r)u^n_{ijk}+r\sum_{q\sim(i,j,k)}u_q^n.\tag{11.1}\]

At a bottom node the centered normal derivative is \((u_{ij,1}-u_{ij,-1})/(2\ell)\). Since the outward normal is \(-e_z\), the ghost value satisfies

\[u_{ij,-1}=u_{ij,1}+2\ell V^n_{ij}u_{ij,0}.\]

Inserting this into the centered second derivative gives the exact discrete elimination,

\[u^{n+1}_{ij,0}=(1-6r+2\Delta t V^n_{ij}/\ell)u^n_{ij,0}+r\sum_{q\sim_{xy}(i,j,0)}u^n_q+2r u^n_{ij,1}.\tag{11.2}\]

11.3 Positivity, order and stability

Proposition 11.1 (Discrete comparison; manuscript argument). If \(0\le r\le1/6\) and \(V\ge0\), the updates have nonnegative coefficients. For nonnegative data, solutions stay nonnegative; if the datum and coefficient are ordered, the resulting arrays are ordered at every step.

Proof. Every off-diagonal weight in (11.1)–(11.2) is nonnegative and the center weight is at least \(1-6r\). Increasing \(V\) increases the center weight; induction gives the assertion. The interior row sum is at most one and the bottom row sum is at most \(1+2\Delta t B/\ell\), where \(B=\lambda/\varepsilon\). Hence \(\|u^n\|_\infty\le(1+2\Delta t B/\ell)^n\|u^0\|_\infty\). This finite-mesh bound deteriorates as \(\ell\downarrow0\).

Supplementary uniform stability and conditional convergence proof
Proposition 11.2 (Weighted maximum stability). Fix \(R,B\) and \(\ell\le\ell_0\), assume the positivity step restriction, and let \(0\le V\le B\). With \(w(z)=e^{-Bz}\), the homogeneous-Dirichlet update matrix \(M_n\) satisfies \(M_nw\le(1+C\Delta t)w\) on active nodes, for \(C=B^2\cosh(B\ell_0)\).

Proof. At interior nodes, the normal discrete Laplacian divided by \(w\) is \(2(\cosh(B\ell)-1)/\ell^2\le C\); the tangential terms cancel. At the bottom the ratio is \(2(e^{-B\ell}-1)/\ell^2+2V/\ell\le B^2\), by \(e^{-x}\le1-x+x^2/2\) for \(x\ge0\). Removing positive weights for Dirichlet nodes can only decrease these bounds. Since \(M_n\) is positive, \(\|M_nv\|_w\le(1+C\Delta t)\|v\|_w\), with \(\|v\|_w=\max |v_i|/w_i\). The product over steps is at most \(e^{Ct_n}\). Because \(e^{-BR}\le w\le1\), this is a mesh-uniform maximum-norm stability estimate at fixed regularization and box size.

Corollary (Conditional convergence). Suppose the exact regularized finite-box solution is sufficiently smooth up to every active grid node and time zero, with uniformly bounded time derivatives through order two and spatial derivatives through order four. Impose exact Dirichlet and initial grid data. Then the maximum error on \([0,T]\) is \(O(\Delta t+\ell)\) at fixed \(B,R\).

Taylor expansion gives residual per unit time \(O(\Delta t+\ell^2)\) at interior nodes. At the bottom, expand \(u(z+\ell)\) about zero. The term \(2u_z/\ell\) cancels \(2Vu/\ell\), leaving \(u_{zz}+\ell u_{zzz}/3+O(\ell^2)\). Thus the residual is \(O(\Delta t+\ell)\). The error recurrence has zero initial error and forcing \(\Delta t\) times this residual, with zero Dirichlet error. Iteration in the weighted norm gives at most \(Te^{CT}e^{BR}\) times the uniform residual bound. This proves the stated sufficient rate, not an optimal rate. The manuscript bump with imposed zero outer nodes need not satisfy compatibility conditions, so this smooth-solution estimate cannot be applied to it without additional regularity analysis. Constants are not uniform in \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\) or \(R\to\infty\).

Supplementary proposition 11.3 — second-order spatial convergence by a strict boundary barrier
Proposition 11.3 (Supplementary, conditional). Under the smoothness, exact grid data and positivity assumptions of Proposition 11.2 and its corollary, at fixed \(B,R,T\), the maximum error over active grid nodes and all time levels is \(O(\Delta t+\ell^2)\).

Proof. Write \(e^n=u^n-U^n\), where \(U^n\) is the exact solution sampled on the grid. The error satisfies \(e^{n+1}=M_ne^n+\Delta t\,r^n\) with zero initial and Dirichlet errors. Taylor expansion gives \(|r_i^n|\le K(\Delta t+\ell^2)\) at interior nodes and \(|r_i^n|\le K(\Delta t+\ell)\) at bottom nodes. These constants are independent of the mesh by the assumed derivative bounds. Put \(\kappa=B+1\), \(w_i=e^{-\kappa z_i}\), \(m=e^{-\kappa R}\), and \(d=\Delta t+\ell^2\). For \(\ell\le\ell_0\), let \(C_0=\kappa^2\cosh(\kappa\ell_0)\). The argument of Proposition 11.2 gives

\[\frac{(M_n-I)w_i}{\Delta t\,w_i}\le\begin{cases}C_0,&z_i>0,\\ C_0-2(\kappa-B)/\ell,&z_i=0.\end{cases}\]

At the bottom this follows from \(2(e^{-\kappa\ell}-1)/\ell^2+2V/\ell\le\kappa^2-2(\kappa-B)/\ell\); missing Dirichlet neighbors only lower the row. Choose \(C=C_0+1\) and \(W_i^n=A d e^{Ct_n}w_i\) on active nodes (zero Dirichlet error). Using \(e^{C\Delta t}\ge1+C\Delta t\), we have

\[W_i^{n+1}-(M_n W^n)_i\ge A d e^{Ct_n}w_i\Delta t\begin{cases}1,&z_i>0,\\1+2(\kappa-B)/\ell,&z_i=0.\end{cases}\]

Since \(d/\ell\ge\ell\), the bracket absorbs the boundary residual; indeed \(d(1+2/\ell)\ge\Delta t+\ell\). Taking \(A\ge K/m\) dominates \(\Delta t|r_i^n|\) everywhere. Positivity implies \(|e^{n+1}|\le M_n|e^n|+\Delta t|r^n|\), so induction from zero yields \(|e_i^n|\le W_i^n\le A e^{CT}d\). This proves the claim. The strict normal slope gives a boundary margin of order \(1/\ell\); treating the boundary residual as a bulk forcing would lose that gain. \(\square\)

This is a sufficient maximum-norm theorem at fixed regularization and domain. Its constants depend on the assumed derivative bounds, \(B,R,T\), and need not be uniform in singular or infinite-domain limits. It is not a convergence theorem for the incompatible manuscript bump.

11.4 Peak, mass and energy diagnostics

For a nonnegative classical solution, integrating the PDE gives

\[M'(t)=\int_{\Gamma_0}V_\varepsilon u\,dS+\int_{\Gamma_D}\partial_\nu u\,dS,\qquad M(t)=\int_{\Omega_R}u\,dx.\]

The second integral is nonpositive. A local peak can fall even when mass increases. The thesis's original diagnostic uses the full-weight grid sum \(\ell^3\sum u_{ijk}\). Its refinement study instead uses the trapezoidal mass (half weight at the bottom, all other faces zero) and analogously weighted squared \(L^2\) norm. We retain both conventions explicitly.

Observed contrast in the original stationary run: the volume maximum changes from 0.9700 to 0.3320 while the trapezoidal mass changes from 0.4695 to 0.5957. Diffusion can lower a peak while amplification increases total heat.

Exact discrete heat and energy budgets
Proposition 11.4 (Supplementary exact discrete balances). Let \(\langle u,v\rangle_\ell=\ell^3\sum_{i,j,k}\omega_k u_{ijk}v_{ijk}\) over active nodes, with \(\omega_0=1/2\), \(\omega_k=1\) otherwise. Extend grid values by zero on the Dirichlet faces. Write \(L_n=(M_n-I)/\Delta t\), \(M_\ell^n=\langle u^n,1\rangle_\ell\), and \(E_\ell(u)=\ell\sum_{\text{horizontal edges}}\omega_k(\delta u)^2+\ell\sum_{\text{vertical edges}}(\delta u)^2\), where each edge is counted once, \(\delta u\) is the difference of its endpoint values, and edges ending at a Dirichlet node are included.
\[\frac{M_\ell^{n+1}-M_\ell^n}{\Delta t}=J_R^n-J_D^n,\quad J_R^n=\ell^2\sum_{ij}V^n_{ij}u^n_{ij0}.\]

Here \(J_D^n=\ell\sum_{\text{lateral face neighbors}}\omega_k u^n_{ijk}+\ell\sum_{\text{top face neighbors}}u^n_{ijk}\); a node adjacent to two faces contributes to both. For nonnegative arrays both fluxes are nonnegative. Each pair of interior edge contributions cancels in the weighted sum; at the bottom the doubled normal coefficient is canceled by the half weight, and the Robin term contributes exactly \(J_R^n\). At the outer faces only the negative contribution from the active node remains. This proves the mass identity, including the edge and corner bookkeeping.

\[\frac{\|u^{n+1}\|_\ell^2-\|u^n\|_\ell^2}{\Delta t}=-2E_\ell(u^n)+2\ell^2\sum_{ij}V^n_{ij}|u^n_{ij0}|^2+\Delta t\|L_nu^n\|_\ell^2.\]

For the energy identity, pair the stencil with \(u^n\). On each active edge the two terms combine to minus the squared difference, including the bottom vertical edge because of the doubled coefficient and half weight; a Dirichlet edge contributes minus the active squared value. The Robin term contributes the positive boundary square. Finally expand \(\|u^n+\Delta t L_nu^n\|_\ell^2\) exactly. The last term is the nonnegative explicit-Euler correction, not a continuous PDE source. Neither identity alone implies decay. \(\square\)

11.5 Coupling, regularization and trajectory sensitivity

The six coupling runs use \(\lambda/\lambda_3=1/2,1,2\), \(\varepsilon=0.1,0.2\), the linear motion, and the original mesh. The five trajectory runs fix \(\lambda=\lambda_3,\varepsilon=0.2\), comparing a stationary pole and \(a(t)=0.42(t/T)^p\), \(p=1,0.75,0.5,0.3\). These reproduce thesis Figures 9.5–9.6. Monotone coefficient ordering explains the coupling and regularization ordering, not the trajectory ordering. The smallest regularization equals the mesh spacing and is poorly resolved.

11.6 Spatial and temporal refinement

For the linear trajectory at \(\lambda=\lambda_3,\varepsilon=0.2\), compare four meshes. Changing only the time step probes temporal sensitivity; changing both the grid and time step probes their combined effects. None of these differences is a certified error bound.

To measure actual error, use a supplementary smooth normal-coordinate problem with constant Robin coefficient \(b=0.7\), initial data \(e^{-bz}\), and prescribed exact value at \(z=R=2\). The exact solution is \(u(z,t)=e^{b^2t-bz}\). It satisfies the heat equation and \(-u_z(0,t)=bu(0,t)\). This one-dimensional benchmark is an exact tangentially constant half-space model, with exact artificial boundary values for the discrete calculation; it is not the original 3D box.

11.7 Finite-domain effects

Keeping the positive regularization, trajectory, original spacing and step fixed, we enlarge \(R=1,2,3\). The bump is restricted to the respective boxes; outer nodes are zero. These are supplementary runs. The mass is integrated over different boxes and is not a norm of a difference between solutions. A common point \((0.4,0,0.1)\) also measures local sensitivity. For nested boxes, positivity and zero Dirichlet data imply comparison by restricting the larger discrete solution to the smaller grid (assuming aligned grids and identical parameters). This does not quantify the infinite-domain error.

11.8 Matched microscopic and macroscopic benchmark

Use the same constant coefficient and exponential datum as Figure 11.6. For the half-line reflected process at height \(z\), with \(X_t=z+\sqrt2 W_t+L_t\), the path contribution simplifies:

\[e^{bL_t}e^{-bX_t}=e^{-b(z+\sqrt2W_t)}.\tag{11.3}\]

The normal moment-generating function gives the exact expectation \(e^{b^2t-bz}\). In this supplementary example, the terminal regulator is sampled from the conditional bridge minimum (Chapter 10), though it cancels algebraically. The finite-interval discretization uses the exact half-line value at its top boundary, so both methods approximate the same solution at common locations. This is not a comparison between the killed finite box and an unbounded reflected path ensemble with mismatched data.

\[\operatorname{Var}(e^{bL_t-bX_t})=e^{-2bz+2b^2t}(e^{2b^2t}-1).\]

For the exponential benchmark the theoretical standard error is \(u(z,t)\sqrt{(e^{2b^2t}-1)/n}\). It is distinct from the empirical standard error. A more stringent test retains the regulator in the weight.

Supplementary benchmark (nontrivial local time). For constant datum one, the reflection principle yields \(L_t\overset{d}= (|Z|-z)_+\) with \(Z\sim\mathcal N(0,2t)\). Splitting at \(|Z|=z\) and completing the square gives
\[m_b(z,t)=\operatorname{erf}(z/(2\sqrt t))+e^{b^2t-bz}\operatorname{erfc}(z/(2\sqrt t)-b\sqrt t).\]

The variance of \(e^{bL_t}\) is \(m_{2b}-m_b^2\). For the deterministic comparison, start instead at \(t_0=0.1\) with datum \(m_b(z,t_0)\), impose the exact time-dependent top value, and evolve for \(T=0.28\). The same datum is used in the stochastic estimator \(e^{bL_T}m_b(X_T,t_0)\). By the time-homogeneous reflected Markov property and additive regulator, its expectation equals \(m_b(z,t_0+T)\). The regulator does not cancel. The positive-time profile is smooth and compatible; the theoretical variance formula above is for datum one, not the shifted datum. Brownian-bridge terminal sampling is exact in law for ideal random draws.

11.9 Reproducibility and the order of limits

There are three independent numerical questions: convergence of the scheme for fixed \(\varepsilon,R\); removal of the outer boundary; and the singular limit \(\varepsilon\downarrow0\). The first is conditional on regularity in Proposition 11.2 and tested empirically for the manuscript bump. The latter two require additional uniform estimates. A large value at small \(\varepsilon\) is not evidence of singular nonexistence; a bounded value is not evidence of existence. The mathematical threshold was established in Chapter 7 and Chapter 8.

Reproduction recipe and data

All manuscript and supplementary box curves are recomputed in double precision using (11.1)–(11.2), with coefficient evaluated at the old time, initial and Dirichlet settings as specified above. The HTML includes the computed data, including 21 stored spatial slices and diagnostic times per experiment. The exact benchmark uses a one-dimensional explicit update and exact top data at every step. The Monte Carlo benchmark uses NumPy's default random generator and the specified seed.

Run python reproduce_chapter11.py numerical_investigations_v3_3.json with NumPy and Numba installed. Seeds 20261127 and 20261128; Python 3.13.5, NumPy 2.3.5.

Chapter 11 · Takeaways

Positive weights imply order, not decay. A weighted maximum estimate controls fixed-regularization stability. Smooth exact benchmarks measure discretization error; finite-box mesh studies measure sensitivity. None replaces the singular PDE proofs.

Source correspondence: revised thesis [5], Chapter 9, Figures 9.1–9.6 and Table 9.1 (PDF pages 56–63).
