For supercritical nonexistence one would need localized negative test functions, a bounded-approximation spectral argument (or a suitable time-dependent replacement), and lower heat-kernel comparison. Proving the analogue of the critical local estimates and the correct global solution class is a separate challenge. No general curved-domain theorem is asserted here.

Three distinct geometric targets
Strict subcriticalityFlatten charts and prove a uniform localized trace inequality with arbitrarily small leading error.
SupercriticalityConcentrate negative-form tests and establish local kernel lower bounds or a time-dependent substitute.
CriticalityControl the remainder at the exact leading constant; a positive relative error is not absorbable.
\[\lambda_N\int_{\partial\Omega}\frac{|\operatorname{Tr}w|^2}{|y-a|}d\sigma\le(1+\eta)\int_\Omega|\nabla w|^2+C_\eta\|w\|_2^2.\]

This is a target inequality, not a proved curved-domain estimate here. Uniform chart size, bounded Jacobians, comparison of Euclidean distances, a global trace bound for the remainder, and kernel continuity at the boundary would all need to be supplied. Compact smooth boundaries are a natural first setting.

13.9 Synthesis and priorities for future research

The critical weighted-energy estimate is established in Theorem 7.5, the singular weak identification in Theorem 7.4, and stationary endpoint probabilistic representation in Corollary 10.5. The research frontier is sharp pointwise critical behavior, critical stability, lower estimates and unrestricted uniqueness, not existence of the integrated weighted-gradient bound.

The table relates the proved extensions to the remaining questions.

Established in this expositionResearch implication and remaining obstacle
Fractional Hardy inequality, nonattainment, explicit sharpness and critical homogeneityWeighted kernel methods at criticality; no exact critical asymptotic from the form identity alone.
Retained critical form estimate, local estimates and strong initial traceCompactness mechanisms without global gradient coercivity; uniqueness and identification of arbitrary limits remain separate.
Joint concentration–regularization scaling and stationary spectral divergenceExplains noncommuting scales; extend local amplification arguments to rough trajectories and geometry.
Harmonic-barrier bound, sharper continuation, full weight interval, endpoint point-source obstructionStationary endpoint solvability now proved in Theorem 13.8; moving endpoint weights and matching lower bounds remain unresolved.
Finite expected action, weighted Feynman–Kac, Stieltjes error, exact sampling varianceDistinguish pathwise finiteness, exponential integrability, sampling error and time discretization.
Conditional mesh-uniform stability, second-order conditional spatial convergence, exact discrete balances, matched benchmarksReliable finite-regularization computations; no uniform bound for removing regularization or outer boundaries.
Dimension-two divergence beyond log powers, optimized small-scale criteria, nonmonotone approximation convergenceExact dimension-two growth rate, full rough-motion classification and critical trajectory stability remain open; Theorem 13.10 handles strict subcriticality.
Time-weighted gradient control, time-dependent characterization under (M), local episodesNonexistence can be triggered after an initially solvable interval; no classification when every interval lacks the required comparison margin.
General harmonic form identity, global integrability and critical volume scalingCandidate weighted heat-kernel approach to profiles, singular data and uniqueness; integrability alone is not a solvability theorem.

The trajectory-stability proof uses the standalone weak identification theorem at strict subcriticality. Proposition 2.2 handles its lower semicontinuity separately from the upper stability estimate.

Suggested sequence of mathematical investigations
1 · Stationary kernelIdentify the minimal weighted evolution; prove two-sided estimates.2 · Data and uniquenessUse kernel estimates to test sharp admissibility and zero-data obstructions.3 · Slowly moving poleTransfer lower estimates while controlling time dependence.4 · StabilityAt criticality establish uniform control of singular boundary integrals.5 · Rough motionInvestigate local and multiscale criteria beyond (M).6 · GeometryCritical separated poles, clustering, then curved boundaries.
Resolved here (supplementary)Still open in this exposition
Stationary endpoint barrierMatching lower profile, moving critical profile, endpoint uniqueness and sharp data threshold
Strict-subcritical strong stability and weak energy uniquenessCritical trajectory stability and unrestricted integral uniqueness
Separated subcritical polesIndividually critical poles, instantaneous collisions, clusters with shrinking separation, curved domains

The proposed strategies require further estimates. The supplementary results require a literature comparison and independent review before publication.

Manuscript correspondence: [4], §7, and [5], Chapter 10, identify the core open questions. The local regularity criterion, improved weighted bounds, approximation results and further deductions refer to the explicitly marked supplementary results in this exposition. Propositions 13.1–13.7 and 13.9, and Theorems 13.8, 13.10 and 13.11, are supplementary arguments in this exposition, subject to independent review. The interactive figures show exact algebraic comparisons or geometric examples, not solutions to the open problems.

For further study, use the theorem catalogue to locate precise assumptions and the appendices for proof tools. The research questions are not assertions of novelty.

Appendix A · Preliminary material

Appendix A · Integration and analytical estimates

When to use this appendix. Use when a proof exchanges integrals or takes a limit; A.1 and A.2 support the kernel and positive-series arguments in Chapters 2 and 7.

A reference for the limiting operations in the kernel, variational and probability arguments. The fundamental measure and compactness theorems stated here are standard prerequisites; the consequences used in the exposition are derived explicitly.

A.1 Nonnegative integration and limiting operations

Work with σ-finite measures and measurable functions. Tonelli's theorem permits integration in either order for nonnegative functions, even when the integral is infinite. Fubini's theorem permits signed or complex integration in either order when the absolute integral is finite. These are invoked as standard measure theory results. On counting measure, integration is summation; this is the justification for exchanging series and nonnegative integrals in Proposition 2.2.

Monotone convergence. If \(0\le f_n\uparrow f\) almost everywhere, then \(\int f_n\uparrow\int f\). This follows from the definition of the Lebesgue integral by nonnegative simple functions (for each simple function below f, approximate its integral from below using the increasing level sets of f_n). The theorem applies to all regularization limits of nonnegative flux densities.

Fatou's lemma. For \(f_n\ge0\), \(\int\liminf_n f_n\le\liminf_n\int f_n\). Apply monotone convergence to \(g_m=\inf_{n\ge m} f_n\) and use \(\int g_m\le\inf_{n\ge m}\int f_n\). This gives the supersolution extension at boundary and terminal observations and the lower comparison for nonmonotone approximations.

Dominated convergence. If \(f_n\to f\) almost everywhere and \(|f_n|\le g\in L^1\), apply Fatou to \(2g-|f_n-f|\); it gives \(\int |f_n-f|\to0\). The pointwise majorant must be independent of n. For instance, the weighted heat-potential proof separates the recent-time interval before applying dominated convergence to earlier times.

Uniform integrability on a probability space. A family bounded in \(L^p\), p>1, is uniformly integrable: \(\mathbb E[|Z|\mathbf1_{|Z|>R}]\le R^{1-p}\mathbb E|Z|^p\). If a uniformly integrable sequence converges in probability, it converges in L¹ (truncate at height R, use convergence in probability for the bounded truncations, and use Fatou along a subsequence for the limit's tails). This is the relevant probability version, not an automatic consequence of bounded L¹ norms.

A.2 Gaussian estimates and approximate identities

Completing the square yields \(g_s*g_t=g_{s+t}\), and rescaling gives \(g_h(x)=h^{-N/2}g_1(x/\sqrt h)\). In particular \(\int_{|x|>d}g_h(x)dx\to0\) for every d>0. Splitting the convolution into the ball of radius d and its complement proves recovery at every continuity point of a bounded datum; compact uniform continuity gives locally uniform convergence. For L² data, Jensen gives contraction, and translation continuity in L² (first for smooth compact functions, then by density) gives \(\|g_h*f-f\|_2\to0\). Even reflection produces the half-space results in Chapter 2.

For d>0 and any real a, \(\int_0^\eta h^{-a}e^{-d^2/(C h)}dh\to0\) as η↓0: substitute \(v=d^2/(Ch)\) and use the exponential tail of a power times \(e^{-v}\). Also \(\int_0^\eta h^{-1/2}dh=2\sqrt\eta\). These are the two independent mechanisms for recent-time smallness in Chapters 7 and 9.

A.3 Polar integrability and homogeneous weights

In m spatial dimensions, for real β, the radial integral of a power over an annulus is the angular constant times

\[\int_a^R r^{m-1-\beta}dr=\begin{cases}(R^{m-\beta}-a^{m-\beta})/(m-\beta),&\beta\ne m,\\\log(R/a),&\beta=m.\end{cases}\tag{A.1}\]

Thus integrability at zero is equivalent to β<m, while at infinity it requires β>m. The boundary has dimension N−1, not N. The graph has boundary space-time measure zero because every time section is a singleton (Tonelli). These facts justify the endpoint exclusions in the harmonic heat representation and the dimension-two obstruction.

A.4 Hölder, Young and Grönwall inequalities

For conjugate p,q>1, convexity gives \(ab\le a^p/p+b^q/q\) for nonnegative a,b. Normalize the Lᵖ and Lᑫ norms and integrate to obtain Hölder. In particular \(ab\le\epsilon a^2/2+b^2/(2\epsilon)\) for ε>0, the adjustable absorption estimate used in Chapters 7 and 13. Minkowski and the triangle inequality then give the standard norm estimates used throughout.

If an absolutely continuous \(y\ge0\) satisfies \(y'\le Cy+g\) with g integrable and C constant, multiply by \(e^{-Ct}\) and integrate: \(y(t)\le e^{Ct}y(0)+\int_0^t e^{C(t-s)}g(s)ds\). If \(y'\) is replaced by an integrated inequality, apply the differential argument to the absolutely continuous majorant \(y(0)+\int_0^t(Cy+g)\) for C≥0 and g≥0. This is the version used in the energy comparison. A nonnegative sequence with \(y_{n+1}\le(1+C\Delta t)y_n+\Delta t g_n\) obeys the corresponding geometric-sum bound because \(1+C\Delta t\le e^{C\Delta t}\).

A.5 Compactness and uniform convergence

Dini. For a monotonically increasing sequence of continuous real functions on a compact set K converging to a continuous finite function u, the open sets \(\{u-u_n<\eta\}\) form an increasing cover of K. Compactness makes one set cover K, proving uniform convergence. The same proof works for decreasing sequences by changing sign.

Arzelà–Ascoli. Uniform boundedness and equicontinuity on compact K imply that every sequence has a uniformly convergent subsequence. For compact metric K, choose finite 1/j nets, take a diagonal subsequence converging at their countable union, then use equicontinuity and a finite net to prove uniform Cauchy convergence. Conversely, if every convergent subsequence has the same possible limit and the family is relatively compact, the full sequence converges. On an increasing compact exhaustion the diagonal argument gives local compactness. For the moving graph, compact subsets must stay a positive distance from the excluded graph; no claim is made about uniform convergence across it.

Weak compactness and lower semicontinuity of norms in Hilbert spaces are recalled in Appendix B.4. The identity of a weak gradient is checked against smooth test functions, and local lower semicontinuity followed by exhaustion gives global nonnegative estimates without assuming global unweighted gradient control.

Appendix B · Preliminary material

Appendix B · Functional analysis

When to use this appendix. Use B.1–B.3 before the energy identity, B.4–B.8 for weak evolution and local estimates, and B.9 before the weighted contraction.

A reference for the energy and existence arguments, including the Hilbert-triple chain rule, form evolution theorem and Sobolev inequality used in Chapter 7.

B.1 Lebesgue spaces and energy norms

For \(1\le p<\infty\), the space \(L^p(\mathcal{H})\) consists of measurable functions, identified almost everywhere, with finite norm

\[\|f\|_{L^p(\mathcal{H})}=\left(\int_{\mathcal{H}}|f|^p\,dx\right)^{1/p}.\]

For \(p=\infty\), the norm is the essential supremum. The real Hilbert space \(L^2(\mathcal{H})\) has inner product \(\langle f,g\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{H}} fg\,dx\). We write \(\|\cdot\|_2\) for its norm unless a different domain is specified. The squared norm is an energy observable, not the total heat \(\int_{\mathcal{H}}u\).

B.2 Weak derivatives and Sobolev spaces

A function \(g_j\) is the weak derivative \(\partial_j f\) if \(\int_{\mathcal{H}} f\,\partial_j\varphi=-\int_{\mathcal{H}} g_j\varphi\) for every \(\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\mathcal{H})\), with the integrals well-defined. The first Sobolev space is

\[H^1(\mathcal{H})=\{f\in L^2(\mathcal{H}):\partial_jf\in L^2(\mathcal{H}),\ 1\le j\le N\},\quad \|f\|_{H^1}^2=\|f\|_2^2+\|\nabla f\|_2^2.\]

For example, \(f(x)=e^{-|x|^2}\) belongs to \(H^1(\mathcal{H})\) since both the function and its first derivatives are square integrable.

B.3 Boundary traces

Values of an \(L^2\) equivalence class cannot simply be restricted to a set of zero volume measure. The continuous trace operator \(\operatorname{Tr}:H^1(\mathcal{H})\to L^2(\partial \mathcal{H})\) extends boundary restriction for smooth functions.

Lemma B.1 · Elementary half-space trace estimate (paper (3.1))
\[\|\operatorname{Tr}w\|_{L^2(\partial \mathcal{H})}^2\le2\|w\|_2\|\partial_N w\|_2\le\eta\|\nabla w\|_2^2+\eta^{-1}\|w\|_2^2,\quad\eta>0.\tag{B.1}\]

Proof sketch

For a smooth function compactly supported on the closed half-space, integrate \(-\partial_N|w|^2\) in the normal direction from zero to infinity, then integrate over the boundary variables. Cauchy–Schwarz gives the first inequality; Young's inequality gives the second. Smooth approximation by reflection, cutoff and mollification extends the bound to \(H^1(\mathcal{H})\) and defines the continuous trace.

Why we need this. The Robin term depends on boundary values, while the energy controls bulk quantities. The ordinary trace estimate handles bounded coefficients; Chapter 6 introduces the sharper weighted Kato inequality needed for the singular coefficient.

B.4 Strong and weak convergence

Strong convergence \(f_n\to f\) in a Hilbert space means \(\|f_n-f\|\to0\). Weak convergence \(f_n\rightharpoonup f\) means \(\langle f_n,g\rangle\to\langle f,g\rangle\) for every vector \(g\) in the space. Bounded sequences in a Hilbert space have weakly convergent subsequences, and

\[\|f\|\le\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|f_n\|\quad\text{if }f_n\rightharpoonup f.\]

For example, an orthonormal sequence converges weakly to zero but its norm remains one. The norm estimate follows by pairing with \(f\) and applying Cauchy–Schwarz (the case \(f=0\) is immediate). Weak convergence can retain an energy bound without giving pointwise convergence.

B.5 Time-dependent spaces

For a Hilbert space \(\mathscr X\), \(L^2(0,T;\mathscr X)\) is the space of strongly measurable \(\mathscr X\)-valued functions whose squared norm is integrable in time. In particular,

\[\|u\|_{L^2(0,T;H^1(\mathcal{H}))}^2=\int_0^T\big(\|u(t)\|_2^2+\|\nabla u(t)\|_2^2\big)\,dt.\]

These functions are defined up to equality almost everywhere in time. In a weak evolution argument one may take \(\mathscr X=H^1(\mathcal{H})\) and its continuous dual \(\mathscr X^*\), with the dense continuous embeddings \(\mathscr X\hookrightarrow L^2(\mathcal{H})\hookrightarrow \mathscr X^*\) via the \(L^2\) pairing. A time derivative interpreted in \(\mathscr X^*\) is weaker than a classical derivative. See B.6 for the precise weak evolution theorem and proof.

Why we need this. Strictly subcritical energy bounds control a time-integrated gradient, while at critical coupling that global gradient bound is not supplied by the same estimate. Local estimates off the singular graph are therefore particularly important.

B.6 Hilbert triples and measurable form evolution

Here the pivot space is a real Hilbert space \(Y\), and \(\mathscr X\subset Y\) is a separable Hilbert space with dense continuous inclusion. Identify \(Y\) with a subspace of \(\mathscr X^*\) using the \(Y\)-inner product. Write

\[\mathcal W=\{v\in L^2(0,T;\mathscr X):v_t\in L^2(0,T;\mathscr X^*)\},\]

where the derivative is a distribution in time. The basic Hilbert-triple result is that \(\mathcal W\) has a continuous embedding into \(C([0,T];Y)\), and

\[\|v(t)\|_Y^2-\|v(s)\|_Y^2=2\int_s^t\langle v_r,v\rangle_{\mathscr X^*,\mathscr X}\,dr.\tag{B.2}\]
Proof of the chain rule and continuity

Since \(v\) is in the ordinary Hilbert-valued space \(H^1(0,T;\mathscr X^*)\), it has continuous dual-space values and may be extended by time reflection past each endpoint. Reflection preserves both local \(L^2(\mathscr X)\) and the weak time derivative in \(L^2(\mathscr X^*)\), with no jumps. Convolve the extension in time with a smooth approximate identity. These smooth \(\mathscr X\)-valued functions converge in both defining norms of \(\mathcal W\). For any smooth difference \(z\) between two such approximations, choose a time \(s\) with \(\|z(s)\|_Y^2\le T^{-1}\int_0^T\|z\|_Y^2\). Integrating the classical squared-norm derivative from that time gives

\[\sup_t\|z(t)\|_Y^2\le T^{-1}\|z\|_{L^2(Y)}^2+2\|z_t\|_{L^2(\mathscr X^*)}\|z\|_{L^2(\mathscr X)}.\]

The approximations are therefore Cauchy in \(C([0,T];Y)\), and their limit represents \(v\). The dual products converge in \(L^1\) by Cauchy–Schwarz, so the classical identity passes to (B.2). Polarization gives the corresponding dual-pairing product rule for two functions in \(\mathcal W\). The ordinary Hilbert-valued fundamental theorem used for the reflection follows by integrating the weak derivative and identifying the difference as a constant distribution.

Theorem B.2 · Nonautonomous form evolution

Let real bilinear forms \(a_t\) on \(\mathscr X\) be measurable in \(t\) on each pair of vectors and assume, with constants independent of time, \(|a_t(v,w)|\le M\|v\|_{\mathscr X}\|w\|_{\mathscr X}\) and \(a_t(w,w)+c_0\|w\|_Y^2\ge c_1\|w\|_{\mathscr X}^2\), \(c_1>0\). For every \(f\in Y\) there exists exactly one \(v\in\mathcal W\) with \(v(0)=f\) and \(\langle v_t,w\rangle+a_t(v,w)=0\) for almost every time and all \(w\in\mathscr X\).

Galerkin proof of the form theorem

Choose a countable dense sequence in \(\mathscr X\), discard linear dependencies and Gram–Schmidt it in \(Y\). Its first \(m\) elements span a space \(E_m\), the union of which is dense in \(\mathscr X\) and \(Y\). Solve the finite-dimensional linear ODE on \(E_m\) using the projected initial value \(f_m\) (orthogonal projection in \(Y\)). The matrices are measurable and bounded for each \(m\); successive time integrals of the integral equation converge by the exponential series, giving an absolutely continuous solution. Testing with that solution gives

\[\tfrac12\frac d{dt}\|v_m\|_Y^2+c_1\|v_m\|_{\mathscr X}^2\le c_0\|v_m\|_Y^2.\]

Multiplication by \(e^{-2\max(c_0,0)t}\) bounds the volume norm and integrated \(\mathscr X\) norm uniformly. Take a weak subsequential limit in \(L^2(0,T;\mathscr X)\). Test the ODE against any fixed basis vector times a smooth scalar time function vanishing at \(T\), then integrate by parts in time. For large \(m\) that vector belongs to \(E_m\). Both terms are continuous linear functionals of the weakly convergent sequence, by the uniform form bound and the continuous pivot embedding. The initial term tends to \((f,w)_Y\) since \(f_m\to f\) in \(Y\). Density extends this integrated identity to every \(w\in\mathscr X\). In particular the distributional time derivative equals \(-a_t(v,\cdot)\in L^2(0,T;\mathscr X^*)\) (strong measurability follows by approximating the strongly measurable \(v\) by simple \(\mathscr X\)-valued functions: for fixed vectors, the weakly measurable \(\mathscr X^*\)-valued form map is strongly measurable since the dual of a separable Hilbert space is separable; the uniform bound then permits passage to the pointwise norm limit). No uniform dual-norm bound on the projected derivatives is needed. Apply (B.2) to get the continuous representative, and integrate its pairing with fixed vectors to identify its initial trace as \(f\). The energy inequality for the difference of two solutions and the same exponential factor give uniqueness. This proves the theorem in the case used in §7.2.

B.7 The Sobolev gain used in Moser iteration

For \(N\ge3\), the Euclidean Sobolev inequality is \(\|g\|_{2N/(N-2)}\le C_N\|\nabla g\|_2\) for \(g\in H^1(\mathbb R^N)\). A standard elementary derivation starts from \(\|F\|_{N/(N-1)}\le C_N\sum_i\|\partial_i F\|_1\) on compactly supported smooth functions.

Derivation of the spatial and parabolic inequalities

Along each coordinate line, the fundamental theorem bounds \(|F(x)|\) by \(A_i(\widehat x_i)=\int_{\mathbb R}|\partial_i F(x)|dx_i\). Multiply the \(N\) bounds after raising each to power \(1/(N-1)\). The integral of \(\prod_i A_i(\widehat x_i)^{1/(N-1)}\) is at most \(\prod_i(\int A_i)^{1/(N-1)}\). To see this last product inequality, integrate in \(x_N\) first, using Hölder with \(N-1\) factors other than \(A_N\). Then apply Hölder with exponents \(N-1\) and \((N-1)/(N-2)\) to separate \(A_N^{1/(N-1)}\) from the other product, and apply the same inequality in dimension \(N-1\) to the functions \(\int A_i\,dx_N\), \(i<N\). The base case \(N=2\) is Fubini. Taking the power \((N-1)/N\) and the arithmetic–geometric mean inequality gives the stated \(L^1\) derivative bound.

Apply it to \(F=|g|^{2(N-1)/(N-2)}\), with smooth approximation at zeros if necessary. Cauchy–Schwarz on its derivative yields \(\|g\|_{2N/(N-2)}^{2(N-1)/(N-2)}\le C_N\|g\|_{2N/(N-2)}^{N/(N-2)}\|\nabla g\|_2\). Cancel the common power (the zero case is immediate); density and Fatou extend the result. Reflecting evenly across the plane gives the half-space version with a changed constant. For \(g\) depending on time, spatial Hölder interpolation gives \(\int |g|^{2+4/N}\le\|g\|_2^{4/N}\|g\|_{2N/(N-2)}^2\) at almost every time. Integrate, taking the first factor in essential supremum, to obtain exactly the parabolic exponent gain in §7.4.

B.8 Time averages and nonlinear tests

The concrete Hilbert triple in Chapter 7 is \(H^1(\mathcal H)\hookrightarrow L^2(\mathcal H)\hookrightarrow (H^1(\mathcal H))^*\). For example, a square-integrable function \(g\) acts by \(w\mapsto\int_{\mathcal H}gw\), a bounded linear functional even if \(g\) has no weak spatial derivative. The weak time derivative in this dual space is characterized by

\[\int_0^T\langle v_t(t),w\rangle\zeta(t)dt=-\int_0^T(v(t),w)_2\zeta'(t)dt\quad(w\in H^1(\mathcal H),\ \zeta\in C_c^\infty(0,T)).\]
Steklov averages

On an open time interval \(I\), define \(v_\ell(t)=\ell^{-1}\int_t^{t+\ell}v(s)ds\) whenever the integration interval lies in \(I\). If \(v\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(I;X)\) for a Hilbert space \(X\), the averages converge strongly to \(v\) in \(L^2(J;X)\) for every compact subinterval \(J\subset I\), and their derivative is \((v(t+\ell)-v(t))/\ell\) there in the distributional sense.

Proof and application to the boundary

The derivative formula follows by testing the moving integral against a smooth time function and changing the order of integration. For convergence, restrict to a slightly larger compact time interval, extend by zero, and use continuity of translations in \(L^2(\mathbb R;X)\), proved first for continuous compactly supported functions and then by density. Minkowski's inequality bounds the difference by the average of the translation differences. With \(X=H^1\) locally, the trace map is continuous, so averages and traces commute and the traces converge strongly in boundary space-time \(L^2\). The averaged variational inequality follows by integrating the original one against translated nonnegative tests. For fixed \(\ell\), the averaged time derivative is locally in \(L^2\) with spatial values, permitting the ordinary chain rule for bounded powers.

Chain rules and admissible nonnegative tests

For a bounded real Sobolev function \(w\) and a continuously differentiable function \(F\) with bounded derivative on the range, \(\nabla F(w)=F'(w)\nabla w\) locally. The same rule holds for the positive part, with derivative \(1_{w>0}\nabla w\), and for \(\max(w,M_0)\).

Justification used in the Moser and truncation arguments

Extend \(F\) outside the bounded range with bounded derivative. On compact patches, approximate \(w\) in \(H^1\) by smooth bounded functions using reflection across the plane and local mollification. The Lipschitz bound gives convergence of the values; the derivative difference splits into \(F'(w_j)\nabla(w_j-w)+(F'(w_j)-F'(w))\nabla w\). Strong convergence and dominated convergence along an almost-everywhere convergent subsequence give the gradient rule. For truncations, approximate by smooth Lipschitz functions with derivatives converging away from the truncation level. The gradient vanishes almost everywhere on any fixed level set: along almost every coordinate line a Sobolev function is absolutely continuous, whose derivative is zero almost everywhere on a level set (at density points where the derivative exists). Fubini gives the assertion for each coordinate. This justifies the limiting derivative even at the corner. Time chain rules for the averaged functions follow by the same absolutely continuous line argument in time. Nonnegative bounded tests with compact support in the spatial ball and open time interval can be approximated by nonnegative smooth tests: extend evenly across the flat plane and by zero outside the support, then convolve with nonnegative smooth kernels, using a slightly larger compact support still inside the cylinder. For the averaged inequality only spatial \(H^1\) and space-time \(L^2\) convergence are needed after the time derivative is moved to the average; bounded flux and trace continuity justify passage. Upper-time indicator cutoffs are handled by smooth approximations and almost-everywhere endpoint limits. These details justify the nonlinear test in §7.4 and the convex truncation in §7.5.

Sources
Source correspondence: [4] §2 and §3.1 (trace estimate and weak evolution); [5] §2.2 and Chapter 4. The introductory examples and norm derivations are supplementary explanations.

B.9 Complete normed spaces and contraction mapping

A Banach space is a normed vector space in which every Cauchy sequence converges in norm to an element of the space. For a nonempty set E with a sigma-algebra, the bounded measurable real functions on E, with pointwise supremum norm, form a Banach space: a Cauchy sequence is pointwise Cauchy, its limit is measurable and bounded, and the Cauchy estimate passes to the limit uniformly. This is the unweighted space used in Definition 9.1 after division by the weight.

Contraction mapping theorem

Let X be a complete metric space and \(T:X\to X\) satisfy \(d(Tv,Tw)\le qd(v,w)\) for some \(0\le q<1\). Then T has exactly one fixed point.

Proof. Start at any v₀ and define \(v_{j+1}=Tv_j\). Successive distances obey \(d(v_{j+1},v_j)\le q^j d(v_1,v_0)\). For k>j the triangle inequality bounds \(d(v_k,v_j)\le q^jd(v_1,v_0)/(1-q)\). Completeness gives a limit v; the Lipschitz bound gives continuity of T and hence Tv=v. Two fixed points have distance at most q times their distance and must agree. Passing k to infinity gives the same geometric error bound. □

For the affine map \(Tv=F+Av\) on a Banach space with \(\|A\|\le q<1\), the iterates from zero are partial sums of \(\sum_{k\ge0}A^kF\), and the error after retaining terms through order j is at most \(q^{j+1}\|F\|/(1-q)\). This is exactly the estimate in Chapter 9. The positive cone is closed under norm limits in the pointwise weighted space, and positive A preserves it.

Parameter integration of nonnegative product-measurable functions against a sigma-finite measure gives a measurable function of the remaining variables. Here is the missing intermediate step. First assume the source measure ν is finite. The collection of product-measurable sets E for which \(x\mapsto\nu(E_x)\) is measurable contains the measurable rectangles. It is a Dynkin system: it contains the entire product, is closed under relative complements of nested members (finite subtraction), and under disjoint countable unions (monotone convergence). The rectangles form a π-system generating the product sigma-algebra, so the π–λ theorem gives the assertion for all product-measurable sets. Nonnegative simple functions and increasing approximation give the result for any nonnegative measurable integrand. For sigma-finite ν choose an increasing exhaustion by measurable sets of finite measure, apply the finite result to the restricted integrals, and pass to the increasing limit. In the Euclidean subspaces used here the product Borel sigma-algebra is the Borel sigma-algebra of the product, by countable bases. Thus this argument applies to the joint kernels and moving coordinates. A signed Borel kernel integral is measurable if the positive and negative parts are finite. This supplies the measurability step in §9.4; Tonelli is used separately to interchange nonnegative space-time integrals during continuation.

Appendix C · Supporting analysis

Appendix C · Fourier analysis and distributions

When to use this appendix. Use this appendix when a boundary function is extended harmonically, especially when the boundary power is not an ordinary integrable Fourier transform.

This appendix supplies the precise conventions and elementary derivations used in Chapter 6. All transforms here act in \(d\) Euclidean variables; normal height is a separate parameter.

C.1 Schwartz space and tempered distributions

A multi-index \(\beta=(\beta_1,\dots,\beta_d)\) has nonnegative integer entries, \(|\beta|=\sum_j\beta_j\), and \(\partial^\beta=\partial_1^{\beta_1}\cdots\partial_d^{\beta_d}\). Define

\[\mathcal S(\mathbb R^d)=\{f\in C^\infty:\ p_{m,\beta}(f)=\sup_x(1+|x|)^m|\partial^\beta f(x)|<\infty\text{ for all }m\ge0,\beta\}.\]

Convergence means convergence in every displayed seminorm. A tempered distribution is a continuous linear functional on this space; equivalently its absolute value on a test is bounded by a constant times a finite sum of these seminorms. We use bilinear distribution pairing \(\langle T,\psi\rangle\) (no complex conjugation). If a locally integrable function has integrable pairing against rapidly decreasing tests with a seminorm bound, it defines such a functional. In particular \(|x|^{-\alpha}\), \(0<\alpha<d\), is locally integrable by polar integration; outside the unit ball it is bounded, so a seminorm with \(m>d\) bounds the tail.

Distributional derivatives obey \(\langle\partial_j T,\psi\rangle=-\langle T,\partial_j\psi\rangle\). For instance, the derivative of the indicator of the positive half-line is \(\delta_0\): integration of the test derivative on the half-line gives its value at zero. Weak convergence in \(\mathcal S'\) means convergence of every such pairing. It is the convergence used for the Gaussian mixture approximants in Lemma 6.3.

C.2 Fourier transform, inversion and Plancherel

Use the unitary convention of (6.3). Differentiation under the integral and integration by parts show that derivatives in frequency correspond to multiplication by coordinate powers and multiplication by frequency powers corresponds to derivatives in space. The resulting bounds by integrals of Schwartz functions prove continuity of \(\mathcal F:\mathcal S\to\mathcal S\).

Derivation of Fourier inversion

For \(f\in\mathcal S\), multiply the inverse integral by \(e^{-\delta|\zeta|^2}\) with \(\delta>0\). Absolute integrability permits Fubini. Gaussian integration gives

\[(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int e^{ix\cdot\zeta}e^{-\delta|\zeta|^2}\widehat f(\zeta)d\zeta=(g^{(d)}_\delta*f)(x),\quad g^{(d)}_\delta(x)=(4\pi\delta)^{-d/2}e^{-|x|^2/(4\delta)}.\]

The right side tends to \(f(x)\) by the approximate-identity argument in §2.3. On the left the Schwartz decay of \(\widehat f\) allows dominated convergence. Thus the inverse is the same unitary integral with the positive sign. The Gaussian integral follows in one variable by squaring the integral and passing to polar coordinates; differentiation in frequency gives its Fourier transform, and products give the d-dimensional formula.

Derivation of Parseval and extension to L²

Insert the inverse transform of \(g\in\mathcal S\) into \(\int f\overline g\). Fubini is justified by \(f,\widehat g\in L^1\); the inner integral is the transform of \(f\). This gives

\[\int f\overline g\,dx=\int \widehat f\,\overline{\widehat g}\,d\zeta.\]

Taking \(g=f\) gives an isometry on Schwartz functions. Density of smooth compactly supported functions in \(L^2\) (truncate and mollify) extends it uniquely by completion; the inverse extends by the same argument, making it unitary. For weak derivatives in \(L^2\), testing the distributional derivative identity gives the multiplier \(i\zeta_j\widehat f\).

C.3 Convolution and distributional transform

For integrable functions the convolution \((k*f)(x)=\int k(x-y)f(y)dy\) is integrable. Fubini and translation give the unitary convolution formula

\[\widehat{k*f}=(2\pi)^{d/2}\widehat k\,\widehat f.\]

The extra factor is essential: the Poisson multiplier is \((2\pi)^{d/2}\widehat{\mathcal P_z}=e^{-z|\zeta|}\). Define \(\langle\mathcal FT,\psi\rangle=\langle T,\mathcal F\psi\rangle\) for tempered distributions; this agrees with the usual transform on Schwartz functions by Fubini with bilinear pairing. Continuity on Schwartz tests ensures that distributional convergence passes through the transform. In this convention \(\mathcal F\delta_0=(2\pi)^{-d/2}\) and \(\mathcal F^{-1}1=(2\pi)^{d/2}\delta_0\).

Multiplying an arbitrary distribution by \(|\zeta|\) is not automatically defined, because this function is not smooth at zero. In §6.4 the density \(a(d,\alpha)|\zeta|^{\alpha-d}\) is locally integrable and of polynomial growth; multiplication by \(|\zeta|\) is ordinary multiplication of functions. At \(\alpha=d-1\) the resulting constant density gives a Dirac mass, explaining the excluded endpoint in the power-to-power formula.

C.4 Gamma and Beta integrals

\[\Gamma(a)=\int_0^\infty t^{a-1}e^{-t}dt,\qquad B(a,b)=\int_0^1 t^{a-1}(1-t)^{b-1}dt\quad(a,b>0).\]

Integration by parts gives \(\Gamma(a+1)=a\Gamma(a)\), and substitution gives \(\int_0^\infty t^{a-1}e^{-ct}dt=c^{-a}\Gamma(a)\) for \(c>0\). With \(t=r^2\) and the Gaussian integral, \(\Gamma(1/2)=\sqrt\pi\). In the product \(\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)\), change variables from \((s,t)\in(0,\infty)^2\) to \(R=s+t\), \(v=s/R\); the Jacobian is \(R\), so the product is \(\Gamma(a+b)B(a,b)\). The same polar-coordinate Gaussian integral gives \(|S^{d-1}|=2\pi^{d/2}/\Gamma(d/2)\). These identities give the normalization of the Poisson kernel and the constants in (6.7) and (6.9).

Why the beta integral is strictly log-convex in its first argument

For \(0<\theta<1\), Hölder's inequality bounds \(B(\theta a+(1-\theta)c,b)\) by \(B(a,b)^\theta B(c,b)^{1-\theta}\). Equality for distinct \(a,c\) would require the two positive integrands to be proportional almost everywhere, impossible since their ratio is a nonconstant power of the integration variable. This proves the strict log-convexity used in §6.5.

Sources
Source correspondence: revised thesis Chapter 3 and Appendix B; research paper Appendix A. Inversion, Plancherel and the topology discussion are expanded supporting exposition.
Appendix D · Preliminary material

Appendix D · Spectral theory and stationary forms

When to use this appendix. Read D.1–D.3 when Chapter 8 turns negative form values into exponential amplification. A form-domain vector need not be an operator-domain eigenfunction.

This appendix supplies the spectral tools used in Chapter 8. We take the spectral theorem for bounded self-adjoint operators as a standard foundational result and derive the particular closed-form representation and heat evolution needed here. All Hilbert spaces may be complexified for spectral calculus; the real forms and solutions are recovered by restriction to real functions. We use inner products and forms linear in the first variable and conjugate-linear in the second; all diagonal values of Hermitian forms are real. A real symmetric form is extended sesquilinearly.

D.1 Spectral measures and functional calculus

A densely defined operator \(A\) on a Hilbert space \(\mathscr X\) is self-adjoint if \(A=A^*\), including equality of domains. The spectral theorem represents a self-adjoint operator using an orthogonal projection-valued measure \(E_A\) on the real line. For disjoint Borel sets the projections have orthogonal ranges and their countable sum converges strongly to the projection of the union. Also \(E_A(\mathbb R)=I\). For any vector \(v\), the scalar measure \(\mathfrak m_v(J)=\langle E_A(J)v,v\rangle\) has total mass \(\|v\|^2\).

For a Borel function \(F\), spectral calculus defines \(F(A)\) on the vectors with \(\int |F(\ell)|^2d\mathfrak m_v<\infty\). Its squared norm on such vectors is that integral, and bounded functions define bounded operators. For real \(F\), the operator is self-adjoint on this domain. Multiplication of spectral functions corresponds to composition whenever the domains permit. These statements for the unbounded operators below also follow by applying the bounded spectral theorem to their bounded inverse after a positive shift, using the reciprocal function on its spectral measure.

Example D.1 · Two orthogonal eigenspaces

For \(A=\operatorname{diag}(-3,1)\) and \(v=(1,1)/\sqrt2\), the scalar measure is \(\tfrac12\delta_{-3}+\tfrac12\delta_1\). The energy is \(-1\), while \(\langle v,e^{-hA}v\rangle=(e^{3h}+e^{-h})/2\). The example in Figure 8.2 evaluates this exact expression. In general the measure need not consist of point masses.

Example D.1a · Negative continuous spectrum

On \(L^2([-3,1])\), let \((Af)(s)=sf(s)\). This bounded self-adjoint operator has spectrum \([-3,1]\): outside the interval the inverse multiplier \((s-z)^{-1}\) is bounded; inside, normalized indicators of shrinking neighborhoods show that \(A-z\) is not bounded below. It has no eigenvalues because each level set \(\{s=z\}\) has zero measure. With \(v(s)=1/2\), the scalar spectral measure is uniform with density 1/4. Its first moment is −1 and its pairing is \((e^{3h}-e^{-h})/(4h)\) for h > 0. The negative energy therefore does not require any negative eigenvalue.

Example D.1b · An unbounded multiplication operator

On \(\mathscr X=L^2([1,\infty))\), define \((Af)(s)=sf(s)\) with maximal domain \(D(A)=\{f:sf\in L^2\}\). This multiplication operator is self-adjoint: testing the adjoint relation against functions of bounded support identifies the adjoint action as multiplication by s, and its domain is the same maximal domain. Spectral projections multiply by indicators of Borel subsets, and

\[D(A^{1/2})=\{f:\sqrt{s}f\in L^2\},\quad q[f]=\int_1^\infty s|f(s)|^2ds.\]

For \(f(s)=s^{-p}\), the ambient space requires \(p>1/2\), the form domain requires \(p>1\), and the operator domain requires \(p>3/2\). Hence p = 3/4 is an ambient-space-only example, while p = 5/4 belongs to the form domain but not the operator domain. Here \(e^{-hA}\) multiplies by \(e^{-hs}\), a bounded function for h ≥ 0. This exemplifies why functional calculus may define a bounded semigroup even when A is unbounded.

D.2 From closed forms to self-adjoint operators

Theorem D.1 · Representation of a lower-bounded closed form

Let \(q\) be a dense symmetric form on a subspace \(\mathscr V\) of a Hilbert space \(\mathscr X\). Suppose \(q[v]+C\|v\|^2\ge\|v\|^2\) and \(\mathscr V\) is complete in the norm induced by \(q_C=q+C\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\). There is a unique self-adjoint \(A\) with \(A+C\ge I\) such that

\[D((A+C)^{1/2})=\mathscr V,\quad q_C[v]=\|(A+C)^{1/2}v\|^2.\tag{D.1}\]

Its operator domain consists exactly of those \(v\in\mathscr V\) for which \(q(v,w)=\langle g,w\rangle\) for some \(g\in\mathscr X\) and all \(w\in\mathscr V\); then \(Av=g\).

Proof. By Riesz representation in the Hilbert space with inner product \(q_C\), for each \(f\in\mathscr X\) there exists a unique \(Rf\in\mathscr V\) satisfying \(q_C(Rf,w)=\langle f,w\rangle\) for all \(w\in\mathscr V\). In the ambient space \(\|Rf\|\le\|f\|\) by testing with \(Rf\), and Hermitian symmetry gives \(R=R^*\). The pairing \(\langle f,Rf\rangle=q_C[Rf]\) is nonnegative. Density of \(\mathscr V\) makes \(R\) injective; hence the range of the self-adjoint \(R\) is dense. The bounded spectral theorem, with the reciprocal function on \((0,1]\), yields a positive self-adjoint inverse \(T=R^{-1}\) on \(\operatorname{Ran}R\), with \(T\ge I\). There is no spectral mass at zero since the kernel of R is trivial. Put \(A=T-C\).

The defining equation for R shows exactly that \(q_C(v,w)=\langle Tv,w\rangle\) on \(D(T)\times\mathscr V\); conversely if the form functional is represented by \(g\), then \(v=Rg\). On \(D(T)\) we have \(q_C[v]=\langle Tv,v\rangle=\|T^{1/2}v\|^2\).

To extend this identity, observe that \(\operatorname{Ran}R\) is dense in \(\mathscr V\) in the form norm: any vector form-orthogonal to all Rf is ambient-orthogonal to every f, so vanishes. Independently, spectral truncations to \([1,k]\) show that \(D(T)\) is dense in \(D(T^{1/2})\) in the square-root norm, by monotone/dominated convergence of the squared norm integral. That norm is complete because the square root is a closed operator and dominates the ambient norm. The two completions of \(D(T)\) in the same norm therefore agree as subspaces of \(\mathscr X\). This proves (D.1). The domain characterization fixes A uniquely. □

Applied to the bounded Robin form (8.6), the shifted norm is equivalent to the H¹ norm by the trace inequality. This verifies the completeness hypothesis. For the singular supercritical form, no such lower-bounded shift exists by (8.3); we use the theorem only for the bounded approximations.

Which domain is needed?
\(\mathscr X=L^2(\mathcal H)\)All square-integrable data
\(\mathscr V=D((A_V+C)^{1/2})=H^1(\mathcal H)\)Finite shifted form energy; the concentrating test vector lives here
\(D(A_V)\)Operator action in L²; smooth members satisfy the Robin condition

These inclusions are continuous in the respective graph norms. Closedness of a form means completeness in the shifted form norm, not boundedness of its associated operator on the ambient space. The multiplication example above shows that the inclusions can be strict. Spectral Jensen needs the middle space only.

D.3 Heat evolution and positivity

For \(A=A_V\ge-C+1\) as above, set \(S_V(t)=e^{-tA}\). It is a bounded self-adjoint operator for each fixed \(t\ge0\) with \(\|S_V(t)\|\le e^{Ct}\). The scalar exponential multiplication law gives the semigroup identity. Dominated convergence in the spectral norm proves strong continuity at zero and therefore at all nonnegative times. For \(t>0\), \(S_V(t)f\in D(A)\), since \(\ell^2e^{-2t\ell}\) is bounded on the spectrum. Differentiation in the spectral norm on compact positive-time intervals gives \(\partial_tS_V(t)f=-AS_V(t)f\).

For \(T_0>0\) and \(f\in L^2\), use Tonelli and (D.1) to obtain

\[\int_0^{T_0}\|(A+C)^{1/2}S_V(t)f\|^2dt\le e^{2CT_0}\int\int_0^{T_0}(\ell+C)e^{-2t(\ell+C)}dt\,d\rho_f(\ell)\le \tfrac12e^{2CT_0}\|f\|^2.\tag{D.2}\]

Thus the evolution lies in the Bochner space \(L^2(0,T_0;\mathscr V)\). For positive times its derivative is the form functional \(-q_V(S_V(t)f,\cdot)\); the boundedness of the form on \(\mathscr V\) gives a derivative in \(L^2(0,T_0;\mathscr V^*)\) distributionally on the open interval. Strong continuity at zero supplies its initial value. The weak evolution uniqueness in Appendix B.6 and the identification in §7.2 show that for compact continuous nonnegative data it equals the positive bounded integral construction. In particular \(S_V(t)f\ge P_tf\ge0\).

For general nonnegative L² data, approximate by nonnegative compact continuous functions in L² (truncate and mollify a nonnegative extension). For each fixed t, boundedness of both operators gives L² convergence; the nonnegative cone is closed in L². This proves domination and preservation of order in the full L² space. Pointwise assertions in Chapter 8 are made only after identifying continuous representatives or evaluating the positive free kernel.

Self-adjointness is not order preservation

For example, the real symmetric matrix \(A=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}\) is self-adjoint, but \(e^{-tA}=\cosh(t)I-\sinh(t)A\) maps \((1,0)\) to a vector with negative second component for t > 0. For the Robin operators, order preservation and free-flow domination come instead from the positive integral iteration of Chapter 7 and density in L². On spectral values \(\ell<0\), the multiplier \(e^{-t\ell}\) grows; this fact by itself gives no spatial positivity or pointwise propagation.

D.4 Relation to logarithmic testing

The revised thesis also explains an older variational mechanism. For a smooth strictly positive regularized solution v and a compact smooth real test \(\psi\) on the closed half-space, assume the local smoothness needed to differentiate and integrate by parts. Then \((\log v)_t=\Delta\log v+|\nabla\log v|^2\) and \(\partial_\nu\log v=V\). Multiply by \(\psi^2\) and integrate:

\[\begin{aligned}\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathcal H}\psi^2\log v\,dx &=\int_{\partial\mathcal H}V\psi^2d\sigma-2\int_{\mathcal H}\psi\nabla\psi\cdot\nabla\log v+\int_{\mathcal H}\psi^2|\nabla\log v|^2\\&=-q_V[\psi]+\int_{\mathcal H}|\psi\nabla\log v-\nabla\psi|^2\,dx\ge -q_V[\psi].\end{aligned}\tag{D.3}\]

This explains why negative energy forces logarithmic growth in a suitable average. It is not alone a pointwise divergence proof. The spectral argument in Chapter 8 provides the required propagation and limit without assuming a smooth singular solution. The original Ishige–Ishiwata proof develops additional estimates for its truncation method; it is not reproduced here.

Sources
Source correspondence: research paper [4], Appendix B; revised thesis [5], Appendix D. The multiplication examples, domain diagram, order-preservation example, Riesz-resolvent derivation of the closed-form representation and spectral energy estimate (D.2) are supplementary explanations of standard results. The spectral theorem itself and Riesz representation are foundational inputs; see [6] and [7] in Sources and references.
Appendix E

Appendix E · Probability and stochastic calculus

When to use this appendix. Read E.1–E.3 for stochastic derivations and E.4 for the numerical sampling algorithm. The foundational probability results are identified separately from their application.

Probability prerequisites for Chapter 10. Construction of Wiener measure, the Brownian strong Markov property, the reflection principle, Doob's maximal inequality and the general Itô formula are standard stochastic-calculus inputs (see [8]); they are not consequences of the heat estimates. The derivations below explain the particular normalization and formulas used in this exposition.

E.1 Probability spaces, conditioning and martingales

A probability space consists of a sample space \(\Omega_{\rm prob}\), a sigma-algebra \(\mathcal F\) and a countably additive measure \(\mathbb P\) of total mass one. A random variable is a measurable function, and its law is the pushforward of \(\mathbb P\). Expectation is integration against this measure. A filtration \((\mathcal F_s)\) is an increasing family of sigma-algebras, representing information available by time s. We use the completed right-continuous Brownian filtration. A process is adapted if its value at each s is \(\mathcal F_s\)-measurable. A stopping time τ satisfies \(\{\tau\le s\}\in\mathcal F_s\) for every s. Conditional expectation \(\mathbb E[Y\mid\mathcal G]\) is the \(\mathcal G\)-measurable integrable variable with equal integrals to Y on every set in \(\mathcal G\), determined almost surely. In particular, it preserves positivity and obeys the tower rule.

A martingale is an adapted integrable process M such that \(\mathbb E[M_t\mid\mathcal F_s]=M_s\) for s≤t. A local martingale has a sequence of stopping times increasing to infinity for which the stopped processes are martingales. To remove localization on a bounded horizon it is sufficient that the stopped values converge and are dominated by a common integrable random variable; dominated convergence in the martingale expectation identity then gives equality of endpoint expectations. The strong Markov property of Brownian motion states that after an almost surely finite stopping time the future increments are Brownian and independent of the stopped past. The same assertion on the event τ<t follows by stopping at τ∧t. Restarting the deterministic reflection map transfers this property to X and the future increments of L.

E.2 Stochastic integrals and the Itô correction

For a simple adapted integrand \(J_r=\sum_j J_j\mathbf1_{(s_j,s_{j+1}]}(r)\), with square-integrable \(\mathcal F_{s_j}\)-measurable J_j, define \(\int_0^t J_r\cdot dW_r\) by the sum of J_j dotted with the corresponding stopped Brownian increments (truncate the last interval at t). Independence and zero means of successive increments give zero expectation; the cross terms vanish by conditioning on the later left endpoint, and covariance gives

\[\mathbb E\left|\int_0^t J_r\cdot dW_r\right|^2=\mathbb E\int_0^t |J_r|^2\,dr.\]

Completion extends the integral by this isometry to square-integrable predictable integrands, using density of simple predictable processes (predictability is measurability for the sigma-algebra generated by adapted left-continuous processes). The conditional expectation property of simple sums passes to the L² limit. Locally square-integrable integrands are defined by stopping. We use Doob's L² inequality \(\mathbb E\sup_{s\le t}|M_s|^2\le4\mathbb E|M_t|^2\) for continuous square-integrable martingales.

Along deterministic partitions with mesh tending to zero the sums \(\sum\Delta W_i\Delta W_j\) converge in L² to \(t\delta_{ij}\), since independence makes their variance O(mesh) on a fixed time interval. A continuous finite-variation path has sums of squared increments tending to zero, bounded by its total variation times its largest increment. Mixed sums tend to zero in probability by Cauchy–Schwarz. The second-order Taylor expansion for a smooth f of \(X=x+\sqrt2W+e_NL\) therefore retains the second-order Brownian contribution \(\Delta f\,ds\) while the regulator contributes its first-order Stieltjes term. More precisely, the standard Itô formula for continuous semimartingales (with localization to compact sets and the time-dependent first derivative for a C¹,² function) gives (10.4). The Taylor argument explains the coefficient; it is not intended as a proof of the general stochastic calculus theorem.

E.3 Reflection principle and exponential moments

For a Brownian motion starting at a, reflect the portion of a path after its first hit of level m≤a. By strong Markov and symmetry the reflected future has the same law. Thus the paths crossing m and ending at b≥m correspond in law to paths ending at 2m−b≤m. For the variance-2 driver the density of the latter endpoint is \(g_t^{(1)}(a+b-2m)\). This establishes the crossing formula of §10.2. Integrating it, or applying symmetry at zero start, gives the maximum of \(-\sqrt2 W_s\) up to t the law \(\sqrt{2t}|Z_0|\), where Z₀ is standard normal. The regulator is its excess above the initial height. The union bound and the reflection principle also give \(\mathbb P(\sup_{s\le t}|W_s|>r)\le4\mathbb P(W_t>r)\) for one coordinate and r>0. For each finite b and finite T, Gaussian tails imply \(\mathbb E e^{b\sqrt2\sup_{s\le T}|W_s|}<\infty\) for b≥0, by integrating the tail. This bound is independent of the nonnegative starting height.

E.4 Sampling a Brownian-bridge minimum

For a variance-2 Brownian driver starting at a and ending at b after a step Δs, the conditional probability that its minimum m_step is at most m≤min(a,b) is the ratio of the crossing density to the unrestricted density:

\[\mathbb P(m_{\rm step}\le m\mid a,b)=\exp\!\left(-\frac{(a-m)(b-m)}{\Delta s}\right).\]

Invert this distribution using an independent uniform random variable U in (0,1). The smaller root of the quadratic equation is

\[m_{\rm step}=\frac{a+b-\sqrt{(a-b)^2-4\Delta s\log U}}2.\]

Conditional on the grid endpoints the bridge segments are independent by the Gaussian increment law, so independent uniforms give the correct conditional minima. Updating the running minimum and applying (10.2) gives the exact joint grid-time law of the normal endpoint and regulator. Tangential increments are independent Gaussians of variance 2Δs. This samples minima, not their exact occurrence times or locations along the tangential Brownian path; for variable V the right-endpoint Stieltjes sum still has discretization error. On any common continuous path its error is bounded by the modulus of continuity of \(s\mapsto V((X_s',0),t-s)\) at the mesh times L_t. This tends to zero pathwise. For bounded V and f the exponential-tail bound in E.3 supplies an integrable dominator, giving convergence of expectations as the mesh decreases. The Monte Carlo estimator at a fixed mesh is an ordinary sample mean of independent path functionals. Its variance is the single-path variance divided by the sample count, finite by the same bound with twice the coefficient. The usual sample variance estimates sampling uncertainty, not bias of the Stieltjes approximation. The browser uses seeded pseudorandom uniform draws and Box–Muller normals, approximating ideal independent draws.

E.4 supplement · Joint laws and paired estimators

Continuous process laws on path space are determined by finite-dimensional distributions at rational times: rational evaluations determine continuous functions and generate the Borel sigma-algebra of the uniform topology on compact time intervals. One-time laws alone do not determine joint laws. Gaussian increment grids reproduce the Brownian finite-dimensional distributions; bridge minima give additional exact regulator grid laws, not full within-step paths.

To couple meshes, use fine driver endpoints and conditional minima, then running minima. A minimum over a coarse interval is the smallest of its constituent minima. Restricting endpoints and regulators to the coarse grid therefore gives their correct joint coarse law. For paired differences Dᵢ, the standard error of their average is the sample standard deviation of Dᵢ divided by √n, not the difference of the two independent standard errors. Equation (10.11) and dominated convergence establish mesh convergence for bounded data and fixed bounded regularization. The numerical experiment assesses finite-resolution sensitivity, not the unknown remaining bias.

For iid Yᵢ with finite variance, expansion of the square of \(\bar Y-\mathbb EY\) gives variance \(\operatorname{Var}(Y)/n\). Expanding \(\sum_i(Y_i-\bar Y)^2\) about the true mean gives expectation \((n-1)\operatorname{Var}(Y)\), proving unbiasedness of the usual sample variance. The law of large numbers and central limit theorem concern increasing sample counts, not guaranteed small error in a given sample. Strong exponential weights may be poorly sampled.

Appendix F · Numerical analysis

Appendix F · Numerical analysis and reproducibility

When to use this appendix. Use this appendix to interpret numerical error, mesh refinement, and reproducibility; an empirical rate is not an unconditional convergence theorem.

Use the weighted grid inner product of Proposition 11.4. Each interior edge contributes \(-\ell\omega_k (u_i-u_j)(v_i-v_j)\) in horizontal directions and \(-\ell(u_i-u_j)(v_i-v_j)\) vertically. This follows by adding the two endpoint terms; the half-weight at the bottom makes the doubled coefficient symmetric. With zero Dirichlet data this summation-by-parts formula extends to arbitrary grid vectors, and the Robin term adds \(\ell^2\sum V u_0v_0\). Setting \(v=u\) gives the discrete energy pairing, and taking \(v=1\) on active nodes gives the outer-face loss.

For an exact solution with bounded time derivatives through order two and spatial derivatives through order four on the closed active region, Taylor's formula with bounded remainders establishes the residual bounds used in Proposition 11.3. They are assumptions, not consequences of numerical stability. If \(E(h)\approx Ch^p\) with comparable time error, the ratio \(\log_2(E(h)/E(h/2))\) estimates the order; self-convergence substitutes inter-grid differences and is not a certified error estimate. The fixed-time-step spatial test can eventually be limited by temporal error.

See the stability and convergence proofs, discrete budget tests, and the reproduction workflow. Data are IEEE float64; the slices are rounded to seven decimals for display, while diagnostics are calculated before rounding. All new curves are precomputed unless explicitly identified as analytical control demonstrations.

Here the Hölder spaces refer to trajectories on the entire compact time interval. The first implication follows from the Hölder estimate; the last is immediate. 9. Weighted solvability and upper singularity estimates · 7 entries
Result and proofStatus / scope
Proposition 9.1 · Two characteristic rootsManuscript / expanded proof

For \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\), the equation \(C_N(\alpha)=\lambda\) has exactly two roots \(\alpha_-(\lambda),\alpha_+(\lambda)\). They obey \(0<\alpha_-<\alpha_*<\alpha_+<N-2\), \(\alpha_*=(N-2)/2\) and \(\alpha_-+\alpha_+=N-2\). The coefficient exceeds λ strictly between the roots.

Lemma 9.1 · Harmonic heat representation (paper Lemma 5.1)Manuscript / expanded proof

Let \(0<\alpha<N-2\), \(\mu=C_N(\alpha)\), and \(x\in\overline{\mathcal H}\setminus\{0\}\). For every \(t>0\),

Proposition 9.2 · Local weighted contractionSupporting lemma / exposition

Under (9.9), \(\mathcal A_I\) is a positive bounded linear operator on \(X_\alpha(I)\) of norm at most \(q_\alpha\). The map \(W\mapsto F_a+\mathcal A_I W\) has a unique fixed point there (see Appendix B.9 ), and it is nonnegative with \(\|U\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\le cM_a/(1-q_\alpha)\).

Supplementary Proposition 9.3 · Sharper harmonic-barrier estimateSupplementary deduction

With the same assumptions and notation as Proposition 9.2, the positive solution satisfies \(\|U\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\le cM_a\). If \(S_j=\sum_{k=0}^j\mathcal A_I^kF_a\), then \(\|U-S_j\|_{X_\alpha(I)}\le cM_aq_\alpha^{j+1}\).

Lemma 9.2 · Weighted heat-potential continuity (paper §5.3)Manuscript / expanded proof

Assume only continuity of ξ, \(0<\alpha<N-2\), and a Borel boundary density \(J\) with \(|J(y,s)|\le L_J|y|^{-\alpha-1}\). Then \(\int_a^t\int_{\partial\mathcal H}K_\xi J\) is absolutely convergent and continuous off zero on \(D_0(I)\), with value zero at a. If \(f_a\) is continuous away from zero and \(|f_a|\le M_a\Phi_\alpha\), its moving free evolution is continuous on the same set including its initial value.

Supplementary consequence · Sharper continuationSupplementary deduction

By Proposition 9.3 on every window, \(M_{j+1}\le cM_j\) and hence \(M_j\le c^jM_0\), while \(\|U\|_{X_\alpha([t_j,t_{j+1}])}\le c^{j+1}M_0\). Therefore the global norm is at most \(c^JM_0\). This follows by finite induction, with the same uniform c; it does not assert a uniform bound as T tends to infinity. The original estimate (9.15) is retained for comparison.

Supplementary Theorem 9.1 · Full characteristic-coefficient intervalSupplementary deduction

Let N≥3, T>0, \(0<\lambda<\lambda_N\) and ξ be continuous with (M). For any \(\alpha\in(\alpha_-(\lambda),\alpha_+(\lambda))\), suppose \(u_0\ge0\) is continuous on the closed half-space away from the initial pole and \(M_0=\sup_{x\ne\xi(0)}u_0(x)/\Phi_\alpha(x-\xi(0))<\infty\). There is exactly one nonnegative integral solution in the corresponding global moving weight class; it is minimal among all nonnegative integral solutions with this datum. The sharper global bound is \(c^JM_0\) for the uniform admissible partition above.

In particular a limiting singular series that diverges at a positive-time interior observation forces divergence of the approximating positive series there; that latter statement does not require their finiteness.

Proposition 13.6 · A conservative multi-pole existence criterion (supplementary)Supplementary deduction

Theorem 13.11 · Uniformly separated subcritical moving poles (supplementary)Supplementary deduction

The sum of the couplings is not required to be subcritical.

Appendix B · Functional analysis · 2 entries
Result and proofStatus / scope
Lemma B.1 · Elementary half-space trace estimate (paper (3.1))Manuscript / expanded proof
Theorem B.2 · Nonautonomous form evolutionSupporting lemma / exposition

Let real bilinear forms \(a_t\) on \(\mathscr X\) be measurable in \(t\) on each pair of vectors and assume, with constants independent of time, \(|a_t(v,w)|\le M\|v\|_{\mathscr X}\|w\|_{\mathscr X}\) and \(a_t(w,w)+c_0\|w\|_Y^2\ge c_1\|w\|_{\mathscr X}^2\), \(c_1>0\). For every \(f\in Y\) there exists exactly one \(v\in\mathcal W\) with \(v(0)=f\) and \(\langle v_t,w\rangle+a_t(v,w)=0\) for almost every time and all \(w\in\mathscr X\).

Theorem D.1 · Representation of a lower-bounded closed formSupporting lemma / exposition

Let \(q\) be a dense symmetric form on a subspace \(\mathscr V\) of a Hilbert space \(\mathscr X\). Suppose \(q[v]+C\|v\|^2\ge\|v\|^2\) and \(\mathscr V\) is complete in the norm induced by \(q_C=q+C\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\). There is a unique self-adjoint \(A\) with \(A+C\ge I\) such that

Notation · Parts I–V

\(\mathcal{H},\overline{\mathcal{H}},\partial \mathcal{H}\)Half-space, closure, boundary
\(\xi,\mathcal G_\xi,\Sigma_\xi,D_\xi\)Trajectory, singular graph, nonsingular lateral boundary, nonsingular space-time domain
\(\nu,\partial_\nu,d\sigma\)Outward normal, normal derivative, boundary measure
\(g_t,G,P_t\)Free heat kernel, Neumann heat kernel, heat semigroup
\(\lambda,\lambda_N\)Coupling constant, sharp critical constant
\(V,V_\varepsilon\)Singular boundary coefficient, bounded regularized coefficient
\(N,T,u,u_0\)Spatial dimension, terminal time, unknown temperature field, initial datum
\(\omega(h)\)Uniform normalized displacement modulus; see (3.2) and §5.2
\(A_\xi,F\)Positive boundary integral operator and free evolution
\(\operatorname{Tr}\)Sobolev boundary trace
\(L^p(\mathcal{H}),H^1(\mathcal{H})\)Lebesgue and first Sobolev spaces
\(\|\cdot\|_2,\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\)Spatial L² norm and real L² pairing
\(L^2(0,T;\mathscr X),\mathscr X^*\)Time-dependent Hilbert-valued space and continuous dual
\(\Phi_\alpha,\alpha\)Positive harmonic weight (defined in Chapter 3) and its homogeneity exponent
\(C_N(\alpha),\alpha_-,\alpha_*,\alpha_+\)Characteristic coefficient, smaller root, critical exponent, larger root
\(\mathcal P_z\)Poisson kernel at height z
\(\mathcal E[w],B_a[w],Q_{\lambda,a}[w]\)Bulk Dirichlet energy, weighted boundary integral, quadratic form (4.3)
\(K_\xi,U\)Moving-frame heat kernel and translated unknown
\(D_\pm,c_\pm\)Diffusivity factors and Gaussian comparison constants
\(b_0,b,q,\Delta x,\Delta t\)Gaussian width, bounded Robin coefficient, prescribed flux, spatial and temporal grid steps
\(\varepsilon,\eta\)Regularization length and exponent loss (Theorem 3.3); locally specified auxiliary tolerances may also occur
\(d,\mathcal S,\mathcal S^{\prime},\widehat f,\mathcal F\)Boundary dimension, Schwartz space, tempered distributions, Fourier transform and transform operator (§6.2–6.4; Appendix C)
\((-\Delta_{x\prime})^{1/2},\Phi_*\)Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator and critical harmonic profile (§6.3, §6.6)
\(b_n,u_n,A_b,S_h,R_h\)Bounded Robin approximations, their solutions, bounded Volterra operator, boundary-to-volume and boundary-to-boundary heat operators (Chapter 7)
\(a_t,\mathcal W,\mathscr B_T\)Time-dependent form, Hilbert-triple evolution space and bounded continuous function space (§7.1–7.2; Appendix B.6)
\(Q_R,\kappa\)Backward parabolic cylinder and Moser exponent gain (§7.4)
\(A_V,S_V(t),q_V\)Stationary Robin operator, its heat semigroup and its bounded-coefficient quadratic form (Chapter 8)
\(\rho,m^{-1},m\rho\)Test-function concentration length, stationary regularization length, dimensionless ratio (Chapter 8)
\(V_m,w_m,\mu_-\)Stationary bounded approximating coefficient, its solution, effective lower coupling
\(\mathfrak m_v,E_A\)Scalar spectral measure of a vector and spectral projection measure (Appendix D)
\(\Gamma,B\)Gamma and beta functions (the boundary form is separately indexed as \(B_a[w]\))
\(X_\alpha(I),D_0(I)\)Weighted Banach space and nonsingular fixed-frame cylinder (Chapter 9)
\(\mathcal A_I,F_a\)Boundary integral operator and free evolution on a restart window
\(\mu_+,q_\alpha,L_\alpha\)Effective upper coupling, weighted contraction bound and continuation multiplier
\(W_s,X_s,L_s\)Standard Brownian driver, reflected path, and boundary regulator (Chapter 10)
\(\mathbb P_x,\mathbb E_x,\mathcal F_s\)Path law from x, expectation and Brownian filtration (Appendix E)
\(A_s,\tau_\ell\)Accumulated boundary action and inverse regulator level time (Chapter 10)
\(\Omega_{\rm prob}\)Probability sample space, distinct from numerical domain Ω
\(\Psi_s,\mathcal W\)Translated critical harmonic profile; weak energy evolution space (§7.9)
\(S(V,f),A_V\)Extended positive series and its boundary integral operator (Proposition 2.2); \(A_\xi\) is the operator for the singular coefficient
\(\rho,m^{-1}\)Concentration length and regularization length in Chapter 8
\(X_s,L_s,A_t\)Reflected path, Skorokhod regulator, stochastic action in Chapter 10 (not the boundary operator)
\(\ell,r=\Delta t/\ell^2\)Spatial grid spacing and discrete diffusion ratio in Chapter 11
\(L_0\)Limiting small-scale displacement modulus (§12.2)
\(\xi_i,\lambda_i\)Multiple boundary poles and their couplings (§13.7)

Notation correspondence: the half-space is \(\mathcal H\) (as in the typeset research paper); the paper’s \(H_\alpha\) is written \(\Phi_\alpha\) here. The paper’s exponent loss in Theorem 1.3 is denoted by \(\eta\). The Sobolev symbol \(H^1\) is unchanged. Symbols confined to a calculation are defined locally.

Conventions: \(\mathscr X=H^1(\mathcal H),Y=L^2(\mathcal H)\) in the variational evolution (locally written X,Y in Chapter 13). In spectral theory, the ambient Hilbert space may be denoted \(\mathscr X\) locally; its operator is \(A_V\) when explicitly defined as self-adjoint, not the time-dependent boundary integral operator. The context specifies the meaning. \(C_N(\alpha)\) always denotes the characteristic coefficient; generic constants such as C(N) can change between estimates. L is a displacement bound or a local logarithmic cutoff width, whereas \(L_s\) is the stochastic regulator. The exponent loss η is distinct from regularization length ε. Symbols introduced only inside individual proofs retain their local definitions.

The revised research paper and thesis are the primary sources. Supplementary results are identified separately throughout the article.
